This tale is written with respect for historical accuracy, but the author begs indulgence for rearrangement in chronology and for his attempt at character visualization and interpretation necessary to fashion a personal narrative from the saga of Captain Cook's epoch-making voyage to Hawaii, which ranks with the greatest voyages, including those of Columbus and Magellan. Substantially the events paraded on these pages all actually took place in history.

The story of Captain Cook's death is the most remarkable in the whole history of ocean disaster. 

-WALTER BESANT, 1890

Lost Eden

1 1 Chapter 1 

TWICE I HAD RUN AWAY FROM MY HOME NEAR NEWCASTLE BEFORE 

I was six years old. Upon the second of these occasions my mother found me at Tynemouth, ten miles distant, embarking with fishermen in a small boat, soiled and disheveled, but happy as a North 

Country lark.

At eighteen I seized every opportunity to walk into the countryside with my sketching materials under my arm. That I might the better paint a picture, often I would climb a tall tree to see new beauties in a landscape, or stretch at length upon the grass to watch the changing clouds of a summer day and their shadows upon the land and sea.

In the year 1776, when I reached the age of nineteen, my mother thought to take a firm hand in the shaping of my future, for my father was long dead. "John Westermark," she said, "no good will come of your dawdling with paints. You had best go in the colliery with your uncle and drive a mule." "It is dirty and dark and dismal there," I replied, “and I see no beauty in a mule's backside." "Shame on you! With a good mind like yours, you can be a breaker soon, and I dare say the best of them, and that is a good, solid living." “No, not a living. Rather, a slow death. I must have open fields and sunshine." "Ah, John, John," she sighed, "if you will forever waste time with your paints, there is no sound future for you, and in the end you may be a beggared old man with no honest trade to bring you a bit of food for your body."

LOST EDEN 

"When I am older I will settle down, Mother," I said, as often I had remarked before, and I gathered up my painting materials and kissed her good-by.

Like most young men, I would not take my mother seriously. A darling she was, and to live with her, and perhaps with a wife something like her, should be good enough for this life indeed, had Nature cast us all in the same mold. But I was different from most others of my age, and this I knew for sure. 

I could never explain to Mother my urge to paint scenes of the world around me, stirring sights like the magic of the light that very morning upon the tipple where the cars unloaded the coal. The sunshine and shadows there caught my eye, and I knew that I must hurry if I were to finish the picture of this scene, a canvas I was eager to complete. 

My strides were long and fast, for I wished not to lose a moment of this sorcery of nature.

I had scarce set up my homemade easel and fixed my painting upon it when I became conscious of someone who walked as fast as I in this sleepy dale where hurry almost was unknown. He was attired like a gentleman, wearing a well-fitted suit of brown cloth with a brown three-cornered hat, and he carried a polished black cane. He came striding briskly across the fields instead of along the road, and the morning air seemed to lift his spirit as it did mine.

He walked straight to me and stopped, blowing slightly. "Ah, excellent!" he said, and then extended his hand. "My name is Banks." "John Westermark. At your service, sir," I said, rising awkwardly and grasping his large, firm hand. As I did so, I noticed that his cane had a small watch set in the head, artistically embellished with gold, which would make this perhaps one of the rarest canes in England. With some trepidation I added, "Are you Mr. 

Joseph Banks?" "Yes. That's right," he answered, in a most casual manner, as though the name meant nothing at all. But I knew that here stood the famous Joseph Banks who had sailed with Captain Cook to the South Sea Islands on the first voyage of that remarkable navigator, clear around the world. The father of botany in Eng-

LOST EDEN 

11 land, he was soon to become Sir Joseph, chosen president of the 

Royal Society. "Now this fellow you have painted here!" he said with enthusiasm, pointing to an old-timer in the picture. "He is the very essence of the men who mine coal for England." "He was my grandfather Westermark," I explained, “and this is the only likeness we have of him. I did my best, because there are many who miss him." "Never a doubt of it, I am sure," Mr. Banks agreed, “and that is what art should be. Tell me, have you heard of Omai?” "Yes sir, I have." He was the native of the Society Islands in the South Seas who came back with Captain Cook on his second voyage around the world. Omai was a tradition by now, a figure at court, a friend of the famous, residing with the Earl of Sandwich in his mansion of Hinchinbrooke. "He must be returned soon to his home, and I have commissioned several artists to paint his portrait. How would you like to have a go at him also?" 

My first commission! And what a subject for an artist, this regal person from another world. I think Mr. Banks was as near to a fairy godfather as any patron a young man might well hope to come by. "That I would like, sir, with all my heart," I answered.

Little did I dream that this chance meeting with a great gentleman would lead to my sailing with Captain Cook on a voyage of discovery which many now say is the most spectacular of all voyages, surpassing in both its danger and the beauty of its surprises the historic sailings of Columbus and Magellan. 

They do call Captain Cook the greatest of all navigators, and the island of Hawaii, which he discovered with my feeble and very obstinate help, set all Britain to dreaming of the fragrance of tropical lands and the allurements of their people.

Chapter 

AND SO IT CAME ABOUT THAT I, A POOR LAD FROM THE COLLIERIES, found myself in the house of the famous Joseph Banks in Soho Square in London, and a grand house it was. There seemed to be no limit to my host's wealth or to the number of his servants. 

My first meeting with the astounding Omai took place in the drawing room before dinner. At that time I could discern none of those disturbing traits which lurked within this handsome fellow with manners so polished. It was hard to believe that he was in truth a savage, sprung almost from the Stone Age, as far as his way of living went, in a land where up to then no metal and no white men were known, and not even the boon of all transport-round wheels.

When I saw him first, Omai wore a coat of red velvet lined with white silk, white satin knee breeches, and white silk stockings.

The jeweled sword given to him by King George III he carried gracefully.

With a slight bow this fabulous person greeted me. As he grasped my hand, the expensive lace ruffles of his sleeve brushed my wrist. His jet-black hair gleamed with perfumed oil and was clubbed behind his neck in the latest fashion. His light brown complexion made him appear not unlike an Englishman much exposed to the sun.

As Omai talked he smiled broadly, and he seemed to smile with all his soul. I had never seen such a smile or such firm white teeth. His well-developed figure appeared to be softer, better padded than the bodies of our men, just as his lips and nose were fuller. 

Here stood a man with sunshine built into him, and obviously different from us of the harsher climate of England. 12

LOST EDEN 

13 I thought it must be his beautiful country which made him as he was, and I longed to see that country for myself. That strange world he came from was the subject of much romancing; it had caught the imagination of all England. In his presence, the charm of Omai's faraway homeland seemed to touch me especially, and I felt the artist's urge to capture if I could that most unusual smile. 

The eyes of all the women were upon him, and two whom I remember upon this occasion were the Duchess of Devonshire and Lady Townshend. "Isn't he fantastic!" said the duchess as we chatted. "He could add a few letters to Lord Chesterfield's." "Omai appears to have been born with culture in him,” I ventured.

There was no doubt that Omai was a lion of our proud society, not like the famous Dr. Samuel Johnson, but quite as popular. I think some of his charm, even beyond his unusual appearance, lay in his quaint use of English and strange expressions coupled so paradoxically with his exquisite manners and peacock finery. "There is such a difference in real manners and tawdry manners," the duchess continued. "For instance, this new custom of using snuff. I declare I will never take it, no matter what the decree of fashion. Now Flavilla, over there, does it in the coquette manner and nothing else. She pulls out her gold snuffbox in the midst of a sermon and passes it about to show she has a fine hand. 

Once when the churchwarden came about for the offering, she asked if he would take a pinch!" "Oh, really?" I tried not to laugh, because Flavilla did seem pretty, and more than a little desirable. She had made up her face to look like that of a china doll, probably by the liberal use of "Venetian Paste," which I was told contained white lead, and her hands and neck and the visible portion of her bosom, which was considerable, she also had adorned with similar enamel. 

You can be sure that I was dazzled by the finery of these grand ladies and their likeness to china dolls, but suddenly my heart leaped within me when into the room came one in plain attire and unpainted face, but with such charm that she seemed a queen. The translucent rose coloring of her skin was such as only

LOST EDEN 

a painter of ivory miniatures might imagine. Her fair hair fell naturally about her lovely face but seemed a halo. Her deep blue eyes looked everywhere but at me.

The duchess was rambling on with her small talk, for how long I do not know. I heard nothing she said until she touched my arm. "That is Lorna Pierce," she whispered, "the daughter of 

Mr. Banks' housekeeper. You will be fast indeed if you get in ahead of some others who chase that little filly. Take care, now!" "She lives here!" I said, ignoring the woman's sly intimations, and I continued to say to myself, unbelievably, "She lives here!"

Only vaguely was I sensible of the chattering of the duchess as my eyes followed Lorna Pierce. I hoped for a glance directly into those clear blue eyes, but she ignored me, going about the room calmly and efficiently, noticing the snuffing of the candles and whether the hearth fire needed mending.

At last, as she left the room, her eyes swept across mine, and in that brief moment some glowing ember inside me burst into flame and I felt the warmth of it in my cheeks. 

Perhaps she understood, for I thought I noticed the faintest of smiles curve the lovely line of her lips. Then she was gone. "Well, it seems to be time for kau-kau," said the duchess, using the Tahitian word for eating as the guests began to move toward the dining room. In smart circles many such terms were taken up. Their use showed that one had acquaintance with the newest social celebrity. It was the thing to do.

We seated ourselves at a great oaken table, heavily laden with food and sparkling with burnished silver and exquisite china. "How good it looks," said the duchess to Omai. word!" "Ver' good, not bad,” he replied. “But no dog to eat. My

This speech again sent the company into gales of laughter. 

Later I discovered that Omai mentioned his native custom of eating dogs as often as could be contrived, seldom failing to create merriment. Another might have been called a showoff, but Omai was a play actor of inborn ability, who invariably held his audience in the palm of his hand.

LOST EDEN 

15 To sophisticated London, Omai represented man in a "state of Nature," and Jean Jacques Rousseau had stirred England by claiming that such primitive souls obtained more from life than did many who vaunted their so-called civilization and enlightenment.

Spice was added to such discussion by the knowledge that charming creatures like our visitor and his female counterparts in Tahiti were accustomed to living entirely in the nude on their tropical islands. They had some cloth made by pounding the bark of a tree into thin sheets, but this was worn more as a display of wealth than as a covering decreed by modesty. The "noble savages" and the freedom of their love was a smart topic of the day, and it fascinated these men and women of London who were so completely and ornately covered by clothing. 

As to Omai as a lover, it appeared that his approach, whether calculated or not, differed radically from Stanhope's. Whereas 

Stanhope openly paid court to the ladies with all the graces recommended by his father, Omai's reserved deportment often resulted in fair-skinned ladies seeking his companionship when he would not seek theirs.

I did my best to concentrate upon the dinner, and dinner with Mr. Banks was a great occasion every night. There was a joint on a silver platter beneath a vaulted silver cover to keep it warm, and roast beef which Mr. Banks sliced with a thin, keen knife, while conversation sparkled. There was a pork pie, but what I liked better was the beef and kidney pie. We had three kinds of fowl, and pheasant was my choice.

Much fine china was set about, as England was well into what the periodicals deplored as "the china craze." People bought all manner of china and pottery, some in most unartistic taste, but Mr. Banks showed rare discrimination. He had much of the beautiful cream-colored queen's ware which had brought fame to a Josiah Wedgwood, and but recently had purchased some of this man's new jasperware of great beauty and delicacy, fit for cameos, with portraits and bas-reliefs, and I was much taken with that of light blue and white.

After the women retired, the candelabra lighted a man's 1

LOST EDEN 

world new to me, for then the cloth was drawn and the port passed around. Mr. Banks got to his feet and raised his glass. "The King!" he called out, and we drained the first pouring of port in a new pledge of fealty to our sovereign, George III. Only at that moment did a proud, tingling sensation arouse me from the dreamy trance into which I had been plunged by two very blue eyes.

For my part, I needed no wine that night to set the blood coursing through my veins, and I took little. However, some may have thought me tipsy because, for the life of me, I could not keep my thoughts from straying to Lorna Pierce.

EARLY THE NEXT MORNING OMAI SAT FOR ME AS I BEGAN HIS POR

trait, a remarkable experience in my life, but now secondary to my emotions for a certain girl. Thoughts of her had disturbed my rest-uncomfortably, yes, but deliciously as well. 

Omai was charming, but he could not keep his mind upon the work in hand any more than could I. Full of life like a young boy, his changing moods flitted across his face too fast to capture on my canvas.

Nor was my hand as clever as I had hoped it might be, for always my ears were listening for the rustle of Lorna's long, full skirts. I could hear her sometimes just outside the door. I thought 

I felt her eyes upon me now and then, and once, when she passed near, a faint perfume came to me, reminding me of jasmine in a moonlit garden.

Often the tinkle of her laughter floated in from another room, and it lightened my spirits. At length she entered the door. 

She passed close behind me and I felt a strange tingling sensation on the back of my neck. "Something has happened to you, my lad," I said to myself.

The light patter of her tiny red fur-trimmed boots formed a melody unbelievably enchanting. 

Only when she was leaving did she glance my way. Once again I looked full into her face. Yes indeed, she was as beautiful as the vision which had danced before me in the night. 

One of my brushes, which was charged with yellow ocher, dropped from my hand and, in trying to seize it, I clumsily sent it flying toward her and it made a bright yellow streak upon her tidy dress. 17

LOST EDEN 

"Oh, how messy!" she exclaimed. 

Her first words to me!

We both reached for the brush at the same time, nearly bumping our heads. I seized it, and in doing so my palette brushed across her face, streaking her lovely cheek with Chinese white, burnt sienna, and madder lake, with a touch of aquamarine squarely upon her nose. 

Omai roared with laughter. "Yah, yah! Ver' good; not bad!"

She grimaced and seemed to wrinkle her pert little nose. “A nice way to become acquainted!" 

Then we also burst into laughter. Never did I see a more charming array of color. But now we were acquainted! Yes, we were acquainted! "I'm most sorry, miss," I managed to say.

She turned to a mirror. "Well, here is one face you painted, right enough. Now how do I get rid of these beautiful daubs?" 

With rare presence of mind I said, “Spirits of turpentine!"

I selected a clean cloth and, holding her enchanting though soiled face in one hand, I performed the most pleasant job of washing ever done on land or sea. siasm. "It takes rather a long time, doesn't it!" she sighed. "Yes, rather," I admitted, and returned the sigh with enthu- The following day Omai sat for me about two hours and then grew restless, but perhaps no more than I, for the distant rustle of skirts and the sound of certain light footsteps disturbed me no end. "I want to get on my great hog and fly into the wind," he said, referring to his horse. "Ver' good, my hog. Ver' noble beast."

Mr. Banks, from his adjoining room, overheard. "Maybe that is enough for now, John," he called to me. "Would you like to come here and see how I amuse myself?" "I would, sir. And thank you, Omai."

Omai quickly came to my canvas to see what I had done. "Ver' goddam good," he said. "Handsome fellow, eh? My word!" 

Then he called out to the footman: "Barlow, fetch my great hog!"

I went in to see Mr. Banks seated in a large red velvet chair and, though I did not know, my destiny was to depend in large

LOST EDEN 

19 measure upon my answers to his questions during the next few minutes. "I want you to meet Dr. Anderson," he said, presenting me to a most learned-looking man with a warming smile which was to cheer me in many a foreign land. “He is a first-rate physician and is fast becoming a famous botanist as well." "I am only a wayward pupil in botany," said Dr. Anderson as he shook my hand, "but I could not ask for a better teacher than Mr. Banks."

The room was large and appeared to be a workshop and laboratory, with scores of shelves upon which were arranged cases and bottles and books, and upon some of the shelves stood strange devices with which he sometimes conducted scientific experiments, like the producing of miniature lightning which his friend Benjamin Franklin of the American Colonies had managed to draw from the clouds.

He took a small ball of gray substance from a shelf. "Here is a plaything that amuses me no end," he said.

He let it fall to the floor, and the ball bounced back almost as high as his hand. "What might that be?" I was greatly surprised. I felt that

I was in touch with greatness, and I was eager to learn all I could. "That is elastic gum. It exudes from a tropical tree. They are beginning to call it indiarubber because ... Watch."

With one of the new graphite pencils he had at hand he made some marks on a paper, and then with the substance that bounced so well he rubbed over those marks and they disappeared immediately with no hurt to the paper. "The same," he said, "would happen with charcoal or like markings." "It is like magic." "It is also impervious to water and can be shaped into shoes," he added with enthusiasm. "There is treasure in this, as much as in gold. England should have more of it.” 

Among his plants there were hundreds I had never seen, carefully pressed and dried and laid under glass. "I collected more than eight hundred new plants during my three-year voyage with Cook," he said. "And see, I have had

LOST EDEN 

these plates made of them for a publication, but that part of the work does not interest me much, and I seem never to get around to shaping a book. Also, the drawings are only in outline. Too bad they are not more artistic and informative. Have you ever drawn plants?" "I have made a few sketches of flowers, and I painted our garden in color. It is a beautiful garden for such a small yard.” “Mmmm.” He always made that sound when meditating something which pleased him. “I took a young artist with me on the voyage." "What fun for him!" "Mmmm. He didn't go far with us. Not quite as far as

Tahiti. He was taken with a fit and died in two days. We dropped him into the South Pacific Ocean." Again he watched my reaction. "Poor fellow," I said, and I am afraid that I winced. "These plants here I gathered in Van Diemen's Land. 

Strange, aren't they?" "Yes, and interesting." "We were caught in a blizzard while hunting them and two of our party froze to death. Solander and I barely struggled through. It was a near thing." Again his eyes searched my face. "I can't imagine such cold. It must be terrifying." "On the contrary, when you come near to freezing you grow warm and sleepy. And when you lie down to sleep, that is the end.

Solander could scarce keep his eyes open and I hadn't the strength to kick him."

Mr. Banks seemed to make the other side of the world come alive for me. My eyes, roving greedily over his trophies, alighted on a preserved head of a brown man who seemed to have died of a bashing on the side of his skull. The skin was dark and had been mummified by drying in the air, but it had contracted in a way to give the face a strained and worried look. "He was eaten," said Mr. Banks simply. "By cannibals?" I asked stupidly. "Yes, by cannibals." "And they have eaten Englishmen, I know." "I would say that they prefer light meat to dark. I am thinking of a boatload of Captain Furneaux's men whom I found

LOST EDEN 

21 cut up and roasted and placed neatly in baskets of palm strippings." 

I was going to ask more about this affair when Omai came to the door. "Ah, Mr. Banks," he said. "I have more present from Queen 

Oberea. You see, ver' small; not big. I lose him; I find him.”

He held out an ear ornament made of three large pearls on a small string. In Tahiti, where Queen Oberea ruled, they adorned only one ear with such ornaments. One of the pearls was discolored and had a faded, yellow appearance, but the perfection of the other two was marred only by the inexpert drilling of the holes. "Thank you, Omai. And thank the queen when next you see her." He took the gift and stepped to a window, the better to examine the treasures, I thought, but soon I noticed that his gaze wandered to the far horizon.

Quipsters had made much of the affair of Banks and this queen of Tahiti who had sent rich gifts to him by Omai. Some of 

Cook's company who wrote of their experiences maintained that all people on the voyages had taken Tahitian mistresses excepting Captain Cook, and certain wags openly penned rhymes about the nights Banks spent on Queen Oberea's sleeping mat. Some of these appeared in the Tatler and in the Spectator, which publications closely followed the latest trends in thought. One anonymous poem which crept into print referred to the night Mr. Banks's breeches were stolen from under his head while at the queen's house. It purported to be a lamentation written by that lady, and in part it ran: 

Thrice happy youth! what bliss with thine could vie, To feed on dog's flesh, and with queens to lie. 

Yet think at least my copious tears you see, And spare one thought from botany for me . . .

Think on the raptures we once have known, And waft one sigh to Otaheite's throne.

Mr. Banks showed much annoyance at these sallies, but he met them squarely. "I presume you have read some amateur verse about me," he said, but did not wait for me to admit or deny the fact. “It is not good poetry, but doggerel seems to be a fashion of the

LOST EDEN 

eighteenth century. A pity it has not by now vanished from the minds of men." "I go now," said Omai, “to ride fast and watch houses go past me in other directions. Good-by. Until we meet again." 

Always he used every polite phrase which he thought might be at all fitting, and he made this elaborate adieu knowing that he was expected back within the hour for luncheon. "Good-by." Banks smiled and then handed the pearls to me. "Wonderful place, Tahiti. It is a veritable Garden of Eden."

THE NEXT DAY, AS I PAINTED, I LISTENED FOR THE RUSTLE OF CERTAIN 

skirts and the fragrance of a perfume, my thoughts more upon a beautiful girl than upon my work.

It was not until dinner that I saw Lorna, and then only for a moment as she busied herself with the candles. She took no notice of me until just as she left the room. Though she gave me but the shortest of glances, her heart, I thought, was in her eyes. 

As we finally arose from table that night, after what seemed to me an interminable dinner, I observed Lorna go into a great hall where many portraits were hung and followed her. More than once I had seen some young blade of the nobility hurry after that fleeting vision merely to make a fool of himself and catch nothing but the echo of her laughter. Even the older ones in powdered wigs made similar errors when in their cups.

The thought came to me that I was such another one, but as I entered the gallery she stopped and stood smiling in a way to melt the heart of a young fellow and limber his spine and stop his tongue. "You really shouldn't come in here," she murmured.

We were alone, and to me it seemed all was just as it should be as I stepped toward her. And then it was nothing she said, but what I saw, that froze me where I stood. Directly behind

Lorna I beheld a striking picture of Omai, a wondrous painted figure which seemed so much alive that it almost spoke to me. 

Here was a truly great portrait that caught the elusive, sunny enchantment of the subject's spirit and his country. 

My heart sank within me, so quickly can the hungers of the artist betray the ardors of a lover. 23

LOST EDEN 

"Why was I engaged to paint him?” I said half to myself. "Mr. Banks has no need of me, for no man can do better than that. Who painted this picture?" "You shouldn't be discouraged," Lorna replied, her eyes serious and seemingly sharing my pain. "It was done by a knight of long experience. He has painted many great gentlemen." "Do you know him?" "Sir Joshua." "You mean Reynolds?" "Yes. Now do you feel better?" “No. I am lost. I had better go back to the colliery." I felt that Mother had been right about my painting. In all of a lifetime 

I could not equal this. My whole world suddenly fell away from me. 

As I gazed upon that canvas in despair, suddenly I felt 

Lorna's soft lips on mine. Before I could gather my startled wits, she went bounding out of the room like a little white rabbit. At the end of the long hall she paused, gathered her skirts to run upstairs, and threw me a kiss. "Good night!" Never did so much music fill a voice, nor could even a Reynolds catch the radiance of her at that moment, and I alone of all men in the world was the one to see. 

That night I lay awake for a long time, tormented by two distressing thoughts, the first that I could never be a supreme artist, the second that the most charming girl in all the world had given me hopes of possessing her when I knew myself to be entirely unworthy.

In the morning I was weary and upset when I met Mr. Banks. "I must go home to the colliery," I announced sadly. "I saw the picture of Omai which Sir Joshua Reynolds painted, and I feel like an impostor. There is too much for me yet to learn." "Yes, that is a rather good picture," he answered, his hands behind his back, observing me thoughtfully. On his face was a serious look, but his eyes held a bit of a twinkle. He held my future in his hands and he knew it. "How would you like to drive with me along the Thames this morning?" he asked at last. "It would be most agreeable."

LOST EDEN 

25 "Then come. And you had best bring your paints. We must have a talk."

Soon we were rolling along in his great coach and four. I marveled at the softness of its springing action as we took the ruts and bumps at a good clip. Few in England traveled as luxuriously as I that day.

Mr. Banks leaned back and talked chiefly about himself, but I know now that he was continuing his examination of me. "I am afraid I was not much of a student at your age," he began. "I had too much energy to sit down and memorize Latin and Greek. I liked all vigorous sports, and young Sandwich was about the only boy who could keep up with me. You are rather robust yourself." "I like to be active,” I said, thinking only that I was making idle conversation and not revealing my nature for his inspection. "I like to take my sketchbook and walk miles into the woods or along the shore." "Mmmm. Do you find much you wish to paint?" "I do, sir, so much that it is difficult to choose the best." "How do you choose?" The man had a way of taking me to pieces. "I choose the best grouping of beautiful or stirring objects. 

I walk fast until I find a composition that strikes my fancy and then I sit down to paint.' "Mmmmm." 

I had scored again but did not know it. "We are coming to Kew. Perhaps you have heard of the botanical gardens there?" "Yes sir, and I know that they are mostly your doing." "Many have helped. I am only the one with the energy to spur them on. There was almost no science of botany in England when I began to collect. I paid sixpence apiece for new plants brought to me by women who were by no means collectors. They were no more than cullers of simples. An acquaintance with herbs was about all the scientific knowledge of botany that England had at that time. I could talk for days on end about the Kew gardens, but Captain Cook's ship, the Resolution, is in the river. Would you care to have a look at her?”

LOST EDEN 

"Very much, sir." How could one imagine a better holiday, or host? "Did you ever paint a ship?" "No sir, but often I have wanted to try." "Then you like ships?" "I do, very much, sir. There is something about them that draws me." I had almost said that there was an inspiration in them like that which I found in Omai, but I had done with talk of Omai. His name was painful to me. "There you see a topmast of her. Cook sailed her clear around the world." "I read his account of the voyage. I read it twice. I wanted to know more than he put into words." "Yes, a book like that is indeed the merest shadow of a voyage. The way to learn is to go along. I wish I could write what 

I know of the South Seas." He rested his chin upon his hand and gazed out at the gray sky above the Thames, as he had gazed out a window with Queen Oberea's pearls in his hand. "I wish I could go again."

Then the ship came into full view and I stared at her strong, broad bow and her lofty masts and great spars. "A sturdy ship,” I ventured. "What does she make you think about?" he asked. "I think of the beauties she has seen," I said without reserve. “I think mostly of green island in deep blue seas and lofty peaks of snow, and fire-spitting volcanoes." "I wonder," he said, “if you would make one more picture. 

Will you paint the Resolution for me while I meet some old friends at the Cock and Bull Tavern?" "I will indeed, sir!" "Good!" He clapped me on the shoulder with his strong hand. "Out you get, then. You have about three hours." 

I jumped from the coach with enthusiasm, and soon coach and horses were hidden in dust as I fell to with a will, my dark mood entirely gone.

I knew the style of the ship somewhat, for she was modeled like those I had seen in the coaling trade. She had been built at 

Whitby and purchased of Captain William Hammond, of Hull.

LOST EDEN 

27 At Deptford she was fitted out for one of the longest voyages in the annals of the sea. Cook had sailed such a collier along the fogbound coasts of England before he entered the Navy, and he knew its seaworthiness and would have no other kind of vessel. 

At the time I thought I was far from being a seafaring man by nature, but now I am sure that I was the same as all others. 

I was born on land, of course, and I grew up on land and loved the things of the land, but one day I saw a ship and loved it more. 

That is what makes a sailor of a lad.

As I studied the Resolution, I felt the nobility of her. Those great masts and spars were a mighty work of the hands of man, and she seemed to throw a challenge to the wild elements of wind and storm as she waited there to take sailor men safely to unknown lands of this spinning planet which is ours. 

What struck me most forcefully was her bow, a beautifully curved, massive breast which defied the seas. To me she seemed to be alive. The strength of that bow and the brave look of her fascinated me so that I sat there, a young painter from the collieries caught in a spell. From that moment the Newcastle lad in me was a sailor.

I could detect the faint odor of tar coming from her on the breeze, and it also stirred me. It is a balmy smell, that of tar, and often in these years when I am growing old I take my cane which I cut from a koa tree on Hawaii Island and walk to a port of ships where I can refresh the boyhood in me with a whiff of salt and tar. 

Little did I think at the time that we were to discover those fabulous lands which Cook named the Sandwich Islands, a discovery great enough at last to bear the name of his good friend, the Earl of Sandwich, and to me the loveliest string of pearls cast up in any ocean. Their memory still haunts me, and sometimes on the English shore I fancy I can smell the fragrance of white ginger blooms and feel the balminess of the breezes which caress that gem of gems I was to know so well, the island of Hawaii. 

I sketched the Resolution's broad beam, which narrowed toward the stern in graceful manner. I took the liberty of changing the angle of a boom to give her better lines, and I moved her in my mind about half a league seaward to set her before a more attractive bit of shore line which was there. I had all but filled in

LOST EDEN 

the color when I heard the coach returning. It came bouncing and rocking to a stop beside me. "Good!" cried Mr. Banks, jumping to the ground and viewing my picture. "Very good. That is the Resolution, and no mistake. Barlow, will you bring the hamper and spread the lunch? 

Master John and I will have a bit of bread and cheese.”

Barlow, the footman, laid a very good lunch. I was accustomed to surprises from Mr. Banks, and they came so fast that often I forgot one while considering the next, but I do remember the convenient way he served the bread. He had cut it neatly in slices, so that it was not necessary to break a piece from the loaf, a difficult thing at such a luncheon where plates were not set out. 

And for further convenience the bread slices were arranged in pairs with tasty bits of food between, some with meat and some with a relish of pickle and some with cheese. "This way you need no forks,” he explained, taking one of the pairs of bread slices in his hands, “and no plates. I learned this from my old friend, Montagu." "Is he the lord you call John?" "Yes, the fourth Earl of Sandwich. Omai lives with him, as you know. This trick of his is being copied everywhere and people are beginning to call it a 'sandwich,' faint tribute indeed for a great man. He eats in this manner at the gaming tables because he is one person who grows hungry as often as I, and eats as much."

After a heavy draught of French wine, Mr. Banks looked again at my picture of the Resolution and then at me. "How would you like to sail on her?" he asked casually. "If it were to the South Seas, I would sell my soul to go." "Then you may perhaps have your wish," he said quietly. "She is voyaging again to the Pacific, and you can go as one of the scientific party. Omai must be taken home to his native country as was promised, and there will be other instructions from the Admiralty. Doubtless they will try again for a northern passage to the Spice Islands.” 

I was squeezing a "sandwich" in my hand so tightly that it had become a sorry mess. My mind was suddenly blown far away to Tahiti and the lands of Spice.

Perhaps it would be better to settle down and build a home, if you know of some desirable girl."

He gave me a sly glance, and I knew that he suspected my feelings about Lorna. In my desire to avoid the subject, I asked, "What could I do aboard ship? I have never been to sea."

"WHAT WILL YOU DO ON THE Resolution?" MR. BANKS REPEATED 

my question as we looked at the ship itself and at the picture I had made of her.

He was thoughtful for a moment and then turned toward me. "You will do what all men do at sea." 

I must have looked very blank indeed. 

He laughed. "You will obey the captain's orders. You will put your hand to any work that is required. But ashore you will paint and sketch as Dr. Anderson directs. That is, when your mouth is not gaping open like a landed fish as it was just now!" 

He was in high good humor at the way he had toppled me over. And I knew not whether I walked on air or earth. "On such a voyage," he continued, "there must be an artist to record the discoveries. Some of your work will be for beauty's sake, but mostly for the sciences. You must sketch new plants and flowers and animals and fishes which are found, because not many can be preserved. Mariners, too, will appreciate accurate records of headlands." "Nothing would please me more." "That I believe. You must learn to work fast because you will find much to sketch. Often you will wish, as I have, that you had some magical machine which would make the picture for you instantly, for there is interest on every hand and never enough time for drawing."

And that was the way it fell out. I was offered a chance to go on what to the English nation is the most momentous voyage of discovery that ever has taken place. It contributed substantially to our country's expanding greatness. Cook's discoveries started the 30

A good man for the Admiralty, Sir Hugh, but he cannot fully understand the value of scientific men on such a cruise. He blocks me at every turn. I must stay home again. It is best to send strong young men like you, who can work the ship." "I am deeply sorry, sir." "Thank you, John. We had better go now."

He talked intimately all the way home, and my head was in a whirl, with two grand vistas of life opening up to me: if I could win Lorna, I might go happily down the long, pleasant lane of life with her, or I might find the beauties of the big, round world on such a voyage as few mortals are privileged to take. 

Perhaps I could have both these prizes. Yes, perhaps.

But I had not reckoned upon a certain young Lord Wadleigh, a midshipman, who was to enter my life with disastrous results.

Just before dinner, when the first guests were beginning to arrive, I went into the parlor, and as I stepped through the door 

I heard Lorna give a little cry of alarm. She and Lord Wadleigh stood facing me.

It was easy to deduce from their expressions that he had been trying to force his attentions upon this charming lass who had so disturbed my dreams, and that she had sought to fend him off, perhaps with not much success. face.

He was about a year older than I, and so loud of mouth was he and so swaggering of manner that only to look at him was to desire to knock some intelligence into his ugly head. different cloth that seemingly naught in this world could bring us peaceably together.

He had been seated near me at table, wearing his splendid midshipman's uniform, and Mr. Banks had been most attentive to me, thinking no doubt to make me feel at ease in my brown homespun clothing. And at each pleasantry from my host I would see Wadleigh's wide mouth turn up at the corners in a smug grin, and his small, beady black eyes, set close together, would glint at me sardonically.

He showed me the same antagonizing face now, as I surprised him with Lorna. "So you're a collier!" he drawled, at which Lorna hurriedly left the room. He drew himself erect in his elaborate red broadcloth and lace and flaunted a gold snuffbox. "A genuine Johnny 

Muckface, with coal dust to powder your wig! I do declare, one meets all manner of people in this house." "That I have noticed also, Your Lordship," I replied, and

I think my manner left him little doubt as to my feeling toward him. "And some are willful beyond their station in life." He took a pinch of snuff and flicked it into his nostrils. 

I could scarce keep my hands from clenching, for I wanted nothing better than to bury a fist in his leering face. "And some," I replied, “are bold beyond their capabilities." "How interesting!" He drew a fine linen kerchief from his sleeve and fluttered it pompously across his nose. "Perhaps you will be good enough to point out such a person one day.” “That would be a pleasure." I turned and left him then, and I heard him chuckling to himself. 

No doubt he thought he had won in a charming game of manners. Perhaps he had. Certainly I felt a sharp sting and he seemed to feel none. I could only wish that this were not the house of Mr. Banks but rather an open field by the collieries, with friends to assure me fair play. I would at least have the satisfaction of a go at this unmannered brat.

Soon after dinner I went out into the garden to meet Lorna.

My legs were strong and willful with much walking, and now they shot me forward, to pit my fists against his gleaming steel leaping from its scabbard.

I felt the sharp point of his sword strike my chest. In the same instant my right arm drove forward and sank my knuckles in his throat. 

A deadly blow is that which strikes squarely upon the

Adam's apple, and it was my luck to make a clean, accurate hit.

LOST EDEN 

He went over backward and lay upon the ground, dead to all the world except for the rolling, pleading little black eyes in his ugly face. 

I opened my waistcoat to examine my hurt. I had a gash almost over my heart, but it had gone no farther than my ribs. 

A swordsman he was, no doubt, and very quick on the draw, but this time the active legs which had carried me over so many bright fields were a match for a swordsman's arm, and I had won. 

When I looked at him again, I saw a streak of red on his right cheek, extending from near his nose to his jowl. He had cut himself as the sword flew from his hand. A trickle of blood flowed down into the lace of his stock. 

I caught up his weapon. Rubies and diamonds glinted in its hilt. The point was tinged with red, and this point I placed against his throat. "How about it now, you cowardly fop?" I shouted.

A slight contraction of the muscles of his face showed that he desired to speak but could not. All color had drained from his face. "You would have run me through!" I cried.

My first thought was to dispatch him then and there, but that I could not do. I flung the blade into a clump of bushes. "We each will have a scar,” I said, "but yours will show."

He began to gasp and I knew he would live, and so I turned away, to leave him upon the ground where he had fallen. A pathetic, whimpering whelp he was, and I thought I had taught him some manners, but in that opinion I was very wrong. 

Lorna had run to another part of the garden, where I think she heard none of our words and must have seen our fight dimly if at all. She came timidly forward.

I expected her to say something of his offense, to be pleased that I had taken such risk in her behalf, and that I had given him what was due him. But how wrong I was! Instead of being pleased, she was indignant at the breach of etiquette I had committed. "You fought him!" she cried. "You, a guest in this house, and you fight another guest!"

This turn of affairs I most certainly had not expected. 

Clumsily I tried to explain, but I must admit that I was somewhat

I buttoned my coat over my wound and stamped inside the house. 

As I passed Mr. Banks' study he called to me. "John, have you made up your mind? If you really wish to go with Captain Cook, I have some papers for you to sign." 

I did not hesitate. “Thank you, sir. I will sign."

I took the quill he offered. I did not even read the papers. 

I thought I was to be the commonest forecastle hand and did not then understand that I was appointed to the enviable billet of midshipman.

A wound over my heart was bleeding and I could feel the warm trickle of blood running down inside my waistcoat. But that did not pain me as much as the heart inside me as I scratched upon the parchment, "John Westermark." 

And with those scratches I was committed to the sea for four long years.

Chapter 

MY HEART WAS HEAVY IN THE DAYS WHICH FOLLOWED, AND A BITTER

ness filled me. Lorna and I did not speak to each other. Such was an aspect of young love which I had not expected to encounter and could not understand. "If she is genuinely fond of me,” I argued with myself, “she will come to me and tell me so."

And that was the way matters rested when I went aboard the Resolution for the first time. Deliberately I tried to forget the girl who had cast a spell about me, and then aroused my anger to the breaking point, and, like many proud youths before and since, I transferred my affections to a ship. 

Aye, 'tis a wondrous moment when a man goes aboard his first ship! The very heaviness of my heart seemed to make her more enchanting, with her promise of freedom and forgetfulness far over the horizon.

Certain well-remembered events stand out vividly in my life, such as the lacing up of my first pair of boots, or seeing the King on parade, but none is so vivid as setting foot on my first ship, the Resolution.

Already she had a proud history of one voyage to the Pacific Ocean with Captain Cook, and her crew had returned with tales as astonishing as those of Marco Polo, and the tales were true. 

She rode at anchor at The Nore in the broad mouth of the Thames, when Mr. Banks took me out to her in the pinnace. Not far away lay the Discovery, a vessel of three hundred tons, carrying eighty people, which was to accompany us as a safety measure. 

As we drew near, my ship seemed a beautiful thing to me, and I refused to think of the disparagements I had heard about 36

LOST EDEN 

37 her: "A mud scow with masts." "The slowest, broad-beamed butter tub at sea.” “Her seams are sprung." "She'll not weather another trip." "A collier making believe she's a blooming frigate."

Not a big craft, she measured only ninety feet in length and was of a burthen of four hundred sixty-two tons, but she seemed formidable indeed as we came near and saw some of her sixteen guns at the open ports. There would be twenty marines aboard for added safety, and in all she would carry one hundred twelve men. 

Always when I thought of the Resolution's complement, I also thought, "And I am one of them.”

When we came alongside, she seemed gigantic, and the motion of the water which made our small boat bob up and down against her hull brought life to her. "This is my ship!" I said to myself as I went up over the side and at last stood on her broad, gently heaving deck. 

Captain Cook was not aboard, and so we were greeted by the sailing master, Lieutenant William Bligh, a young man of twenty-two, only three years older than I but already a lieutenant and master of this ship. I admired him, and the firm way he held his mouth, which I regarded as self-discipline. I did not then see in him the characteristics which thirteen years later made him the object of a mutiny on H.M.S. Bounty, and later an insurrection in 

Australia, although the stern, almost vicious aspect of his nature did appear at times on our voyage. "This," said Mr. Banks, introducing me, "is Westermark's first voyage. He is an artist." George Vancouver turned out to be one of my best friends on the voyage, perhaps because he was just my age, nineteen, and since those days he has done some creditable exploring on his own and has a large island named after him off the west coast of North 

America by the Strait of Juan de Fuca.

He conducted us below deck, where I had my first smell of that mixture of vinegar and gunpowder which was to be with me night and day for more than four years. As a health measure, Captain Cook had all decks and bulkheads of the people's quarters swabbed with vinegar and then smoked with gunpowder. It is an

LOST EDEN 

39 odor that lingers in the nostrils like that of the tar which is used so freely aboard ship, and once a man has sailed with these strange perfumes, he seems to have them with him for the remainder of his life.

It was at Plymouth, whither the Resolution had proceeded from her berth at the mouth of the Thames, that we made final preparations for departure.

In the notion of Mr. Banks, who was on hand although he would not go with us, there could be no more stirring port whence to sail on such an expedition. In truth, the Devon town was memorable in our history. From Plymouth, Drake had set out just two hundred years before on his voyage around the world. Those whom some called Pilgrims last touched land there when they departed for America; and the British fleet sallied thence to meet and, with the grace of a providential storm, destroy the Spanish Armada.

So there we were in Plymouth Sound, just off The Three Towns and in sight of Smeaton's lighthouse tower, on the historic day of July fourth of the year 1776. Ours was a ship of the British 

Navy, and on that day we were at war with the Colonies in America.

Although none in England actually knew of that war until a sailing vessel arrived weeks later from across the Atlantic with the news, even then we had talk in plenty of its likelihood. "The Colonists will never fight England," declared Bligh,

LOST EDEN 

positive as he was in all things. "They would be overwhelmed. We have the strength to defeat a powerful nation like France. What chance would those colonial fanatics have against us?” "But they are mostly Englishmen like ourselves," observed Captain Clerke of the Discovery, a man of notably reasonable outlook. "They will fight for their rights. It's natural they resent those heavy taxes, for they are as yet a poor community and need money more than England does." "They will find many friends in England," observed Mr.

Banks. "It is not a war that many of us would welcome, unless perhaps it be the press gangs." "The impressing of seamen is a necessity if we are to maintain a strong navy," Bligh insisted. "Colonists are still British subjects and should have no objection to it. I am on the King's side, always, and if I were sure there would be a war and a chance of promotion, I would not relish the journey before us.”

I will admit that I was more interested in my new clothes than in the possibility of a war. In the assembling of my wardrobe, Mr. Banks exhibited his usual generosity. One pair of breeches was of finest white silk from France, fitted expertly by his tailor. My blue tail coat boasted the best of broadcloth, lined with white silk, trimmed with white, and set off by gold anchor buttons. My pigtail had now come to a mature growth. Neatly braided and tarred, it rested rakishly on the white patch of my collar which sailors call "the weekly account." This gave me the most pleasure of all my accouterments, unless perhaps it might be my three-cornered reefer's hat.

Mr. Banks had spent above one hundred pounds on this finery for me, knowing that it would be worn only a few days before sailing and that the sea and the weather probably would claim it before long.

As this sum of money was more than I had thought to earn in a year at the colliery, it may be imagined with what exalted feelings I strutted about. Only nineteen, I was pointed out as one of 

Cook's men wherever I went, as were all of our ship's people, because it was well known by now that we were going upon an exploration surpassed in interest by few voyages in history. 

It was pleasurable, I do admit, to parade the ancient streets

LOST EDEN 

41 of Plymouth. One day when I was with Mr. Banks perhaps it was my finery which put him in the notion of further extravagance, for suddenly naught would do but that he must secure a supply of red feathers. 

To this end he purchased the feathers of two hundred geese at the poultry mart. They were to be had cheap enough, as they were good for nothing but pillow stuffing. Then in his coach we took our purchase to a dyer, where Mr. Banks ordered they be dipped in the finest crimson dye. "If ye say so, 'tis done," said the dyer, sorely mystified. "But if I were to sleep on these, I would dream of hell for sure."

Later Mr. Banks explained their usefulness to me. "In the islands," he said, “a red feather may have the value of a five-pound note, and often you can buy a grown pig for a twopenny nail. Aah, lad, you will see the wonders of the world and laugh yourself sick betimes."

My happiness knew no bounds when Captain Cook invited me to sup with him in his quarters, with Mr. Banks. I had met him before, but never had I been allowed the opportunity of talking with him until this night. I was a midshipman now and would be permitted the company of officers, and this was my formal acceptance into their society.

Cook was a giant of a man, above six feet tall, not corpulent but strongly built. However, he was deceptively mild-spoken and rather quiet until some spark set off his temper. 

It was all I could do to keep from staring at this famous face, with eyes wide apart and a nose large and long, almost like the beak of some great bird. If the shape and size of a nose mean anything, I would say that his was partly inquisitive, as you might expect in an explorer or a man concerned in scientific research, and partly the nose of a manager of large affairs. Perhaps also it was a barometer of violence as well as forceful character. 

In all, he seemed too mild to command rough men or fight a storm at sea as it must be fought in a sailing ship, though I was struck by the continued evenness of his voice, the sureness of it, and the ready courage coming through without the least show of braggadocio. "I have just learned," he said, "that the sloop Four Winds

LOST EDEN 

came in with two out of five hands dead of scurvy, and she sailed no farther than the French coast. Above fifty men dead in a voyage of four months!" "Ah," said Mr. Banks, "what good is science if a commander will not use it? I daresay that would not happen on a ship of yours. Fresh food is the thing for combating scurvy." "True. But you must not give me credit for the findings of the scientists in this matter. My task lies only in putting their knowledge to use, which should be simple enough." food." "But it takes clever work to induce sailors to eat proper "Yes, a British sailor is a mighty lad, but he is set in his way of eating as strongly as he is set in the ways of fighting the King's wars. Left to himself, he will have nothing new. Even when we show him how tasty things are and how healthful, he will have nothing but salt pork and peas and beans, and so the scurvy takes him by the throat. I am so much convinced of these new discoveries concerning food that on this voyage I will get fresh vegetables into the skins of all my men even if I have to flog the hides from their backs. Quite likely it will come to just that!"

His voice became tense and his eyes took on a piercing quality, the first indication I had of the fires within him. The look of him that moment made it obvious he could become as stern as he seemed mild, and, with great suddenness, a volcanic quality that in the end led to his undoing. "Have they healed the Resolution's joints and stopped her leaks?" asked Mr. Banks. that." "They have tried," the captain answered. "I often think of my beginnings, with a father who was a common laborer. He learned his letters only recently, and that was because he wanted to read my books with his own eyes. England is a remarkable country. I am not a gentleman born, but I am treated as one, I who came into the Navy through a hawsepipe. 

And the same may be in store for you, Westermark.”

His mood changed to one of friendliness, and his eyes,

LOST EDEN 

43 which were none too warm, nevertheless gave me a look of cordiality and seemed to search for my best parts. 

I tried to learn as much as I could about the ways of life at sea in those days before sailing, and probably I got most from an old seaman named Edward Flint. Ed was truly a salty sailor, an incarnation of jack-tar himself. Of his forty-two years of life, more than thirty had been at sea. "Thirty years afloat is like sixty ashore," he said, and I could believe that when I looked at his weathered face and the huge, knotted talons which hung from his sleeves and served as hands. 

He could drink two bottles of rum without losing the sparkle of his pale gray eyes, and in this condition he would sing old sailing ditties in a way to shake the walls and bring the tavern keeper to threaten him with eviction.

One night as we sat in the Francis Drake Tavern he was well into his second bottle of rum and I, in my youthful curiosity, was contemplating the ordering of cordial waters, for those sweet, delicious-tasting drinks pleased my taste tremendously. "I am minded to try Oil of Venus tonight," I remember saying, when five of the King's men came in noisily, four of them armed with musket and pistol and the fifth a sergeant with a sword bumping at his side. "No one will leave until I give the word," said the sergeant. "A press gang," Flint whispered. "You," said the soldier to Flint, "stand over here. You will sail with a company of dragoons to settle some trouble with the 

American Colonies." "That I will not, ye blasted slaver!" His huge fist descended upon the table with a bang. "Hold your tongue, before I cut it out!" The sergeant drew the sword from its scabbard, and an ugly one it was, with two large nicks in the blade. I doubted not that it had seen rough usage. "I am signed on the Resolution," Flint barked, “and Captain Cook will deal with ye if ye lay a hand on me or my mate here." "Pfah! Whyn't ye say so, ye rum puncheon? The other two there, look sharp now and come with me." He pointed his

LOST EDEN 

sword at two young sailors who had returned from cruising the shores of France only a week before. The scurvy was still upon them. They appeared to have spent their pay freely, and likely had not drawn a sober breath since coming ashore.

As the two lads were led out, one of them threw me a farthing. "Here, matey," he called, "it's my last one and I'll not be needing it now as much as you."

Unsteadily he and his companion ambled off with the soldiers, whose boots struck the cobblestones smartly as they marched away into the night. 26 "Tis why a sailor spends so free," said Flint. “But they will never impress me again." "How can you be sure of it?" 

'Tis me last voyage, matey." He grinned at me cunningly. "And if ye have aught to think with in that pate of yours, this first one will be your last one also." "What do you mean?" "Now, now, belay there. Old Flint's tongue is not one that's loosened with rum. I'll tell ye when we sail, but not before. 

Ye'll know that I am right, too." He leaned over the table and whispered, “Them women is pretty nice down there in the islands." "So I've heard." "Never a doubt. Never a doubt. And as many as ye can serve. Oh, SET THE SAILS, ME MATEY!" He raised his voice like a clap of thunder and startled everyone in the taproom, and from then on he sang, and never a word more could I draw from him about his plans.

At last we were ready for sea, on the tenth day of July, 1776, and we had orders to make sail at dawn with the turning of the tide. It was the last evening ashore for the men, and they made the most of it. 

I went with them to Plymouth for a last look at old England, and I had scarce stepped from the pinnace upon the square stone steps of the old quay when I heard my name called. There stood Lorna, wearing a cape of blue broadcloth that reached down almost to her ankles, with a pointed hood drawn up over her head that cupped her face so that it looked like a lovely flower.

LOST EDEN 

45 "John!" was the single word she had called out, and that name I had heard thousands of times, but never before did it stir me so. There is that of music in the sound of a lover's voice which naught can equal. 

My heart leaped within me. "Lorna! What brings you so far from London?" "Don't you care to see me?”

A troubled look came into her wide blue eyes. A pathetic little smile hovered upon her lovely lips, and it so melted me that I forgot those most unpleasant moments in London when her anger flashed and her contempt stung me so intensely that I thought all was over between us. I had done my best to forget her as I prepared to sail away to the far ends of the earth, but now again I was afire, and all in a moment. “Yes! Yes, I am . . . tremendously glad," I stammered, searching for adequate words but finding none. "It is like our evenings together in Soho Square." "So you liked those evenings?" A taunting little smile flickered about her lips.

I thought that anyone with half an eye could see that I had fallen in love all the way and was all but daft with the torment of it.

I offered her my arm, half fearing that she would refuse it but longing for the touch of her. She took it gently at first, and then she drew me tightly to her in a way almost to make my legs wilt beneath me.

We began to walk through the streets of old Plymouth silently, not knowing what to say. But when one loves, it is not talk that explains and reveals; it is the physical storm within which arises at the touch of a hand, a sigh, a look, the pressure of a fond embrace.

I wished to say much, but knew not how to begin. Many women had come to Plymouth to wish us Godspeed, but I had not expected that Lorna would be one of them, nor that she would come for me alone. She stirred me again so deeply that I almost could have wished that she had remained away. It would indeed be difficult now to leave her.

LOST EDEN 

... as "Have you come here to tell me that we part as friends?" I began, scarce hoping for more than that, but longing to tell her what was in my heart. "There is no ill feeling?" "Ill feeling! No. No." Then a look came into her eyes which told me that now, if ever, I must speak out. "Lorna, I love you... I..." 

Her hands gripped mine tightly. Her deep blue eyes filled with tears and she laid her head upon my shoulder and began to sob. 

The nearness of her set my senses spinning. Her soft cheek was upon mine and her golden hair lightly touched my face. I held her close, and the sobs which shook her went through me as well, in a mingling of rapture and pain. "Why didn't you tell me?" Again I groped for words. "It is too late now. We sail at dawn. I should be returning to the boat even now. If you had said only one word . . .”

She continued to sob, and my distress increased. I went on with my talk senselessly. "One word and I would have gone running to you. And now you have come all the way from London. What other girl in the world could act so handsomely? No other girl in the world, unless..." I raised her head so that I could look into her eyes. "Do you love me too?” her. "Need I say it?" Then all I had hoped for was true.
