LOST EDEN 

47 Sweetly serious, she looked me full in the eyes. "A midshipman staying ashore when his ship sails?" She shook her head from side to side. "No, John." "I must stay! If you but want me, I'll do anything on earth."

She took my face in both her hands. "This is for remembrance,” she whispered, and gave me a long kiss. She held to me tightly and a bit desperately, I thought. "Now you must hurry to the boat."

She took my arm again, and we walked to the quay sadly in the gathering darkness.

A cheery hail from my friends waiting in the boat was a welcome stimulant which returned me partly to my senses. "Then it is good-by now," I managed to say, taking her in my arms once more. "Good-by for one long year upon another, until I return, if ever I do." "Good-by, John. God be with you.” 

I felt her warm tears upon my cheek, the first of such tears that ever had I known, and they added to my own sorrow to make it almost more than a man could bear. 

With the outside of me resolute, but a melting within, I turned my back on Lorna and stepped down to where my fellows waited in the pinnace.

We began slowly to pull away, and Lorna came to the very edge of the landing. As the distance between us increased, her slight figure grew dim in the waning twilight. She waved her kerchief high above her head, and a sad little flag of forlorn hope it seemed. 

I answered with a salute of my three-cornered hat, one more sailor off to sea leaving a broken heart ashore.

Chapter 

THERE WAS MUCH BUSTLE AND MERRIMENT ABOARD THE Resolution that last night in English waters, but I sought my berth. I wanted to be alone and think. In the morning I would see the last of my native land for several long years, and I knew I had best put cares behind me if I could, and look forward to days of freedom and forgetfulness, experiences in sunny lands with good companions. 

I went to my room below, a tiny affair, scarce large enough to swing three hammocks. With three sea chests on the deck, serving as chairs and table, we were well stowed, with hardly enough room to hang another hat.

In the light of a small oil lantern I saw Ned Thorson, a midshipman of seventeen. "John, we're off!" he exclaimed in high good humor. "The next time we walk upon land we will be far away." "Aye, Ned," I replied. I liked this lad and thought we would have a jolly time together. "Who is to be the third to live with us in this dark cubbyhole?" "They say 'tis a gentleman. We'll be in fine company.'

At that moment Lieutenant Bligh came to the door and we stood at attention. "Westermark, Thorson," he said curtly, "here is Lord Wadleigh, senior midshipman. He'll be in charge here."

Abruptly he turned away, and into the room came my sworn enemy. “Well, well!” he exclaimed. “If it isn't Johnny Muckface from the collieries!" "That is not a name I relish, sir." “But I like it very much, Johnny Muckface. And I like the 48

LOST EDEN 

49 wench you couldn't get. A tasty little bit, she was. One day I must tell you, Thorson, what fun we had at the home of Mr. Banks.” "And while you are at it," I said, “you might tell him how you came by that scar on your cheek." "Ah yes. Yes indeed. It minds me of a little score I have to pay. And plenty of time to pay it off, on this cruise. Let me see now, Westermark, we'll begin by assigning you the farthest hammock. there. When we get a bit of a sea, it will smack your rump against the bulkhead. You'll not change the hanging of it without my permission." 

And so that was to be our relationship for long years at sea. 

I knew that a green hand must abide by such conduct from a superior, but I had not thought to find the unctuous Lord Wadleigh in a position to give me orders. I burned to strike him again, then and there, and I could do naught but let the fire rage inside me.

I left him abruptly and walked about the ship for a time in the gathering darkness, and almost everywhere found our men with women. For most it was a last carouse. The bawdyhouses of 

Plymouth must have been deserted that night and the grogshops sold out of rum and gin. Drunken singing came from many quarters and licentious conduct openly prevailed until at last the women were ordered over the side and into the small boats which waited. 

Such sights as I saw deepened the hurt inside me. Was I to become like so many of my mates, taking my love so lightly wherever I might find it? I began to wish that I had stayed ashore, for somewhere beyond the mist that closed in upon us my Lorna's little boots trod the soil of fair England. Her faint, final good-by echoed in my head as I made ready to spend my first night on a ship.

I gained my hammock with difficulty. I twisted and turned to make myself comfortable, but my spine seemed to bend most miserably no matter how I laid myself. These contortions, together with thoughts of the ties I had sundered this day, kept me awake until well after the eight bells of midnight. Then I dozed off, but not for long, because I moved in my sleep and fell from my strange, swinging cat's cradle.

LOST EDEN 

I struck the deck with a resounding thump and awoke in the darkness with a severe pain in my left jaw and hip. My first befuddled thought was that the ship had struck a rock and would go down with all hands.

The laughter of my mates soon set me aright, but I gave up my attempt to sleep and dressed myself and went upon the main deck, where I waited for the dawn.

Although most of our people were asleep, many well sodden with alcohol, there yet was considerable noise from the livestock we had taken aboard. Those poor captive creatures whinnied and lowed and bleated and crowed all night in their close confinement below decks. 

King George desired that Cook might leave in distant lands as many useful domestic animals and fowls as possible, to multiply and serve mankind. Accordingly, on this ninety-foot bark we had shipped a bull and a cow, a ram and some ewes, a stallion and a mare, a buck and a doe, goats, rabbits, pigs, turkeys, geese, chickens, and many other living things, in addition to one hundred twelve human beings. Deep and broad of beam as she was, our ship, with its animate complement and endless stores, was indeed fantastically crowded. "If only we had some women," said our captain, "we would be a modern Noah's ark."

Our people greeted the dawn with much commotion and lusty shouting, and when the command to weigh anchor was given, a great cheer went up which echoed back from the shore. 

It was a moment longed for during the weeks of preparation, and one I never will forget. I think never was such a lusty chantey sung at the capstan bars as the great iron hook freed itself from the mud of the bay. Slowly our ship filled her sails and came alive and began to breast the sea.

As the Resolution gathered way, our cannon boomed out a salute of seventeen guns, that number being allotted to the First 

Lord of the Admiralty, Lord Sandwich, and we were answered by seventeen from the dimness of the shore. The mighty roaring sent a tingling up and down my spine. If gun salutes are intended to ennoble the spirit, then these served their purpose, because I felt

LOST EDEN 

51 myself part of greatness far beyond me, happily caught up in the march of destiny.

When we stood for the open sea, I saw Captain Cook alone on the quarter-deck, taking, it seemed, his last look at England forever. It is a moment that I well remember, for I thought also it might be my own last sight of my native land. I felt that I had much to lose, but he had more. High honors were his and sufficient means to enjoy them. Likewise the private ties of home, I am sure, were hard for him to cast aside, for he was devoted to his wife, a comely woman who was expecting her sixth child. 

Slowly, as the land faded beneath the gray sea, Cook turned about on his long legs and noted the trim of the sails, a captain of the Seven Seas, every inch of him, his uppermost thoughts thereafter given over to the ship whose destiny he ruled. 

I remained gazing at the spot where Lizard Head, the last speck of land, had sunk below the horizon, for I could think of nothing else to do. Soon I was to learn so much about a ship that my work seldom would be finished, but at that moment I leaned comfortably on the rail, idle in the bliss of ignorance. 

Ed Flint paused beside me. "That's the last of England, eh, matey?” he said. "I hope not the last," I replied. "And what's wrong with hopin' it's the last?" "Do you really wish not to see England again?” "That I do. And you, too. Come, now, I know the minds of many of the lads aboard, and I dare say you are no different. 

They will not come back. Not this time. They will stay in the islands, and so will you." "That would not be to my liking." "But you may think different when the worms are in the bread, and you've no meat but salt pork and naught to wash it down with but stinking water with slime in it, aye, and wigglers, too. What d'ye say then?" He rolled his quid of tobacco in his mouth and spat neatly over the rail. "It is not a pretty picture you paint." "No, 'tis not. And the press gang? What of that? Ye're a blooming sailor man now, and when ye get back ashore the King's men will collar ye for another cruise."

LOST EDEN 

Whether I might be impressed or not, I did not know, but the possibility of it struck me of a sudden. ""Tis not only the abuse that has swayed me," Flint continued, "'tis the pleasant life of the islands that I mind me of, and ye'll know what I mean when we get there and see the women."

With that he gave me a knowing pinch, and he grinned as he said, "Good luck to ye, mate."

At this time the swell of the sea produced a strange sinking feeling in my stomach, and quickly I began to pace the deck to avoid what I had hoped would not happen, but I met with ill luck, and for three days I lay with the misery of seasickness upon me, and my desire to live was but the feeblest.

Nor did I receive any sympathy from the sailors. "Look!" said one. "He has had nothing of hardship and already he wants to die." "See what I have here," said Lord Wadleigh, holding out a piece of meat. "How about a bite of nice fat pork?"

At that I retched again, and these rough fellows laughed their heartiest.

Now and again old Flint would look in on me, and his wrinkled face would crack into a genuine grin. He seemed to be my only friend. "Stay with it, mate," he would say, and sometimes he would lay a gnarled hand upon my brow as a father might touch a sick child. "I knew the feel of seasickness long ago, but I have forgot. 

Maybe 'tis because I've seen so much that's worse at sea. Ye must tussle with it alone now while I bear a hand aloft. So many green ones are on my watch that I am the only man to be trusted on the yards."

I watched him amble off, his capable hands swinging in a way to mind me of an ape.

Three days of agony it was, with me wishing that every dip of the bow would take the ship under and set her on the bottom of the sea but, miracle of miracles, on the fourth day I struggled to my feet, then paced the deck, then smiled and ate a hearty meal.

LOST EDEN 

53 After that the movement of the sea served as a tonic to me, as did the clean, fresh breezes which filled our sails. I found pleasure in walking the deck, but I staggered much and held fast for support to whatever lay at hand, which awkward action amused the crew.

A young man's first landfall is something which he long remembers. It marks the successful outcome of a struggle with the unfriendly forces of a mighty ocean. He feels a pride in his ship which has carried him through, and as well the spirit of the explorer and the satisfaction of accomplishment. 

Such emotions were enhanced by the charm of the island of Madeira, the first land we touched after sixteen days on the open sea.

Of a sunny morning this lovely gem of green arose from the horizon, one continuous hill of a wonderful height, extending from east to west, a declivity on the south side cultivated and interspersed with vineyards, wherein the merchants of the place have fixed their country seats, the white of their walls and warm red roofs rendering the prospect most enchanting. Below that idyllic community, in the blue waters of Funchal Roads, we let go the anchor for the first time. "Are you ready with your sketching kit?" asked Dr. Anderson. "You and I have work to do this day. This land has been known for three hundred and fifty years, but none have had much inclination to examine its plants. We may find some new ones." "I am ready," I replied, "and I will welcome a long walk on land."

When I stepped out of the pinnace and felt land under my feet once more, a great zest for living came over me. I had crossed many leagues of ocean and had come safe to a new country where people spoke a strange language and wore bright sashes and head windings, the men with large gold rings in their ears. I wished to pause and examine the small shops which displayed crude leathern boots and clothing of bright red and green, and I longed to taste the wine which a swarthy fellow dispensed from a goatskin carried on his back, but instead off we went, up the wooded hillside, to seek plants. And on this day I became an explorer in a real sense. 

We had not gone far when my companion stopped abruptly.

LOST EDEN 

"Hello! Here is a new one." Stooping, he pulled up a small plant with red berries. "We must find out if it is useful for food or medicine. You must question the people often. Learn something of their languages, if you can, but much can be done in the sign language. If this is good for naught but bird food, it may be useful, because many birds are valuable to men."

He did not need to tell me to unlimber my sketchbook. "I have just the red for the berries,” I said. "Good. We will call this... let me see . . . I have it," and with a pencil he wrote on my sketching paper, "Westermarkus Madericus."

And so, in my first few minutes ashore on a foreign island, I had one of earth's growing things named after me and I was a plant explorer whose name would be in scientific books for all time to come. The possible dangers and tribulations of the voyage ahead were forgotten in my enthusiasm, and I count this as one of the perfect days in my life.

While we were exploring for plants, our people tried to catch fish for food, so that our stores might be the better husbanded, but the only fish taken in sufficient numbers were shark. 

They were the first sharks I had seen, and truly they are hideous things, but I only partly understood how they could inspire such cruelty among the sailors. A sailor is merciful to other fish, but to a shark he will do any dastardly deed of murder which comes into his head.

It was decided to cook and eat the younger sharks, but the crew would have none of that fiendish food. 

I was surprised when I saw Cook take a large helping and eat it with apparent relish, he who could have ordered the best of food for his table. "It is a good thing," he said, "to have fresh food like this. 

Although some think it has nothing to do with the prevention of scurvy, I hold that it does, and my continual thought is for fresh food of all kinds, whether fish, fowl, meat, or fruits and vegetables." "I will have some," said Dr. Anderson, passing his plate, "if for no other reason than to set a good example to the crew, as you do. I, for one, accept every word of that paper you wrote

LOST EDEN 

55 for the Royal Society. And you, Westermark?" he continued, offering me a slice of shark's meat. "Gladly, sir,” I said with great misgivings.

The first bite almost sent me to the rail to unload. It not only was tough, but it tasted as spoiled fish smell. I could chew but I could not swallow. The shark is a scavenger of the sea and will eat carrion, and his flesh takes on an odor and taste accordingly. Hurriedly I stuffed a great dab of chopped pickle into my mouth and began to tell myself that all was pickle and very good. 

Cook smiled. "It is a courageous try, Westermark," he said. "You may spit it out now. Better luck next time."

I understood now why he had invited me to sup with him. 

He wished to give me an early start on new eating habits which were to be so essential to our voyage. I was determined to measure up to his expectations, and so I gulped hard and down went my first morsel of shark. "Good!" cried Dr. Anderson. "That much at least you have in your system to fight the worst plague of the sea." "It is a good example for the crew," said Cook, “when the greenest hand will do it. Our seamen are much set in their ways and will not try one new thing until their officers shame them into it. More than once I have been tempted to flog a man for refusing to eat what is good for him."

east into the broad Atlantic, with the sun rising higher in the sky each day until it scorched the deck and reflected like white fire from the sea. We were approaching the equator. That imaginary line had always stimulated my imagination. It somehow seems more important than the other parallels near it, especially to seamen who take much store in the ceremony they call "crossing the line."

It is at the equator that one meets Neptunus Rex, and the way I made his acquaintance was to me most unexpected. Lord 

Wadleigh, with a patch over one eye, a jury-made crown on his head, and a belaying pin in his hand, bore down on me. "By King Neptune's order," he said, “you are under arrest. 

Come with me.' 

When I accompanied him into the waist, I saw most of the hands gathered there. At sight of me a great cheer went up, and my captor led me before a man with a long wig of sisal sitting behind a cask and pretending to be a judge. He wore a robe of the red cloth we had brought for trading. "Mr. Westermark," he said gravely, "it is understood that you wish to invade King Neptune's realm, and if you do, you must expiate the crimes with which you are accused, namely: cheating at cards, stealing grog, vomiting into King Neptune's ocean (hearty laughter at this point), eating of the King's sacred shark, objecting to the odors of the livestock aboard, and chasing wenches without success (more laughter). Have you anything to say for yourself?"

I did have much to say, but was not permitted one word. 56

LOST EDEN 

57 "No! No!" the hands cried. "Very well," said the judge, "I find you guilty. You are sentenced to three duckings in the equator." 

A block and tackle had been rigged to the end of the main boom, and dangling from the falls was a rude seat. Into this they forced me, and Wadleigh shouted, "Whip!"

Eager hands whipped the line and up I went to the boom's end. Immediately they swung me far outward, then down I went, clothes and all, into the Atlantic.

I dropped far below the surface, and in my surprise I had not taken a deep breath, and so I struggled there in much discomfort until the drag of the ship brought me to the surface. They hauled me up, wet and dripping and blinking. "How's old Nep? My regards to the king! Bow again, you landsman!" they shouted.

Down I went again, but this time my lungs were filled with air. The third time was easier yet, but the water was much colder than I thought, and I was swung back inboard shivering and coughing and spitting.

The men had a merry time, and they seemed to like me the better for that sport, but I did not feel so good toward them when I discovered that I could have bought immunity for four days' wine ration and none had told me of that custom. 

These were sunny days of hope and longing, and for many of us this was a period of training which seemed only fun at the time but which stood us in good stead as we headed for adventure in distant lands.

As a midshipman, I had a sword, and I had regarded it only as a proud badge of honor until now, when Sergeant Vancouver undertook to teach me what a swordsman of the King should know. With unsharpened and unpointed blades, we thrust and parried on the deck, and this likable fellow made the motions of cutting me to ribbons while our people laughed. But at length I found that I could come near to holding my own, thanks to his clever instruction. "You'll do," he said at the end of a most tiring lesson, “unless one day you tangle with a Frenchman, and then you'd best stand clear and use your musket."

LOST EDEN 

But I had also much to learn about my double-barreled rifle. The aiming of it was not difficult, and soon I became a fair marksman shooting at flotsam or an occasional shark; but skill which all sought to acquire was quick reloading and priming, for life often depended upon the speed of recharging the piece. I came to value my cartouche box far above my sword.

Often in this period of balmy weather I would sit with Omai in the bow, looking for whales and porpoises by day and wondering at the curious blobs of phosphorous light which came to the surface when our prow cut these waters. At such times he would be giving me lessons in the Tahitian language, when he was not describing the beauties of his islands, until I could fair smell the flowering trees upon them. "My girl's name Hokoolele. Mean shooting star," he said one evening. "Ver' nice. But her mother is Humuhumunukunukuapuaa. Mean grumbling old woman with snout like pig." "But your Hokoolele is charming?" "Yah, yah! Much olapa. Mean joys of woman's body." 

And sometimes he would recite a kind of Tahitian Song of

Solomon, the chant of a beautiful woman who offers delirium and rest to her lover. In this manner I soon was able to converse with him in his own language, if not fluently at least with a satisfaction which was not lessened by my discovering that Captain Cook, with all his accomplishments, had himself mastered none of the dialects of the lands he had explored.

Those were pleasant days indeed, compared to others in store for us, as we sailed into the South Atlantic, headed for the Cape of Good Hope, where we would gain access to the Pacific Ocean and thence as far toward the South Pole as circumstances might permit.

Nowhere had any navigator been able to enter the Pacific except by this route or by weathering the stormy southern tip of South America. Cook's orders read that he should first seek a continent near the South Pole and thereafter sail up the length of the Pacific to the Arctic, where he would seek a passage there, either eastward or westward into the Atlantic. A vast undertaking this was. It encompassed the farthest south and farthest north, as well

LOST EDEN 

59 as the search for land in between, and much of it in seas no man had sailed before.

At Good Hope we waited a full month for the Discovery, and at last she sailed into the bay, and a pretty sight she was. She would have been with us a week sooner if a gale had not blown her off the coast. Upon the whole, she was only these seven days longer in the passage from England than we had been. 

On the thirtieth of November, at five in the morning, we weighed and stood out of the bay. We steered a southeast course, with a very strong gale from the westward, followed by mountainous seas, which made the ship roll and tumble exceedingly and gave us a great deal of trouble to preserve from injury the stock we had on board. Notwithstanding all our care, several goats, especially the males, died, and also some sheep. This misfortune was, in a great measure, owing to the cold, which we now began to feel most sensibly.

The seams of the Resolution had been sprung in her previ ous voyage, and now we found that they were badly caulked and also that they had been further opened by the heat of the tropics we had but recently encountered. In my room I found my clothes soaked by dripping water. It was the same with all aboard. Cold water dripped everywhere. It wet the spare sails and mildew came upon them.

Men began to show signs of scurvy, those ominous red blotches on arms and legs, and were given a wort composed of malt and water. It helped, but not enough. Raw sores appeared where their flesh had brushed against the rigging. Cracks came into their hands and faces from the action of the cold, wet wind. "North to the sunny islands!" was the cheering phrase which ran along the deck, but Cook continued south.

Chapter HOW SOON I BECAME WELL ACQUAINTED WITH MY SHIPMATES WAS 

to me surprising. They were like neighbors of long standing after 

I had known them but a month. And I knew them well indeed toward the end of the fourth month when one day we sailed lazily into the teeth of a raging Antarctic storm which God seems to reserve for these antipodes of hell, perhaps because He expects no ships in these wastes of ice and cold. Indeed, we knew that we tempted Providence every day we made southing. 

On a day in December, toward noon, I was watching the dim, threatening sun slip along the horizon, which it seemed to hug for warmth and left reluctantly for only a few hours around midnight.

We had clothes of sealskin now, and knitted caps for the most part, and furry mitts of one kind or another, and the pat, pat of these mitts against each other to gain circulation of blood was as common a noise on deck as the sharp song of the rigging. 

The furs about our faces glistened white with the hoarfrost from our breath, and icicles formed upon beards and mustaches to render us a strange, spiny-looking breed of men. 

My thoughts were mostly of these fellow men who passed me, each so cold and lonely inside his wrappings and yet so dependent upon the others and upon the heaving ship which bound our lives together. Each nursed his own grievances, and their small bickerings were growing large for lack of useful work to do. "A fine frosty day," I said to Garth Harworthy as he ambled past me. He paused and let his pale blue eyes rove the sky, while his breath sailed off to leeward in little white clouds like smoke. "A bloody fine bastard of a day," he replied, with never a 60

LOST EDEN 

61 glance at me. In his late thirties, he was an old man, his face wrinkled by the winds of distant seas and tanned by the burning sun of many a tropical land. Two pouches of leathery skin hung limp from his jowls in the manner of a seagoing squirrel who could find no nuts to fill them. Often I wondered if these sacks were formed by the gritting of his teeth, for always his teeth seemed to be clenched, asleep or awake. "It seems as good a day as we can expect in these latitudes." "Ye've no weather eye, me lad," he objected, spitting a neat stream of brown into the icy gray waters which slapped the ship, he being one of the many who could spit to windward in half a gale. "D'ye see the cloud there about midships, the one that's a bit darker than the rest?" "Yes, I do. It appears to have something like hair on the top of its head."

He slapped the cold rail with his big mitt and laughed with a kind of bitter chuckle. "I say 'tis wind, and lace-panties Bligh on the poop shakes out the tops'ls and crowds the sail on 'er. I'll lay ye ten to one he splits 'em before the noon water ration." 

I had seen the mainsail split in the Bight of Biafra, though it was double-reefed, and such activity and excitement as followed was high adventure to me. In my innocence, I thought I might never have a better yarn to tell when I returned to Newcastle, and so I made note of the straining stays and the swarming of men up the shrouds and how some thought we would be dismasted. "I hope we'll not have trouble," I said, for the thundering crack of a split sail in a reefer's ears is something to resound in his dreams for years to come. "She's nervous," Harworthy said, looking at the topmost canvas. "She's like a horse that senses something in the dark beside the road. They didn't caulk her right and they didn't fit her right, and anyhow, once around the world is enough for the likes of her. 

She's strained and sprung, and today she's skittish. She don't rise to the water, confident-like. "Tis plain as day she's asking for a harbor and a mooring, and all she gets is deep sea and ice." 

He placed his ear to the mainmast, like a doctor listening to a heartbeat. He watched the movement of the sails and he listened to the wind in the rigging. He went forward, treading lightly

LOST EDEN 

on the badly caulked deck as though feeling for heartbeats with his feet.

Aye, that sorry man must have sensed the presence of the unnatural, for soon I noticed two brilliant little clouds on either side of the sun. What struck me first was the flaming brightness of them, seeming almost as intense as the sun itself. Both were alike as two twins, elliptical, standing on end, each spaced the same distance from the sun and on a horizontal line with it. "Garth," I called, "there are three suns in the sky. What do you make of them?"

He stared at this curious phenomenon first with a blank face. Then he took on the look one might expect to see on Judgment Day. "God, Father in heaven!" he cried. “The sundogs!”

Tales of sea serpents and other apparitions in the ocean had not interested me beyond noticing the effects of such weird beliefs upon simple-minded seamen. But here, before my own eyes, stood an apparition which I could not explain. A clamor arose on deck as all turned to stare. It was as though they expected the Angel of 

Death to appear in the sun and beckon one and all. "What does it mean?" I asked Garth, feeling more than a little the awe which gripped the crew. "It means cold, COLD, me lad! The goddam cold that freezes yer eyelashes shut." He seemed almost to congeal in his own boots.

I have since learned that most mariners believe these sundogs to be caused by fine ice particles in the air which reflect the sun like a mirage. "Too cold to snow," is what they usually say. 

And there must be wind to blow the icy mist aloft. All seem to shudder at the thought, and with gestures try to describe the cold which they cannot describe in words. 

The wind came directly from the sundogs and it freshened as we stared. Cook heard the murmuring of the hands and came on deck. Calm, as always, he looked toward the sun and, as usual, his presence instilled confidence into the crew.

He gave the apparition but a hasty glance, and then his eyes snapped to the dark cloud with wind-blown hair above it, and I could perceive a slight but quick stiffening of his spine.

LOST EDEN 

63 "Strike the tops'ls!" he commanded in a loud, firm voice which carried to every part of the ship. 

More than a score of men sprang instantly to obey, and they began to work their way up the shrouds like a horde of ants bent upon a feast of honey. At this moment our thoughts were all the same-to save our ship, the life of all of us; the urge to care for the almost-human old girl who pitched and rolled us by day and by night and breasted every sea at our bidding. Up they went, without faltering, and never do the masts seem so high as when men climb them and shrink in size. 

But the people had a late start in their race with the wind. 

I heard a crack aloft like one of our six-pounders going off, and the upper main topsail fetched away the topmast and fell down to foul the lower topsails. The pennant which had flown above it broke clean away and scudded down-wind like an awkward but fast-flying bird.

The deck came up at me so fast it bent my knees, and the rail struck me under the chin as the ship heeled over and dipped her lee scuppers into the icy water. 

The ominous dark cloud we noticed had suddenly grown to immense proportions, and now it blotted out the sun. Two small extensions on either side gave it the appearance of a monster with outstretched arms swooping down upon us.

The wind howled in the rigging so that shouts seemed only whispers. I saw the Adventure, about a mile to leeward, and she also was heeling, but her topsails were down, and I thought her captain must have been alert to take such timely warning from our predicament. It was the last I saw of her before the black cloud closed in upon us, full of sleet which stung our faces like needles shot from a gun.

As neither Garth nor I were on watch at the time, we stood by for orders. The deck gave a great heave and all but pitched us aloft. 

The Resolution lay on her side so long I thought she never would right herself. Gray-green water came over the port rail and cascaded into the well. Cook had permitted only one fire outside the galley, a small fire in an iron kettle at the bottom of the well, partly for warmth, but mostly to help keep the ship dry and stave off illness. I heard this fire go out with a sizzle, and saw a small wisp of steam come up. "Yes. Yes. I'll try," I shouted to Garth, in answer to his request, but I will confess that my mind was more upon the gathering darkness and the storm. "Always wanted... mantel clock, she did... always. Two quid not enough. Can't buy... bloody... two quid. Maybe you . . someday."

The long, lean cook came crawling along the deck, reaching for handholds but seldom finding any. He had a gash over his left eye and blood trickled from it. Several red drops on his chin seemed to be frozen.

Bligh pulled him to his feet in front of us. "Christ yes, you "Sir," the cook shouted, “shall . . . put out . . . galley fire?” whoreson!" Then he resumed his crawling. There would be no hot food tonight.
