Help... cut away if necessary." aloft . . . furl, damn it!

That meant climbing to the top of the mainmast where hands were struggling to free the fouled topsails. I had been aloft more than a few times and had learned to lend a hand with some intelligence, but I was yet far from being an able sailor. 

Garth started first, with his jaws locked tight. When I laid a hand upon the shrouds to climb I found them already coated with glistening ice. They were hard and slippery. As I climbed, the wind became stronger and colder. It shrieked in the rigging with

LOST EDEN 

65 a thousand sharp, angry notes. The sea was running higher and I could see mountainous waves rushing upon us. When one would strike, I would hold tight while the ship heeled over so far that I could almost stand upright upon the icy network of ropes. 

When I reached the futtock shrouds, the ship was no longer directly beneath me, but only that angry, churning water. 

We took another large wave in the well, and as I held on tight, far over the sea, I saw a full-grown pig come up with the backwash of water and float over the rail. Its mouth was open and it must have been squealing, but I heard no sound from it as it bobbed up and down in the chop of the sea and floated away. 

I caught a glimpse of Cook on the quarter-deck, calm and tall, his left leg stiff and his right knee bent to meet the tilt of the deck. What he was thinking, I could not know, but he held that crew with his eyes and his demeanor and drove them to their tasks without a word. There was that in his eye which commanded and could not be disobeyed. It was as though he held the ship together and held the people on it with his very glance. 

The men struggling with the dismasted upper topsail now managed to cut her free, and down she came. I think there was nothing else for it, and they did the right thing, but the yard struck Garth on the top of the head and he lost his grip and fell heavily upon me.

I seized him with my right hand, but he could not regain his hold before the ship gave a lurch and left him hanging there, with my clutch upon his jacket as his only hope of salvation. 

I called for help. Aye, perhaps I screamed.

Lord Wadleigh had followed us into the rigging and was now scarce six feet away. "Lend a hand!" I bellowed into the wind.

But his first reaction, as always, was to leer at me, taking joy in any unfortunate predicament in which I found myself.

I cursed him roundly as I felt my hands quickly tiring, both the one with which I held Garth and the one which strained to keep my own hold upon the freezing rigging. 

There is a superhuman strength which comes to a man at times like that, or I could not have held him a moment. But slowly my cold, tired fingers began to lose the fight. Garth turned

LOST EDEN 

his face up to mine with less fear than I might have expected as he dangled from my fingers. He seemed to know that the hateful thing he had feared so long had caught up with him at last. It was as though he were saying, "I told you so."

Then the strength of my fingers failed, and I could do naught but let him go.

He hung in the air a moment, growing smaller, and then he splashed into the mad ocean. No boat could weather that sea, nor indeed could we come into the wind at this moment to save our souls.

Unable to swim, he nevertheless came to the surface once, his hands clawing the water, and I had one last look at him. I thought his teeth were chattering, as well they might have been, but perhaps he was grinding them once more to prepare for the greater adventure beyond this life. "I'll buy it!" I shouted. "I'll buy the clock for her!" 

I knew not what else to say. I know he did not hear me, but I hoped that he understood. It may have eased those few moments remaining to him before the icy hand of the Antarctic clutched him and took him to that land beyond the sundogs and the storms of stinging sleet, where lie the pleasant things about which apprentices dream when they run away to sea. "What did you call me?" The surly voice of Lord Wadleigh sounded in my ear. "A goddam ape, am I?" “Yes, and a stupid ass to boot!" I shouted, for at the moment it seemed to me that I had spent all my strength, aye, risked my very life to save a mate, while Wadleigh stood idly by, too stupid or indifferent to help. 

His undying hate of me flooded into his ugly face and matched the fury of the storm which whipped us.

Then he swung a fist and clipped me on my left ear, which for some time had been almost cold enough to fall off, and indeed I thought he might well have knocked it into the sea, judging from the pain I felt.

Now of all the places I would choose for a go at fisticuffs, this was the least desirable, high in the iced rigging over a thundering cold sea with hands so tired and numb and spent that I scarce could make them clasp a line. Yet swing I did, and I struck

LOST EDEN 

67 his nose. It was more brittle than soft, and it seemed to crack. A little blood began to flow out with the frost of his breath. 

His small brown eyes grew wild, with a look resembling that of Garth when he fell. Here was a mad man, with some viciousness inside him I could not understand. Nor was I calm, indeed. I knew for the first time in my life that there comes a turn in the affairs of men when fight they must, though odds are against them, though reasons for a struggle are slight, though little is to be gained and all may be lost. All this I had to learn in the rigging of the groaning Resolution, far down on the cold bottom of the world, where sundogs are set in the sky to guard a forbidden region of ice and snow. 

I saw Wadleigh's fist shoot out again. I felt it strike my chin. My tired, cold hands gave up their struggle and came away from the icy shrouds. I hung in the air, and the masts and spars of the Resolution floated upward. I thought it could not happen to me. But yes, I was falling from aloft.

Chapter 10 

I WAITED FOR THE CHURNING, ICY SEA TO ENGULF ME. PERHAPS I 

closed my eyes. I do not know. In a fall, all reason seems to be suspended. Life is a vacuum, a wait, a terrible surprise, a disbelief.

I felt a sharp pain in my left foot. It seemed to be trying to part from my ankle. It had caught in the fouled lines which had been cut away from the topsails, but this I did not understand for some moments. Upside down, I saw the masts of the Resolution swing giddily. The men also seemed upside down upon the yards. 

Why did the water not close in upon me? Ah, there the sea came at last, over my head and shoulders, striking as keen a blow with its icy hand as any headsman ever struck with ax. Yet there was something of warmth in it, in contrast to the bitter chill of the wind. 

It took my breath away. Then it was gone. I had been dipped in the waters of the Antarctic Circle as I had in those of the equator, but this was a different game. I knew my predicament, and I sought to grasp the line which held me, but that I could not do. Up and down I went, with the dizzy movement of the ship. Sometimes the water would race past my nose, but more often it slapped me in the face. Once I struck the hull a crunching blow just above her line of barnacles.

Looking past my feet, I saw men above me heaving upon the line which held me, and with the next righting of the ship I found myself tossed into the flapping mainsail. I seized her buntlines in a dead man's grip and locked my arms about them. 

Soon I was straddling the boom, looking in amazement at a ship right side up, my own ship, and mates who had pulled me from the roaring sea. A trickle of ice water ran down my back. 68

LOST EDEN 

69 I took off my knitted stocking cap to wring it out, and as I did so, the cold wind and sleet slapped me as the waves had done. "Fetch from the boom. dry one!" shouted the bosun, and pulled me

I could scarcely stand upon the heaving deck because of the pain in my foot, but I was most thankful for my return from an icy grave. 

What sails were not blown away were now being furled or reefed so that we could heave to, and we headed into the wind to meet the increasing swells head on. As a man looks at a knife which has cut his finger, I looked up to the yard from which I had fallen, but it was almost obscured in the gathering darkness. 

Although the time was now only a little past midday, an eerie reddish night had fallen, broken only by one small hole in the murk, through which I could see dark clouds scudding astern like the smoke of a broadside from our guns. "Here!" called the bosun, reappearing with another cap from some dim corner of the deck. "We're ... damn well in for it now, chappy."

With one arm around a stanchion, I pulled the cap over my cold ears, which I touched gingerly lest they break and fall on.

The wave which came at us then must have been about the size of Lizard Head. My eyes were not forward, and I could not tell for sure. But I knew it for a murderous monster when my knees crumpled and I sat down heavily upon my sore ankle. 

The ship seemed to stop still and shudder. She seemed to strain and break apart and give up the struggle. Water surged around me to my waist, and as I looked up, one of the ship's boats raced past me and over the side and went bobbing out of sight. "We're done for!" someone shouted. “Boat . . . gone!"

I could not tell where the voice came from, and thought perhaps another hand had gone into the sea. "Rig man line!" The capable bosun was back again, placing a line in my hands. He had made the end of it fast. "Carry . . . aft.”

Suddenly I was in the air again, the deck some feet below me. Then it came up and crumpled my legs under me. A solid

LOST EDEN 

wall of water struck me, and aft I went with it, the line racing through my hands.

I fetched up heavily against the wheelhouse, and when I regained my feet I was high above the sea. I think the stern of the ship was out of water, making the rudder useless. We seemed to be coasting down a steep hill.

I secured the line with considerable satisfaction, and then 

I spoke to the helmsman. "What do you make of it?" I asked, but he did not answer. 

His eyes stared into the strange, dark, murderous waves ahead. "What," I repeated, “do you make of it?” "Go... the devil!" he shouted, not deigning to look at me. "I can steer . . . goddam ship to hell.”

The man at the wheel stared ahead with glassy eyes, trying to penetrate the murk which all but obscured the bow, his muscles tensed, feeling for the next murderous swell which was certain to be racing at us. I saw now that masts, spars, and rigging were coated with silvery ice which gleamed dully in what amber rays of the sun managed to pierce the gloom. I seized the line I had rigged, and it also was now coated with ice. 

At the Cape of Good Hope, Cook had surveyed the stalls and pens and calculated that he could stow more stock aboard. Accordingly he had added two young bulls, two heifers, two young stone horses, two mares, two rams, several ewes and goats, and some rabbits and poultry.

As I clawed along the frosty man line, I saw a chicken frozen solid in ice which filled a corner by a hatch. I thought of 

Mr. Banks's description of death by freezing, the sense of warmth and the desire to sleep. A lucky chicken, that, sleeping warm and snug in the ice of a pitching, yawing ship headed for the South Pole.

When I opened the door to a companionway, a crazed duck flew to my head and sent its sharp toes through my cap. Then it sailed over the side and out into the red-black night which had descended upon us at noon.

On the dim lower deck I saw other fowl flying about and heard their mad screechings. Their cages must all have been bashed in by the storm.

LOST EDEN 

71 A lamp swung from a beam and flickered and guttered, and suddenly in front of it the dark bulk of Omai appeared. "My great hog!" he shouted. “Come, help.”

He trembled and his teeth chattered. Likely he felt the cold more than the rest of us, for he had known so little of it in his life. 

Going forward with him, I saw a horse wedged in a doorway. Its rump stood high in the air, but its head was down, and 

I noticed that its left forefoot was broken. A bone protruded, but little blood appeared, perhaps because it froze almost as fast as it flowed.

A seaman appeared with an ax, lifted it high, and brought it down heavily upon the horse's head. A good and merciful blow, that. Slowly the hind legs relaxed, and the animal settled to the deck and expired. We would have more than enough of horse meat if the cook ever got the galley fire lighted again. 

I heard Omai sobbing and, turning, saw tears in his eyes.

Mr. Peckersgill came crawling over the dead horse and surveyed the scene. "Westermark," he said, “you are about done in. You had best take a caulk.”

That is what I had thought some time ago, but I had continued with the necessary work, using up some unknown store of energy which Nature seems to keep in reserve for such emergencies. “Aye, aye, sir,” I replied, and staggered to my room.

I took off my wet clothes, and a chill seized me and set me shivering as though I had the ague. Quickly I donned dry gear, but my teeth chattered and rattled in my head. The ship pitched and thundered and groaned, but by now I cared little what might befall her. The last I remember, before a merciful sleep claimed me, was looking at two small icicles hanging from the deck above me. Aye, there would be no drip upon me in this sleep; the ship was caulked with ice.

Chapter 11 

IT WAS EVER CAPTAIN COOK'S WAY TO KEEP HIS PLANS TO HIMSELF. 

No one knew whither we were bound, and colder it grew, day by day, as we bore southward, toward the Pole. "Land ho!" shouted the lookout one day, and lusty cheers rang in that miserable ship. Not in over four months had our feet touched aught but the heaving decks of that wet, cold ship. 

But when we fetched what we thought was the land, we found only ice. A great ice island lay floating in the sea. As we drew nearer we could feel the chill of it. It appeared to rise straight up, a pale, wintry wall, reminding us, in a sad way, of the white cliffs of the Channel at home so far behind us. 

Southward we continued to sail, and soon the sea was filled with these ice islands, each reaching far out under the water to snatch the planking from our bottom.

One day we thought we had discovered a southern continent at last, for great cliffs arose from the water and extended east and west as far as we could see. But this also proved to be ice, and so steep were the cliffs that no landing could be made. Nor did we dare to go near them, for constantly came roaring sounds like peals of thunder, at which great avalanches of ice would break away and plunge into the sea. And sometimes mountains of ice would arise near the ship, for this was a birthplace of ice islands or bergs, as some have called them, taking the name from the German. 

There were, too, smaller bergs, many of them more sizable than several ships put together, but at that lesser than the monstrous peaks of floating ice.

It was the smaller bergs, by some called floes, that at least contributed to our welfare. Some of these were like miniature 72

LOST EDEN 

73 tablelands, their partially flat surfaces being a few feet to a dozen feet above the sea. Sometimes it was possible to take the ship directly beside such a floe, or at least reach its side in a small boat which members of the party would hold there while others foraged for water that had melted in hollows of the icy "shore." 

The ice itself, though formed from salt water, was free of brine through some curious provision of nature I did not understand. Thus the water in those pools was fresh, unless it was so near the edge of the floe that salt spume from storm-tossed waves had blown into it. Scooped up with buckets that were passed from hand to hand as fire fighters do at a blaze, we filled casks in the small boats or the great water barrels on the deck of the Resolution. 

Unless you have known the misery of bad drinking water, and not enough of it at that, good or bad, you cannot picture how welcome was this clear sweet liquid so providentially provided from the midst of the desolation that surrounded us. 

Group of odd birds, strongly marked with black and white so that they seemed not unlike dressed-up toys, appeared on some of the icy islands, and so a shooting party set out in the pinnace to add some fresh food to our larder. These birds were called penguins and they did not fly at our approach, nor actually could they fly with their rudimentary wings which were far too small for their heavy bodies. And whether from stupidity or lack of any enemy to fear, they came to us as friendly as tame ducks, walking pert and upright in a comical manner. "It seems," said Dr. Anderson, “that in all their history they have had no enemy on the ice, and so they expect no harm from us. But watch them as they go into the water for food. They must have enemies there, because they hesitate. Now, see those on the edge of the ice pushing one of their number into the water. That one is their explorer. See, he comes up, and now all the others dive for food. I would like to draw the seine and see what foes they have below."

Dr. Anderson further determined that it was the females who did the pushing, and always it was a male whom they threw into the water for a test dive. If he were not eaten, it was safe for them to follow.

The penguins tasted of fish, as most sea birds do, but we

LOST EDEN 

ate them on the calm days when the cook could safely build a fire in the galley.

Often I tried to sketch this changing panorama of ice and snow, for cold beauty lay on every hand, but I made poor work of it. My fingers were chapped and swollen, and a numbing stiffness had crept into them, so that they would not obey. I drew as a child of ten.

But I tried to fix in my mind the colors, for ice is blue, aye, and purple and sea green, and no more fantastic fairy castles have been imagined than those the frost has built. "If I live to paint again, and if my hands recover their ability," I thought, "I will have much work to do."

I drew some comfort from the paintings and sketches I had made already, and one day when I rummaged through them in the 

Antarctic twilight my heart stopped still, for there at the very bottom of my chest lay a pencil sketch of Lorna. 

A moldy dampness had come into the chest from the bottom and had discolored and all but obliterated part of her image, but she looked at me much in the way she had in those first days 

I had known her.

Again I heard the rustle of her skirts and smelled the perfume which lingered when she had passed. I wondered how she fared, and what swains pursued her through the corridors of the Banks mansion, and what luck they might be having. 

I tried to rub the green-brown stain from the sketch, but only made it darker and the drawing less distinct. "This I have done to you, Lorna," I thought. "I have broken your heart and smudged the last likeness of you I may ever see upon this earth. I did not know how pleasant 'twould be to have an image of you hard by the South Pole. I must paint you in color from the image in my mind if ever I paint again. Yes, if ever... .”

Each day the sun appeared lower in the sky, but it remained above the horizon longer, and this surprised many of our people who could not understand that in December near the South Pole the sun does not set at all but travels around the horizon. Hours of light were added to each end of the day, and the nights never were truly dark like those at home. "No man," said Cook at last, "can sail farther south than

LOST EDEN 

75 this. I think I have dared as much as any sovereign could expect of me, and I would willingly risk more, but it is the part of prudence to turn north again.' 

And so we headed north, but scurvy had laid a heavy hand upon us and many were unable to get about. There was some consolation in our heading for warmer climes, but we all knew that there was much bad weather to be borne before ever we could see those famous, sunny islands.

Our physical condition began to affect our minds, in spite of all we could do to maintain our sane disposition, and often the men fell to quarreling and fighting. The long-continued wet and cold, and the lack of proper food, sometimes turned a good hand into a vicious one, and this state of mind, which is called "the sailors' vapors," came near to being my undoing. 1

Chapter 12 

AS LUCK WOULD HAVE IT, ONE TO BE STRUCK BY THE VAPORS WAS 

Lord Wadleigh, the only man aboard, I think, who disliked me.

It was on a cold, dark night when we were hauling on the mainsheet that I accidentally trod upon Wadleigh's toe. Now I can scarce blame him for what he did, because our toes felt the cold more than other parts, and some had frozen partly, as his had, and become gangrenous. "Damn your eyes!" he shouted, and swung his fist at me.

Instinctively I swung my right fist at him and struck him full in his left eye and closed it. 

He raged like a wild bull. "Blind me, will you! Stinking pig!" With that he drew his sailor's knife from his belt and came at me. 

That might well have been the end of me, except that all of the hands kept a sharp lookout for just such bickerings and immediately would seize the opponents so that neither would be injured. Four men seized Lord Wadleigh before he could make a thrust, and I was safe, but in doing this they let go the mainsheet and it ran through the blocks with a scream and set the mainsail to flapping and cracking in the icy wind.

Instantly Lieutenant Bligh was upon us. "What's this?" he barked. “A knife-drawing? You know the penalty. Carpenter, fetch a mallet. Mason, Gilworthy, pin his hand to the mast." 

And before my eyes they raised Wadleigh's hand high upon the mast and drove his own knife into the fleshy part between the thumb and forefinger. The knife went into the wood about three inches. 76

LOST EDEN 

77 "Farther!" commanded Bligh. "We'll have an end to this kind of conduct."

And so the carpenter drove the knife almost to the hilt. "Now, Wadleigh," said Bligh, "you may draw it again if you can."

In this punishment, which I had thought never to see, the man must draw his knife from the mast with his free hand. 

Wadleigh seized the handle, and his huge arm muscles strained, but the knife did not come out. It had been driven in so far that 

I think no man could have drawn it. 

Profanity burst from his lips, but Bligh turned away and calmly ordered, "Set the mains'l."

It may be that such punishment is necessary to fend against death by violence at sea, and it may be that many of our people's lives were saved by the example of this punishment, but it was a piteous thing to see, the more so to me because I had brought it about. 

All the remainder of that night Wadleigh stood swaying at the mainmast in the bitter cold, with his hand hopelessly pinned above his head and dripping blood upon his face. It was forbidden that any should assist in freeing him or giving him food or drink, but when the false dawn first spread a faint light in the frosty sky I still had not slept and I spoke to Midshipman Thorson, who also lay awake in his hammock next to mine. "I am going to take Wadleigh a stiff go of rum," I said, "while there is still some dark to cover me."

I had thought to hear a strong remonstrance, but all he said was, "Luck to you," for he had been thinking of the same thing and I liked him the better for it. 

And so I crept to the deck carrying a flagon brimming with rum, enough to ease the worst of pain, I thought, and warm the freezing man. I kept to the shadows and fetched the mainmast, but could not see Wadleigh there. In the dim light I saw the knife still in the wood and a dark stain of blood below it. 

Then I heard a groan and, looking in the direction from which it came, I saw him prone upon the icy deck. I crept to him and turned him upon his back, but he gave me no recognition.

LOST EDEN 

A stupor of cold and pain had come over him, and I think the poor fellow was half frozen to death.

I raised his head and poured some of the spirits into his mouth. "Ahhh," he moaned, and seized the flagon. I saw that the knife had torn through the flesh of his hand as he had fallen fainting upon the deck, and had so released him. "Thank you, mate," he whispered, for as yet he had not recognized me. "God bless you. A right good draught this is." "I am sorry," I said, for truly I was much disturbed by this punishment which I could not wish for any man. "Ah, you, Westermark." He grimaced as he recognized me. "I'd not take a sip of your goddam rum but that I'm half dead.” "The cursed cold made us ugly," I offered. "Many times these days I've been that cold that I would have welcomed death. 

A man takes leave of his reason in times like these." "Aye, the vapors are upon the lot of us. God help us. But a fine revenge you got this day! You made a fool of me, and I'll remind you, sir, that this cruise is not yet finished."

But this time I held my peace when he did not. I let him finish the rum and then pulled him to his feet. "I'll take you to Dr. Anderson and he'll wash your wound,"

I said, supporting almost his full weight, for he scarce could move. 

We found Dr. Anderson huddled in his bed beneath a pile of damp blankets, with a trickle of cold water coming down upon him from the deck above. "It is a bad wound, but not too bad. It will heal,” he said, and he washed it with spirits and bound it neatly. "I'll have a scar the likes of Cook's," said Wadleigh. He grinned awkwardly at the doctor, and I knew the rum had taken hold. "Is that how the captain got the mark upon his hand?” I asked, for it was in the same place as this ugly cut. "None can say for sure," the doctor answered, "but he has lived rough before the mast, and you can make your own guess as to that." 

And one bitter-cold night soon after this, a more tragic event took place, one which augured ill for our cruise. I found

LOST EDEN 

79 John Oglethorpe leaning over the rail gazing at the chunks of ice floating past the ship. "Cheer up, Jack," I said. "We soon will find better climes."

He looked at me mutely for a moment and I noticed a wildness in his eyes, like the fear and desperation of a hunted animal at bay. 46 "Tis no go," he said. “I am done for." "Have you seen the doctor?" "Tis not sickness. I wish it were no more than that. A man can live through sickness, with luck, but not this. Westermark, I am a thief." "I don't believe it." “Aye. The devil took me on watch just now and I have looked into his face these two hours since. But I was that cold as a man might think on death every minute of his watch. The door of the rigging locker was open, and what did I see? I see a nice warm skin of seal. Me that's never stole before, I see the warm fur and I pick it up. God, I says to me shaking bones, I must stuff this fur inside me jacket or I will die. And so I took the fur to warm me." "I understand." "But Lord Wadleigh did not. That he did not. A thief, he called me. A blooming thief. He says 'tis for rigging we keep these skins, and a man that will take the rigging from his ship and shipmates is the lowest kind of thief. And in the morning he will report me as a thief. And that I am. Before God and me mother I am a thief.” "What will it be for you, a flogging?" "I wish that were all, mate. Indeed I do." He turned his face again to the water. "What else, Jack?" "Me honor, lad. Me, a marine . . . I have disgraced the uniform." "I'll say a word for you, and I know your record will stand you in good stead.” ""Tis too late. I have stolen." He shook his head and gazed at the water. A scurf about his neck showed that he suffered severely from scurvy. I took his hard, cold hand. It was chapped

LOST EDEN 

and cracked about the knuckles, and I saw that the cracks recently had bled. "Get into your hammock and I will bring you some rum to warm you and put you to sleep," I offered, for some of this precious commodity still remained of the store which Mr. Banks had given me. "You'll make amends, I dare say."

But he shook his head again, and I did not comprehend the full bitterness of his despair until some moments later when I went forward with a noggin to lift his spirits. As I passed along the gun deck a chill wind caught me, and I saw that someone was opening a gun port. "Belay there!" I called, wondering what kind of folly this might be. ""Tis too late!" Jack's voice came to me, and I saw in the dim light of a guttering lamp that he was dressed only in his underclothing. "God be with you and all the others!” he called. 

Then he turned and plunged head foremost out the port.

With the weight of that theft of sealskin on his conscience, he had stripped off the uniform he had dishonored and in scant clothing he leaped into the teeth of that bitter, Antarctic wind, down into that dark, cold, icy water, to meet his Maker. 

I gave the alarm, but by the time we could wear ship and slowly come around, he had disappeared utterly in that freezing waste of sea.

In his hammock lay his uniform of marines, neatly folded. 

On it we found a scrawled note. "God and Mother forgive me," it read. 

That was an Englishman's regard of theft, and so our future difficulties can be the better understood when it is pointed out that we were sailing into the greatest nest of thieves on the face of the earth.

Chapter 13 NEAR THE ANTARCTIC CIRCLE WE LOST SIGHT OF THE Discovery, and for a fortnight we sought her by crossing and recrossing the course on which she steered, but without success. Such ill luck was to be expected, because often the fog lay so thick that a ship scarce could be seen two cable lengths away, but this mischance was an added disheartenment to all. 

And now, when we most needed the aid of the Discovery, we were to be landlocked long weeks in the treacherous waters inside the Great Barrier Reef of Australia and at last to crash disastrously upon the rocks. 

As we sailed northward into this unknown trap for mariners, we tried to content ourselves with the hope that we would make rendezvous with our sister ship some weeks ahead in New Zealand, this measure having been agreed upon in case of separation. New 

Zealand was indeed a likely place for meeting because Captain Cook had charted it accurately and had been the first to circumnavigate both of the New Zealand islands, thereby sailing through the water separating them, which is called Cook Strait. 

However, those were lonely days without sight of that friendly sail, for the whole purpose of dispatching two ships on this mission was that if aught befell one, the other might effect a rescue.

As we made northing, the weather moderated and grew warmer day by day, and in the vicinity of 42 degrees south we saw land, a most welcome sight for all. It was called Van Diemen's 

Land upon our charts, but it was also known as Tasmania in honor of the great navigator Tasman. 81

LOST EDEN 

How good that land felt beneath my feet, and the clear, cool drinking water, how invigorating. "Someday I will build a house beside a waterfall," I said to Ed Flint, "and never, never move away." "Aye, and for me it would be right here if a likely maid could be had," he replied. "Give a hand with the animals," said Bligh. "Look sharp now. They are 'most dead." 

And so Flint and I helped to bring our livestock ashore, and a piteous sight they made. They fared worse than we. Our limbs were rheumatic and reddened with scurvy, but we still could walk. 

They could not, for the most part, make much effort on their own behalf. "Begad!" Flint exclaimed. "They won't even eat the grass, and this one a billy goat that got his reputation eating me breeches, buttons and all." "Let's have a look," said Dr. Anderson, for his interest in science extended to every living thing in a most humane manner. 

He looked inside the goat's mouth. "Ah, poor fellow, your teeth are so loosened they are about to come out. We will chop your grass for you this day."

And so we did chop grass, and we fed the animals with spoons as though they had been babies, and in a few days they came around and frisked upon the virgin pastures of the place. 

It was here that I saw my first nude savages, and a strange sight it was. Often had I read of such things, and it is a view to capture the imagination of a sailor, especially a young one. 

About a score of them came to us, men and women, as naked as they were born. I am sure they wondered why we had coverings upon us, in the way of clothes, for no rain was falling nor any snow. And it makes any man wonder why he covers himself on the hottest day when Nature would do better by him if he let only his skin suffice. Aye, they may be wiser than we. 

The women came to us unafraid. Nor did they mind if their breasts had fallen well nigh to their waists, or if their paunches were big enough to hang out in what we might call an undignified manner.

It may be that, in their primitive wisdom, they felt that we

LOST EDEN 

83 knew what the organs of the body were designed for and took no more thought of it. And, upon my word, that was the reaction of almost all of us. Here and there one of our people would titter and point, but for the most part we felt no different than if we had come upon some children in the bath. For such they were, children in a state of nature, taking from the earth what the good 

Lord had placed there for them.

And the strangest thing was that the rules of civilization seemed to apply here more than in some places I have known in England. Plain it was that here stood a father and mother with their brood, and there a young man with his sweetheart, and the whole spirit of the group was the same as any handful of our folk at Brighton.

These people came to us in the friendliest manner, which was what our captain always desired, because by making friends we could procure what water and food the country offered and also we could learn much, but here we had our first example of Omai's growing arrogance and feeling of superiority over other savages. "Stop that!" he ordered when the savages began to roll some of our empty casks about and laugh like children at their new-found sport. “Belay there! Go away!”

But, not understanding, they continued their fun, whereupon Omai fired his piece over their heads. They sent up a pitiful cry at the report and struck themselves on their heads with the palms of both hands and ran away into the wilderness. "Omai!” shouted Cook, coming up to us. "You will return to the ship, and if you ever do that again I will have you punished." "They spoil the casks," replied Omai. "I must teach these vile savages." ?? "Go!" It was apparent that Cook contained his wrath with great difficulty upon this occasion. "It will be a happy day when 

I set you ashore for the last time." "Ver' good," said Omai, with the pout of a small child, and he went and stood by the boat in great dejection. "Come," said Cook, and several of us went into the wilderness and eventually came upon a party of more than twoscore of these people. Some of the women had animal skins upon their

LOST EDEN 

backs, tied with thongs about their waist and shoulders, but otherwise quite naked, and it seemed a strange costume indeed until we saw that it was a sling for carrying a child. They were so ignorant that they took no interest in our presents of mirrors and beads, and they threw away some bread we gave them without so much as tasting it, although we ate some to show its purpose. “This,” said Cook, "must be humanity at its lowest ebb.

Foster, fetch a pair of pigs and we will leave them here with the blessing of King George and hope to improve the lot of these poor people as best we can."

But when the pigs were brought, the natives seized them by the ears and made out to kill them on the spot. "It is no good," said Cook rather sadly. "We can't hope to stock this place with swine. We must leave these people just as 

Nature made them."

He did give them some hatchets and small tools, but I think they were soon lost or thrown away. In more than a hundred years since Tasman discovered this land, the white men had made no improvements, nor could we now, and I thought, "Westermark, you are a lucky fellow to have been born in England instead of 

Van Diemen's Land."

And so we left them and headed to the north again, much pleased by sailing into warmer climes. At each going-about we studied our new course with misgivings, thinking that we might again reach for the ice fields, but we continued to make northing and were considerably cheered. 

Lieutenant Hicks spied out a bit of land one morning, and merry cheers ran along the deck. "This will be easy to name," said Cook, coming from his cabin. "Henceforth, on all the charts of the world, this will appear as Point Hicks." "Thank you, sir," said the lieutenant. "Rather I should thank you," said the captain, "because you have supplied me with a name. If new lands do not stop bobbing up in front of us, I must need start naming them after my enemies instead of my friends."

Although we now were upon the coast of a vast continent called by some Greater Java and by others Australia or New Hol-

LOST EDEN 

85 land, the explorers who previously had touched upon it had placed their findings so inaccurately upon their charts that little could be learned from them. Cook made the first accurate survey, with the aid of astronomy and his most useful Hanley watch. 

We sailed from Tasmania most of the way up the eastern coast of this vast land, not knowing that we were entering most dangerous waters. We were to be cut off from the open sea by a seemingly endless barrier reef, and were to sail above one thousand miles in three months with always a man in the chains heaving the lead to sound the shoals night and day.

At Botany Bay, named for its luxury of green growth and the many new plants it afforded us, Cook took possession of the land for King George and named it New South Wales, not knowing the great extent of it. He displayed the colors there, and upon a large tree one of the joiners carved the name of our ship and the date. Always our captain felt that these new lands would be of little use to our country, and indeed he hoped that they might always remain unspoiled by white men.

Proceeding northward again along this uncharted coast, we came up with the barrier reef in the vicinity of a cape which our captain named Townshend. A reef was nothing new to us, you may be sure, but this one, seeming harmless and far from the land at first, closed in on us day by day until it caught us and wedged us against the land.

Here we met with so many uncharted bays and promontories that Cook was most hard put to it for names to bestow, and now and then he placed a whimsical one upon his chart. There was 

Thirsty Sound, so named because it denied us fresh water, Point Hillock, which presented nothing of distinction but a small hill, and Magnetical Isle, so called because of a deviation in our compass as we passed, and Botany Bay, where many new plants were collected.

Cook ran down the list of his friends in England, and Lord Sandwich was remembered here as in many other places of the world, with his name placed upon a cape.

And Cape Upstart got its name because of its abrupt rise from the sea, and so it went until we first gave a name expressive

LOST EDEN 

of distress, and that was Cape Tribulation, which lies approximately in latitude 16° S. and longitude 215° W.

On a Sunday evening, just before six o'clock, Captain Cook was at his ease in his cabin. Suddenly he got to his feet and made for the quarter-deck. It was said that he could sense shoals in his sleep, and it may well be true, for almost always he was the first to predict a landfall. "Heave the lead!" Cook shouted, and Flint made a cast but could not reach bottom.

All appeared to be well in the gathering darkness, but soon Cook saw some small, rocky islands with the aid of his glass. He shortened sail and hauled off close upon the wind, and he kept standing off until near nine, with a fine breeze and a bright moon. 

This most important task of heaving the lead is wearisome and so monotonous that in a short time a man may lose his interest in the measurements and so be of little value. Accordingly, Captain 

Cook changed the men at this post frequently, and I had to take my turn. “Westermark,” he said, "you must be the most important man on the ship this night. You must heave the lead, and one wrong reading of the line may set us upon a rock. Will you assume this responsibility?” “Aye, sir, if I am properly instructed." "Then ask your good friend Flint to show you how. He is the most capable of the lot, and it is time to relieve him." 

And so I went into the chains where Flint stood on the windward side of the bow. With the line coiled neatly at his feet, he would hold it at a point a few yards from the leaden weight at the end, swing the weight forward, and back along the side of the ship a time or two, then make a circle about his head with the weight and throw it out in front of the ship and let the line run free.

Almost always the lead would dangle free in the water, but when he felt it strike bottom he would call out the depth of water, as measured by markings on the line. "Now this first mark, with the three strips of leather, is three fathoms," said Flint. "And a fathom is six feet."

LOST EDEN 

87 “Aye. In the olden days it was as far as a tall man could reach, but we are more accurate now and we make it six feet exact. 

And when ye get into three fathoms, that is eighteen feet and very dangerous water. Ye sing out, 'By the mark three!' and ye'd best let go with a damn good roar, me lad.”

I took careful notice of the three strips of leather. Farther up the line I saw a white rag tied. "And what measure is this?” I asked. "The white rag is five fathoms and the red rag is seven." "Is there a mark for four and six?" "No. That ye must guess, and when 'tis four fathoms, ye sing out, 'By the deep four,' and if it be four and a quarter, the shout is ‘and a quarter four,' but if it be four and three quarters, ye call, 'quarter less five.' And so it goes. Now try a cast."

I swung the lead and heaved it as far as I could. "No. Not that way. "Tis too far. "Tis not a game of strength but of science. Ye must gauge yer throw so that when the lead sinks to bottom it will be fair beneath yer feet and the line will be upright and taut. Only that way do ye know the proper depth.” "You had best stay here while I learn more,” I said, and he laughed. “That I will, mate. And I don't need to be told to do't by the captain. 'Tis the eyes, ears, and nose of the ship that ye be tonight."

And so I was, because darkness gathers quickly within the tropics, and to further obscure our way a heavy haze came upon us and blotted out all help from the light of the moon and the stars as well. "By the mark twenty-one," I would call, or, "By the deep twenty."

The work was so monotonous, and my throat grew so tired of shouting, that when I called, “By the mark twenty-three," my voice hoarsened and was not clear.

Instantly Lieutenant Bligh was upon me. "Did you say three?" he screamed. "Twenty-three, sir." "Perhaps you don't know the difference, you blithering young fool! Take care, now, and let me hear each cast."

LOST EDEN 

We were in twenty-one fathoms, when suddenly we got into twelve. "Give me the lead!" Flint shouted, and his great, capable hands heaved and pulled with great expertness and rapidity. "By the deep ten!" he shouted in his thunderous voice, and then, "By the deep eight." "All hands ahoy!" Bligh called out, and when the men came pounding onto the deck, he gave the order, "To your stations!"

We were on the point of anchoring when, on a sudden, we had deep water again. And so we thought all danger was at an end, and that we had sailed over the tail of some shoals which had showed themselves before darkness. 1 In less than an hour, however, the water shallowed at once from twenty to seventeen fathoms. "Look alive, there in the chains!" shouted Bligh. "Let me take it," said Flint, and he gathered up the line, but before he could make another cast the ship struck heavily upon a rock.

There was a grating and rumbling from below. The ship shuddered and stopped dead, immovable. rocks!" "Damme!" shouted Flint. "It has come! We are on the

A chill ran up and down my spine, which lessened only slightly when I heard Captain Cook's voice, loud but calm and even as always in an emergency. "Get the sails off her, Mr. Bligh," he ordered, "and swing out the boats. What do you make of it, Mr. Peckersgill?" "It might be a coral ledge, sir," that officer replied. “If so, the coral will grind away the hull with any sea at all." "Have a boat carry an anchor astern and we will try to pull her off." "We are taking water in the hold, sir," reported Peckersgill presently, returning from below.

The ship had been carried over a ledge of the rock, it was determined, and she lay in a hollow within it. Finding the water deepest astern, they carried out the anchor from the starboard quarter and dropped it, fastening the hawser to the after capstan, at which the people applied their whole force, but in vain.

LOST EDEN 

89 She beat so violently against the rock that the men could scarce keep their legs. They spent their strength and had reason to regret it later. But it must be remembered that habitable land appeared nowhere in sight, there being only two small rocky places visible to Cook, and the men did not know of these at the time. 

Few of the men could swim. I could make a fair showing in the water, but for only a short time. When this is considered, the thoughts of the men may be guessed when, in the bright light of the moon, they saw sheathing boards of the ship's bottom float to the surface one by one, till at length the false keel followed, so that they expected instant destruction.

Only a few could go in the boats. The rest would be at the mercy of the sea. "We must lighten her," said Cook.

First we threw overboard six guns, but that seemed to do little good, and so next went many of our precious stores, along with oil jars, casks, and all manner of gear. It was necessary to throw over many useful light articles in order to get at the heavier stores below.

In this dismal business we were employed until daybreak, but few words were spoken and not an oath was sworn, so much impressed were the sailors with a sense of their imminent danger. 

Cook raised his glass frequently to his eye at the first hint of light in the eastern sky, and soon he saw land some eight leagues distant, but not a single island nearer on which part of the crew might land while the boats went on shore with the rest; so that the destruction of the greater part of the people would be inevitable if the ship were to go to pieces entirely as already had occurred in part.

The wind died away to a dead calm, and as high water was expected about eleven o'clock, everything was prepared to make another effort to free the ship. But the tide fell so short that she did not float by eighteen inches, although we had thrown overboard nearly fifty tons of precious freight. "Eighteen inches between life and death," said Flint as he passed me on deck. A rough man, he was now pale and weak, and dark circles showed under his eyes. Few, I think, had strained at the capstan more than he.

LOST EDEN 

"Can you swim?" I asked him. "Never a stroke. A lump of lead I am in the water." "If it falls out that we must get into the water, stay by me," 

I said. "Keep on your back and I may tow you to a bit of wreckage." "You are a good lad, and thanks, but 'twould be a waste of time and only spoil your own chances. I've said me prayers and spent me strength, and no more can a sailor man do." 

He sat down wearily on the deck, his knees seeming to give way under him. "Just the same, I'll have an eye on you, Flint," I insisted.

This he did not hear. So tired was he that already his eyes were closed and he was fast asleep.

As the tide fell, the ship bore more heavily on the rock and the grinding noise increased. Sea water poured in so rapidly that we scarce could keep her free by the constant working of two pumps. Soon she began to list.

Our only hope now depended upon the midnight high tide, and preparations were made accordingly to exert every effort to get the ship off. Some took fitful naps. Others stared at the pieces of the ship's bottom which floated on the sea, and men rested only when they were about to fall from exhaustion. 

The tide began to rise at five o'clock, but the leaks likewise increased to such a degree that two more pumps were manned, but only one of them would work. Therefore three pumps were kept going constantly until nine o'clock, at which time the ship righted herself. But so much water had been admitted by the leak that they expected she would sink as soon as the tide bore her off the rock. "We're done for, lad," said Flint, pale and weary. "Have ye primed yer pistols?" "I had not thought of them," I said, surprised. My energy was not so great as some, but I had given it all, and at first I was too tired to get his meaning. "They say it'll be a fight for the boats. When Cook gives the word, it is every man for himself, and no man knows his friend. 

Will ye think of Flint when ye hear the command to abandon ship?"

LOST EDEN 

91 "That I will.” "It may be our last fight, lad, and I thought I'd best tip ye off. There's a heap of evil stalks the deck this night."

I began then to notice the faces of the men, and I better understood what Flint had said. In addition to their show of exhaustion, some wore the look of the hunted and some of the hunter, and fear was everywhere.

Yet it was also considered that those who were left on board would eventually meet with a milder fate than those who, by gaining the shore, would have no chance but to linger out the remainder of life among the rudest savages in the world, and in a barren country whose scant food would afford them a wretched existence at best. 

At ten o'clock the tide ran high. "To your stations!" called Cook, and the despairing men once more manned the capstan.

They would heave for a few moments and then fall upon the deck to rest. For twenty minutes this continued, and then the ship moved. "Heave ho! Heave ho!" they sang in rhythm, and at the third heave she came free and floated off the rock.

Such celebration as the people had strength to make was checked quickly when word spread that there were three feet nine inches of water in the hold, and the bilge seeming to deepen despite all efforts. "Pump! Pump for your lives!" the officers ordered.

This the men did, by now so much exhausted that they could stay at that difficult task no more than a few minutes at a time and then, quite spent, they would throw themselves upon the deck.

At the next sounding of the bilge, it was reported that the leak had gained instead of lessened, the water having risen in the hold more than a foot. A foot! That was dismal news indeed. "Now!" said Flint. "Stand with me near the pinnace. When the order is given to abandon ship, it will be every man for himself, and only the timid and the dead will stay aboard. 'Tis them or us, matey, and we're damn well in for it now."

LOST EDEN 

I stepped to my room and buckled on my hanger, for Vancouver had told me to rely upon my sword more than upon pistol or knife in such a fray.

And as we were thus engaged in altercation, a half-crazed sailor named Cartright dashed up screaming, “Abandon! Abandon!"

Lord Wadleigh also wore a sword, and now he made the same quick maneuver of drawing it that I had encountered in Mr. Banks's garden so long ago. Then here, at last, our long bitter feud had brought us to a fight unto death with cold steel. 

I did not draw so quickly as this ugly swordsman of long standing, but I was quick enough to parry his first thrust, which must have plunged his blade to its jeweled hilt had it found its mark. 

Then we surged back and forth, our swords clashing, and once I think he would have split my chine like a butcher had he not slipped upon a stave which had been left on the deck in the haste of our jettison operations.

And now it was my turn to trip upon some other odd piece of gear, and down I went. As I fell, he knocked my sword from my hand.

Quickly he placed his point at my throat, the same gesture that I had made upon him with that same jeweled weapon in a garden of Soho Square. The scar I had made on his face accentuated the hideous aspect of his furious countenance. "Remember the garden?" he cried, gasping for breath. "And the chambermaid? Now, by God--"

AT THAT MOMENT A CHEER WENT UP. ALL SEEMED TO RECOGNIZE IT 

for good news.

Slowly Lord Wadleigh replaced his sword in its scabbard and with much show of disgust and disappointment turned away. 

And fortunate it was for him, because Cook had not given an order to abandon. That order came only from the half-crazed sailor, Cartright.

In his hotheaded haste for revenge, Lord Wadleigh had been the only one to draw a blade against a shipmate. Only by the matter of seconds had he been saved from murder and a rope halter at the yardarm.

An officer had gone below to see what sudden change had taken place that the water in the hold rose suddenly to five feet, and had returned with an explanation. 

Between the inside lining of the ship's bottom and the outside planking there was a space of about seventeen or eighteen inches, and the man who had taken the depth of the water hitherto had plumbed no farther than the ceiling or inside lining, but he had been relieved by another who took the depth at the outside planks, and it appeared by this mistake that the leak had suddenly gained upon the pumps the whole difference between the two sets of planking.

When this mistake was explained to the men, they rallied again and set to pumping once more, and they worked all through that dismal night, each giving of his last ounce of energy, but it was not until eight o'clock the next morning that they showed an appreciable gain on the water which was coming into the hold. 

Nor was this an altogether cheering sign, because a heavy 93

LOST EDEN 

sea might yet break the ship apart, and so the officers made plans to run her into some suitable harbor and build another vessel from her materials, a slow job at best, requiring many months. First, of course, it was necessary to find such a harbor and to pump incessantly the while. “An idea has come to me in the night," said Flint, “and I am minded to speak to the captain. I've not got the brains of 

Cook, but 'tis a heap more sailing I've done." 

And so he made bold to approach our captain, whose eyes were heavy from lack of sleep. "Beggin' yer pardon, sir,” Flint began, touching a weary hand to his forehead, “I've a plan to save the ship, but 'tis only a seaman I am and I hope ye'll take it kindly.” "Speak out," said Cook, to Flint's surprise, for seldom did our commander seek advice of any man. "Have ye thought of fotherin' the ship?" "No. A strange performance, that. What do you know of it?" "I've seen it done, sir." "And did it help?" "Aye. That it did, sir, and I can show ye how to do't." "Good." His decision was made immediately.

Fothering is the crudest of repairs and is seldom undertaken, but it offered some hope for the people and they undertook it with a will.

They fetched an old studding sail and spread it upon the deck. Then they brought some available oakum and wool, chopped small, and this they stitched down in handfuls on the sail as lightly as possible. Over this was spread the dung of our animals and all other manner of filth and waste which could be scraped together. 

Thus prepared, the sail was hauled under the ship by ropes, which kept it extended until it came under the leaks, when suction carried in the various materials from the surface of the canvas. 

Lieutenant Bligh scowled through the whole performance. "So we have a barnyard sailor aboard," he said. "Aye!" cried Flint, coming from the hold with a broad smile. "And the pumps are gaining!"

This was true. The hands began to shout the news, and some pounded old Flint upon the back.

LOST EDEN 

95 The experiment succeeded so well that, instead of three pumps, the water easily was kept under with one. "You have saved Cook's life," I said to Flint, "to say nothing of your own skin and mine.” "Don't speak too soon," he answered. “There is something you should see. Come with me to the hold."

He led me through a narrow passage between casks of salted beef and boxes of bread, all of which later proved to be spoiled by the deep bilge, and in the light of a candle he paused and pointed. us in." “Look,” he said. “We have picked up the rock that stove

In the wash of about a foot of water I could see a piece of coral which appeared to be about a foot in diameter. Its sharp point had pushed through the planking, and it had broken away and remained wedged in the splintered bottom of the ship. "If it falls away," said Flint, "we are scuttled. We may sink before we can get a boat away."

In this condition we proceeded slowly to the land we had seen, but it had no harbor and seemed not able to support life. 

Accordingly we sailed on, for it was necessary to find a good haven where we could careen the ship. With luck we might repair the bottom, but it was necessary to make plans for taking the ship apart and building her over again.

A pathetic handful we were, with the livid spots of scurvy spreading upon our legs, and with sailcloth and dung holding us up from the bottom of the sea. And all the time there was that great hole in the hull stopped with a rock which might fall away at any moment and leave us to founder.

Cook gave the name of Hope Islands to the two rocks we had gazed at so longingly and hoped to reach when we were aground. "The Hope Islands are hopeless," said Flint, "and so are we."

But in the afternoon we fell in with the land again and saw a harbor. The captain took soundings in a boat and found the channel to the harbor very narrow, but made his decision to stop here for repairs which we so sorely needed. 

It blew very fresh, full upon that narrow channel, so that,

LOST EDEN 

with high seas running, we dared not attempt the entry, but remained outside, standing off and on for four days and nights, manning the pumps, in sight of our haven and some measure of safety, but ever on the verge of disaster.

The strain upon the people became so great that on the fifth day, with the wind as fresh as ever and the seas turbulent, Cook decided to attempt an entrance. This we thought to be as great a challenge to seamanship as a crew might ever encounter, but in we went with all hands tense at their stations. 

We had not got far into the channel when we struck. The ship shuddered, and men stared blankly at one another. Slowly she moved off and floated free, but in a moment we struck again and this time we were fast aground. "She's done for now," said Mason. "That she is not!" shouted Bligh, who overheard. “We'll sail her in without a bottom if need be, and you'll bear a hand. 

To the foreyard with you."

We took down the booms and the foreyard and the foretopmast and made of them a raft which we lashed to the side of the ship, and when the tide came in, this raft gave the ship enough buoyancy to float. We soon got her into the harbor and moored her close to the beach, taking out anchors and cables to make her fast. "Look!” cried Flint as he jumped onto the shore. "Land! "Tis land beneath me feet!"

Nor was he the only one who felt that thrill which solid land can give to weary sailors. Some threw themselves upon the sand to feel its solid warmth. Others knelt and prayed and many stood staring at this bleak, forbidding land, for it had almost nothing to offer a shipwrecked adventurer.

A boat was dispatched to catch fish for those who had scurvy, but it returned without having procured any. "It wouldn't be worth fightin' for," said Flint. "If yer ship sinks in these waters, ye might as well drown as try to live on this land." “There are many who are sorry they left England,” I said. "Aye," he replied, "and many who have no mind to go back again."

LOST EDEN 

97 It was five days later that the ship was warped high enough on the beach to examine the bottom, and as she heeled over I stood with Vancouver in a group of several score of our people, all anxious to view the damage.

It appeared that rocks had cut through four planks into the timbers and that three other planks were damaged. In these breaches not a splinter was to be seen, the whole being smooth, as if cut by an instrument. "Look at the rock!" several cried as they first caught sight of the coral bung to our barrel. “God in heaven! A blooming miracle it is. St. George preserve us!"

Each in turn touched the rock that almost wrecked us but by an odd circumstance kept us afloat, and they felt the planks which had been worn away. "Better thank Flint," said Vancouver as he intently examined the many breaches which were stopped by bits of wool and oakum and the dung of our animals, some of which were so weak that they had to be carried ashore to forage. Even the openings around the coral rock were caulked with Flint's preparation. 

And I thought it odd that one of the lowliest of our company had performed the greatest of services and had saved us when all others had failed, had indeed saved the very life of our famous commander. I was growing very fond of Flint. 

We stopped thirteen days at this little harbor near Cape

Tribulation, but in all that time we found less food than would suffice to support one man. Often we drew the seine, but to no purpose.

Omai was the only one who had been able to catch fish, and in this he reverted to his savage state, as indeed he had a tendency so to do quite often. It was partly this and partly his increasing arrogance which caused him to lose his friends aboard. He had been provided with every kind of wealth which might create envy in the people of his homeland or the natives of other lands where we touched, and he took pains to impress all such natives with his importance.

His primitive manner of fishing he displayed to impress the rest of us, who could catch nothing from the sea. He would wade into the surf, not minding the breakers, being like a porpoise in the

LOST EDEN 

water, and would lower his head underneath the surface and now and then come up with a small fish in his hands. "Not bad, do you think, old fellow?" he would say. 

Then he would bite the wriggling fish just behind its head to kill it and with great relish eat the whole of it raw. This food seemed to be what he needed, for the scurvy left him and he regained his health, whereas few of the others improved at this place. 

On the day of our departure I went inland with Flint for one last walk. I looked for plants, and he searched for one of the speedy mouse-colored animals which had outrun Omai's favorite dog. It had bounded away on its hind legs and it carried an immense tail. Later we found that the natives called it a kangaroo. "Do ye think a man could live here, John?" Flint asked me. 

I knew that he had desertion in mind. "There are no fish for fishermen such as you and I, and if you care for meat you must learn to run faster than Omai's dog," 

I said. "Aye, 'tis a scurvy place. A man might do better to die aboard the Resolution. I do hear said that they give you double grog at the end." 

That day we found neither new plants nor kangaroos, and so we sailed away, this time feeling sure that we would fetch the fabled Tahiti, but instead we made sail for New Zealand.

“D'YE THINK THE Discovery SANK AMONGST THE ICE ISLANDS?”

But with calm seas and fair winds a sailor's heart is soon lightened, especially when he finds new wonders to shape the tales he will tell at home, such as the day it rained fish upon the deck. "B'God, 'tis a fish!" cried Flint as we sat in the lee of a four-pounder and a fingerling about three inches long fell beside us and flopped its tail upon the deck. "A good jump, that, from the sea to the main deck," I said, trying to explain the phenomenon and also looking about to determine if some wag was up to a monkeyshine.

But others of the crew also found fish beside them and were looking into the sky and pointing. We looked also and saw another minnow fall from a great height and land upon the wheelhouse. 

We could hear exclamations of the people as they ran to the opposite side of the ship, and so we followed them and saw a 99

LOST EDEN 

strange cloud about a mile off the larboard beam, and from this cloud depended a long tail which curved somewhat and reached down toward the sea. "A waterspout," said Dr. Anderson, who also had come to see the excitement. "A whirlwind on shore, but a waterspout at sea. It is carrying the water up to a great height, and some of the smaller fish with it."

It seemed a logical, scientific explanation to me, but a German named Zimmerman fell to his knees and crossed himself, and others also straightway said their prayers for the first time since we ran upon the rocks.

The waterspout lasted fully a quarter of an hour. It was a column which appeared of the thickness of a mast or middling tree and reached down from a smoke-colored cloud, about two thirds of the way to the surface of the sea. Under it the water appeared to be perturbed for a considerable space, and from the whole of that space arose a dark-colored thick mist reaching to the bottom of the pipe, where it was at its greatest distance from the water.

The pipe itself was perfectly transparent and much resembled a tube of glass or a column of water, if such a thing could be supposed to be suspended in the air. It very frequently contracted and dilated, lengthened and shortened itself, and that by very quick motions. It seldom remained in a perpendicular direction, but generally inclined either one way or the other in a curve, as a light body acted upon by the wind is observed to do. 

During the whole time it lasted, smaller ones seemed to attempt to form in its neighborhood; at last one almost as thick as a rope formed close by it and became longer than the old one, which at that time was in its shortest state; upon this they joined together in an instant and, gradually contracting into the cloud, disappeared.

Another day we came upon Holothuriae, or Portuguese men-of-war, as the seamen call them, and I busied myself with making sketches and paintings of those fascinating creatures. The body consists of a bladder, on the upper side of which is fixed a kind of sail, which he erects or depresses at pleasure. The edges of this he also at pleasure gathers in, so as to make the sail concave

LOST EDEN 

101 on one side and convex on the other, varying the curve for the conveniency of catching the wind which moves him slowly upon the surface.

Under the bladder hang down two kinds of strings, one smooth, transparent, and harmless, the other full of small round knobs having much the appearance of small beads strung together; these he contracts or extends sometimes to the length of four feet. Both these and the others in this species are of a beautiful ultramarine blue, but in the more common one, which is many times larger than this, being nearly as large as a goose's egg, they are of a fine red. 

With these beaded strings he does his mischief, stinging or burning. If touched by any substance, they immediately throw out millions of exceedingly fine white threads, which pierce the human skin and adhere to it, giving very acute pain. When he thrusts them out of the little knobs or beads which are not in contact with some substance they can pierce, they appear very visibly to the naked eye like small fibers of snow-white cotton. 

The next day some objects three feet long and half as broad floated very buoyantly past the ship. They were supposed to be cuttlebones, which indeed they a good deal resembled but for their enormous size.

That was a day when many stories of sea monsters were revived and the older sailors talked about the remora, that great sucking fish which pulls men from the deck, and the great horny sea serpent called a kraken which eats ships as easily as a donkey consumes a carrot.

A few days later many were sure that a kraken was at hand when a disturbance came to the water beside us, less than a half a cable's length away, and extended in a line thrice the length of the ship. "A kraken! A kraken!" the people cried, and gunners unlimbered the guns and marines caught up their muskets, for a series of ash-gray humps protruded from the water, undulating up and down in the manner of a monstrous snake slithering through the water beside us. "Porpoises," said Bligh.

And so we laughed about the "serpent," but many still believed that someday we would meet with a real one and that it would be no laughing matter.

To add to the wonderment of these lonely sailors so far from home, we had yet another mystery, and this one remains unsolved. At seven o'clock in the morning a small white cloud suddenly appeared in an otherwise cloudless sky, and a train of fire issued downward from it. We heard a distinct explosion and then the cloud disappeared. "Now, what d'ye make of that?" Flint asked. "A volcanic action of some kind, don't you think?” I said. Chapter 16 VANCOUVER WAS FORMING THE WATERING PARTY AS THE NEW 

Zealand coast began to open up and show us Grass Cove. First and most important always was the watering party, for the stink again lay in the scuttle butts and the whole crew was athirst for that precious stuff which is on this earth in overabundance for all but those at sea.

He had a boat's crew gathered about him when his eye caught the far-off glistening of paddles and the thin lines of war canoes near shore. "Here they come now, dozens of canoes. Look to the loading of your pieces,” he said.

The natives came down the coast fast and gave me my first sight of those odd-looking craft which we were to see so often in the Pacific. They were fashioned of giant trees, hollowed out with stone and shell instruments and by the building of small fires inside them. 

I could not understand the high bows and sterns, protruding sometimes eight or ten feet almost vertically, until I saw them used in the surf, where they fended off the tumbling breakers and came through dry whereas often our own boats could neither land nor pull away from the shore. "There will be no shooting until I give the word," said

Cook, who heard Vancouver's command to load and stand ready. "Some are most anxious to even the score, sir," George replied. "Their friends died here."

"Speak up, now. Which of you would like to give the cannibals a ball in the heart?" "I, sir." "And I." “And I.” "Vancouver, you will replace these men with some of milder manners. We have nothing to gain at any time by killing a few childish savages and much to lose. There is good water here, and refreshment of fowl and fish, and green things to eat against the scurvy.
