Soon the canoes came up with us, and here we beheld one of the sights of the world of savages which we had most longed to see. Some of the canoes held as many as one hundred men, and because it is unlikely that larger canoes can be made from single trees these warriors must have felt themselves to be the most powerful in the world, and indeed that was the attitude they exhibited toward us. As they came near, their cries of defiance filled the air, as they shook their spears, some about sixteen feet in length, and brandished their stone battle-axes and the bludgeons they call patoo-patoo, one of their many double words which amused us who think one word should suffice. "Look at the comic idols," said Williams, whose extra-keen eyesight made him the first to see the strange figureheads at stem and stern of the smaller boats in particular. The figures were of men with large eyes composed of white shells, and always a tongue of enormous size thrust from the mouth, and the whole face a picture of abject deformity. "Gad, I'm scared," Gibson laughed.

LOST EDEN 

107 But our merriment increased when the men began a war dance in their canoes. They distorted their limbs and agitated their faces with strange convulsive motions; their eyelids they drew back so far that white circles appeared around the eyeballs, and their tongues they thrust out of their mouths to an amazing length, for all the world like the figureheads they had carved. "I think," said Cook, "their sculptors have no lack of models."

There was admirable vigor and activity in their dancing, and they sang well together, keeping such exact rhythm that when sixty or a hundred paddles were struck against a boat they made but a single report.

Some of these warriors were nearly black, but most were brown and not much more colored than a Spaniard. For clothes they wore matting, one piece over the shoulders and one tied around the waist, and some were ornamented with feathers. One man was almost completely covered with the plumage of the red parrot. "Here is where we do some fancy trading with our red feathers," said Vancouver, for he had some of this currency in his sea chest as most of us had.

In each canoe stood an elderly man holding a kind of staff of distinction formed of a whale's rib, about six feet long, quite white, adorned with carving and feathers and the hair of their dogs. All were daubed generously with the black stain they call amoco, and these elderly leaders displayed more of it than the others.

It was in this strange manner that our first cannibals came to war upon us, and one could not but admire their courage in attacking such a large ship as ours. When they approached within a stone's throw they let fly a good quantity of missiles, some spears, but mostly stones, some of them half as large as a man's head. 

So many of these stones came at us at once that the entire lot could not be dodged, and some of our people took hurts of a minor nature. "It is time to fire a volley over their heads," said Cook, and accordingly Sergeant Vancouver gave the order. A score of pieces belched out what must have seemed a mighty roar to these primi-

LOST EDEN 

tive souls, and their activity then was such as you might well expect of a covey of quail, scrambling and screeching and flying in all directions. "Not the best of beginnings," said Cook, "but we have taken the fight out of them for the moment. It will be for you, Sergeant, to make friends with them on shore, at least to the extent that you return with fresh water. Off with you, now." “Aye, sir,” George replied with enthusiasm.

I went with him in the cutter, and never did I see men guard their powder and their pieces against sea water so well as these. We rowed into a pleasant little stream of clear water, and hardly had our boat touched the shore when all were on their knees scooping up this precious liquid in their hands and drinking of it with great zest and murmurs of pleasure and smacking of lips.

In this attitude of joy and thanksgiving we were called to attention sharply by Sergeant Vancouver. "At the ready, men!" he cried, and we saw that some forty dark-skinned natives had broken cover and were advancing upon us, brandishing their long spears and their murderous patoo-patoos. 

We were but eight, and not better armed than Furneaux's men, all of whom were killed. Each of our pieces had two barrels, one loaded with small shot to frighten or maim, and one charged with ball to kill. At best each of us could shoot but two of these cannibals, which would account for about sixteen, and then it would be a hand-to-hand fight against large odds, and we were on the very same shore where another boatload of Englishmen had been killed and roasted.

Often in later years I have wondered how we managed to put such zest into that first landing among eaters of human flesh. "Peace!" shouted Vancouver, using the New Zealand word

Dr. Anderson had taught him. He advanced two or three paces with his arms outstretched, holding a piece of red cloth in one hand and a small hatchet in the other. 

Then he laid these articles on the grass and gestured for the brown folk to come and accept these gifts. And this gesture they understood, for they changed their attitude at once and be-

LOST EDEN 

109 came friendly, and they gathered around us and accepted gifts with delight.

They had rubbed their bodies with red ocher, either dry or mixed with oil, and many had worked this substance into their hair, which they had tied in bunches on the crowns of their heads and adorned with feathers of birds or pieces of bone or wood. 

All had anointed their hair with oil melted from the fat of fish or birds. The poorer people used that which was rancid, so that they had a very disagreeable smell; but those of superior rank used fresh oil.

When peace seemed to be assured, women began to come to us, dressed much the same as the men but with their hair flowing over their shoulders or cut short. / 

Both sexes, but the men more frequently, had marked their bodies with amoco. In general the women stained only the lips, but sometimes exhibited black patches on other parts of their bodies. The men put on additional marks from year to year, so that the bodies of those who were very old were almost covered. 

Exclusive of the amoco, they marked themselves with furrows of a hideous appearance, the edges being indented and the whole quite black. The designs on their cheeks were drawn in spiral form, both cheeks of the same pattern, while the paintings on their bodies resembled filigree work and the foliage in old chased ornaments; but no two faces or bodies were decorated exactly after the same model.

The ears of both sexes were bored, the holes stretched so as to admit a man's finger. The ornaments placed in these holes were feathers, cloth, bones, or pieces of wood. A great many of them made use of the nails given them by the English for this purpose as we became better acquainted, and one great fellow proudly carried one of our chisels in the lobe of his ear. "Now there is a toolbox," said Gibson, "that is always at hand when you need it." "Come here," said Vancouver to one who had pierced the membrane between his nostrils and thrust a bone through it to form a comic decoration. "Here is a sail to bend on your sprits'l yard." He gave the man a small piece of cheap red cloth, and in his thankfulness he joined in our laughing.

LOST EDEN 

And that was the manner in which George Vancouver landed and conquered this place, and it is not surprising that he later became a captain with an expedition of his own and met with considerable success.

We learned that the first hostile demonstration of these natives had come from their fear of us, believing that we had returned to revenge the killing of Captain Furneaux's men, but when they discovered that this was not our purpose, they gathered in large numbers and erected their huts on the shore near where Captain Cook pitched his tents for the purpose of making astronomical observations. "There is Kahoora! Kill him!" they said several times, for

Kahoora was the chief who had led the massacre of the Englishmen. He seemed to be more feared than loved by his people, and they suggested that we do away with him immediately because of his misdeeds.

However, had we adopted such a policy, we might have extirpated the whole race, for the people of each hamlet or village by turns applied to us to destroy another community. It appears that the New Zealander lives under perpetual apprehension of death by violence and is never off his guard either by day or by night. They believe that the soul of a man whose flesh is devoured by an enemy is doomed to a perpetual fire, which belief is no small vigilance.

Yet it was among these people that the unhappy Ed Flint chose to live the remainder of his life, long or short as that might be. "Meet my sweetheart, Gowannahee," he said to me one day as he came down to the beach leading a buxom young girl by the hand.

She wore two pieces of matting; one tied over her shoulders reached to her knees and the other wrapped around her waist fell almost to the ground, and I thought she must be indeed a modest maid, but I discovered later that these great wrappers were not designed so much for modesty as for protection from the weather. 

This matting was more a cloth of great strength, made from a kind of plant with leaves resembling the leaves of flags, some of which are yellow and the others a deep red. Of the fibers of these

LOST EDEN 

111 leaves the natives make lines and cordage much stronger than anything of the kind in Europe. 

A smile came to her blackened lips and wrinkled the spiral designs on her cheeks. "Is this to be your bride?" I asked of Flint. "That she is, and the pick of the lot. A little on the dark side, but a chief's daughter she is, and nothing less." 

He set to work with a small comb, and the pulling of her hair annoyed Gowannahee until she saw the mysterious way the comb operated to smooth her black tresses and give them a semblance of our own.

I had my sketching materials with me and began to make a picture of this dusky belle. With much looking into my notes for the words of the people which Dr. Anderson had collected on his previous voyage, I began to ask her about the massacre of the 

Englishmen here. "The white men started the fight with their fire sticks," she said, and soon I gathered that it had been a quarrel which might have begun anywhere.

It seems that a native brought a stone hatchet to barter, but the Englishman to whom it was offered took it and would neither return it nor give up anything in exchange for it, upon which the brown man snatched up some bread and fish as an equivalent-a most natural act—and thus the quarrel began. 

Our people shot two of the New Zealanders dead, to begin with, for this small theft of food. The remainder of the natives closed in with the English in great numbers, using spears and stones and clubs, and the murder was done. "There, you see? They are good people," said Flint, and he began to braid Gowannahee's hair and point to my pigtail for comparison, and she grew very merry and much pleased. "If somebody takes your hatchet, you take a little something from him. If they start the shooting, you fight like a man, you do. And that's that."

Thinking that Flint surely would regret his choice of this place for a home, I looked up some more words and asked this question of Gowannahee: "Did you eat of the flesh of the white man?" 

She dropped her eyes and would not answer.

LOST EDEN 

"Now what did you do?" asked Flint, who did not understand more than a word or two of her language. "You offended her, you did. Come, baby, smile again at old Flint." 

She raised her head sadly. "I can't tell," she said. "My family would kill me.” 

This I translated to Flint, adding: “A pleasant family, that.” "You don't scare me that way," said the unshakable Flint. "They eat only their enemies they kill in battle. They think it makes 'em strong. It's a religion. You would eat 'em, too, if you learned to do't in Sunday school." "Ask her," he said, "why her old man don't seem to like me so well. He's friendly and all that, but he seems to feel superior. 

I got to get along with the old man if I stay here. Him and me is a-going to run this country." 

I asked her this question. "He is not tattooed," she answered. "Then, begad, she'll tattoo me!" Flint was equal to any emergency. "Send her home for the damn sticks and paint." 

And so I left him that afternoon lying on his stomach, with

Gowannahee bending over him. In one hand she held the bone of a bird with several teeth upon it formed in a line. This she would dip into a dark ointment made from an oily nut, and she would apply it carefully to the back of his thigh and strike it with a stone so that the teeth penetrated the skin and carried the dark stain into his flesh.

She made a series of curved lines running up across his buttocks, and at every blow of the stone Flint would wince and groan and curse, and little drops of blood would ooze and flow and large welts would form. "You'll be a model husband," I said. "Stow it, ye jealous rat!" he cried. "I would rather be tattooed in that spot than kicked in it the way the Navy has always kicked me." "Then here's a long life and a merry one to you," I said, pouring out the last of my spruce beer and raising it in a toast. "Hell's sulphur pits! An' I can make a better brew when I am blind and deef and dumb."

Chapter 17 

ON THE SIXTH DAY AFTER LANDING IN NEW ZEALAND, I HEARD SHOUT

ing among our people and, looking up, beheld the Discovery sailing into the cove, a sight which warmed the hearts of all. We found much cheer that day in greeting old friends and in the thought that again we would have a friendly sail beside us upon the unknown seas.

Much merriment was made here where food again was plentiful and the native women most hospitable. But Dr. Anderson and I had never enough time for our botanizing. In the joy of walking with him through this land and exploring for plants, I soon forgot the dangers which I had met at sea.

Altogether we found about four hundred species of growing things, all of which were unknown to England, except garden nightshade, sow thistle, two or three kinds of fern, and one or two sorts of grass.

Here was a bounteous place to live, and had I not been so busy, I might well have formed an attachment as so many of our people did, and a desire to desert the ship. But when the day came for our departure, the Society Islands still held my imagination and pulled me strongly toward them, and so I took leave of my old friend Ed Flint. ""Twould be a merry day indeed for me but for the saying of good-by to you," he said. “Now come and sup with us in me new house. And if there be a bit left in the midshipmen's wine cask, I am not the one to refuse it this day." "It is my own thought, too," I said, and so about eight bells of the afternoon he led me inland to a secluded glen where the friendly native people already had built him a house some sixteen feet long, ten wide, and seven in height. The frame was of 113

LOST EDEN 

light sticks of wood, and the walls and roof of dry grass, firmly knit together. A lining of the bark of trees they fashioned expertly to fend against the cold airs of winter. "Now you play the gopher," he said as he got down upon his hands and knees and crawled through the door, which was barely high enough to admit us in this fashion. “It is their own style, and I'd no opportunity to pinch some hinges, but here is a jolly door."

He showed me a piece of plank about two feet square, adorned with a sort of carving, and this he placed in the entrance. 

There was a square hole near the door serving as both window and chimney. A fire burned in the center of a hollow square, rimmed with stones, and over this Gowannahee held the breast of a duck to roast it. She grinned and showed the even white teeth with which so many of these people are blessed. "Our first guest, my darling," said Flint, placing his arm about her.

They made a strange picture of domesticity, those two, the one a plump little maid of Nature, as likable as a wild young rabbit in a sweet-scented wood; the other a derelict of the sea, at last with a place he could call home, where he could rest his weary body in the twilight of his life.

We supped well, and we talked mostly of England, which he still loved, until the time of parting came. It was not easy to say good-by. 66 'Tis the last white hand I will ever grasp," he said, and a moisture came to his eyes. "God bless ye, Westermark. A rightenough mate ye'd make for me on this island. I hope that rotten tub will take ye safe wherever ye want to go, but if she finds no more rocks before ye fetch Tahiti, I hope ye'll hop it inland there and be rid of her." "There are good arguments for it," I answered, because in truth I had lost my taste for sailing uncharted seas. "If ever you come that way, we may meet again." "Good-by, lad." "Good-by."

Gowannahee could not understand our words but felt the emotion of the moment nonetheless, and tears flowed down her tattooed cheeks as she repeated, "Goo-by."

LOST EDEN 

115 And so I was far from cheerful when I went aboard and found the officers making merry with unwonted zest, eating their fill of fresh food in prodigious quantities and having up an extra go of wine for digestion's sake and then more of it as a farewell. 

In the midst of this feasting, when we were about to weigh anchor, one of the watch came hurriedly to Captain Cook. "Sir," he said, "Seaman Flint cannot be found aboard. It is said he plans to stop ashore."

All laughed, because his love affair was by now well known. "I envy him," said Dr. Anderson. "Were the wenches a shade lighter, I too might be gone." served. "We will miss the tide if we go to fetch him," Bligh ob- "My first thought," said Cook, "is to let him stay. But it would set a bad example. If I were to permit this kind of conduct, I might have to sail the ship home alone. We must find Flint.” 

And so he sent a boatload of marines ashore in the night.

One of them knew of Flint's new dwelling, and therein lay the weakness of his plan. But they proceeded with great caution on account of the slight ties of friendship among these brown people. 

A false move would precipitate a fight, and the end might be a cannibal feast.

Creeping carefully through the grass, they found Flint's hut. 

When they pushed open the little carved door board, they saw the old fellow sleeping peacefully beside his Gowannahee, a glow from the embers of the fire lighting his wrinkled face, his brawny arm circling his bride's brown body as they lay upon the floor mat. "Now what?" he cried, starting up and seizing the hilt of his long sailor's knife. 

But they pinioned him before he could draw it. "Sorry, old fellow," said the corporal in charge, "but 'tis

'Tis none of your doing, but me own stupidity that I have not hid meself better. Hell-fire and damnation! So this is the end of me planning. Well, mates, so be it. And if ye'll let go your hold upon old Flint and step outside,

LOST EDEN 

I'll make me farewells to this lovely lass." martial. "There is not much time, but we'll do't," said the corporal.

And so Flint came back wearily to the ship and stood court- "What have you to say for yourself?" Cook asked him. "It is only that I craved retirement and life ashore, sir. I am grown old in the service of the Navy, and 'tis rest and home life I am wanting." "I understand your motives," Cook replied, "and I find them good with the exception of the matter of desertion. We need you to help sail the King's ship back to England. However, I will overlook it this time. But if you try to run away again, you will be punished severely." "Aye, sir."

And so Cook took no disciplinary action at all. If another officer had conducted that court-martial, he would have been under suspicion of having mellowed himself with wine, but Cook never took more than one glass except of a Saturday night, when he would have a second, and always he would propose the same toast: "To the health of all good women everywhere."

We midshipmen did not fall asleep at once in our hammocks that night as the ship took the open sea and swung us back and forth, for we had slept comfortably many nights ashore and were unaccustomed to our swaying berths. We talked a long time in the dark. "If I had known there would be no punishment for being caught," said Bill Cowper, "I would have chanced a hide-out myself." "Are you sure the cat will not scratch Flint's back the morn?" asked Ned Thorson. "They say he is forgiven entirely," Bill replied, "for that 

Cook thought him sad enough a'ready. Right fond of the wench he was, more's the pity." "Cook is a strange one," Ned murmured sleepily. "A bloomin' forgivin' angel one minute and a fire-eatin' devil the next."

And so in this manner Flint, the first of our deserters, left what he had planned to be his island kingdom and sailed with us again into unknown seas.

Chapter 18 

AS WE SAILED FROM NEW ZEALAND AND SAW THE LAST OF THAT 

land, which Cook named Cape Farewell, we began to hope again that we would fetch Tahiti. That Paradise Captain Cook regarded as merely a port of call on his way toward the North Pole in search of a passage to the Atlantic, but to most of the others aboard, Tahiti was a goal, a realization of a fond dream.

Much cheered we were as we sailed north toward the tropics. And all stood ready to face whatever hardships were to come, but I think no man among us could have predicted the three days of peculiar agony which the sailors called "The Battle of the Onions."

When Cook had discovered the New Zealand islands some eight years previously, he had planted many seeds so that the natives might have such benefits of civilization as King George could bestow upon them. Among these seeds were those of onion, and they thrived and multiplied so that when we sailed we took with us a large supply of these juicy but bitter bulbs. 

They were served to us generously, raw, stewed, in soup, and cooked with meat. All of us were thoroughly tired of this stinging food when, on the twelfth day out, Cook took stock of what remained. "These onions will not keep long," he said. "We must get them into the skins of the men. Mr. Peckersgill, give me an account of them and of how much can be apportioned to each man." "I can do that now, sir," said Peckersgill. "There are about fifteen pounds remaining for each man, and I doubt if they will be wholesome after three days from now." "Very well. Give orders that each man shall eat five pounds a day," said Cook, and he added, "raw." 117

LOST EDEN 

I think no commander but Cook would have issued such an order. In his fight against scurvy he could not have found a more vicious weapon. The onions had been strong and bitter when freshly pulled from the earth, and they had grown stronger and more bitter each day. To eat but one in its raw state was enough of a chore, and the swallowing of five pounds was near impossible. 

Eyes wept onion tears and the foul onion breath of the people was laden with curses. "Pfah!" cried a seaman named Cabot, and threw most of his first pound into the sea. So full of wrath was he that he cared not who saw him, and some of the hated bulbs narrowly missed striking Captain Cook. "Cabot!" shouted the captain. "You will fetch another full five pounds from the galley and stand before me and eat them all. 

And if anyone else tries that trick, I will have him flogged." 

But all that day onions floated past the poop, beneath

Cook's very eyes, and we played a merry game of hide-and-seek, the men trying to get rid of the hateful food and the officers endeavoring to catch a man for a flogging. 

The sting and bite of this diet became almost unbearable about sundown, when the fifth pound was issued to each man, and I stood holding my share of the hated bulbs and talking to Midshipman Thorson, who shared sleeping quarters with Lord Wadleigh and me. I felt that I could not swallow one more bite. "My mouth is sore from this pig fodder," I said. "I am sore clean through," Thorson complained, “and I can eat of nothing else. Cook is a fool. I can eat no more." "Cook has given an unjust order,” I said as an idea came into my head. "Perhaps many of the onions will not keep, but the ones we have here are in quite good condition, for the most part. 

They will keep much longer than three days. It is unwise to eat them now." 

Thorson smiled with understanding. "They will do us much more good a week from now." "Aye. And midshipmen are to exercise their own judgment in emergencies." "By God!" he cried. "The emergency has arisen. Wadleigh, come here. We have an idea."

LOST EDEN 

119 Lord Wadleigh ambled up beside us and listened to young Thorson insolently but with interest. "Now," said Thorson with a sly grin, "would you think it in the best interest of our King and country to preserve this most healthful and life-giving food for a later date?” “I would, most certainly," he agreed readily enough. “And you, Westermark?" “I am for it.”

I had learned to mistrust Wadleigh at every turn, and it was only after he had hidden his own onions behind a chest in our room that I ventured to place mine there as well. 

And the next day, when more of the hated food was issued, I began to eat of it with apparent relish and walked toward our room. I noticed that Lord Wadleigh stood talking to Lieutenant

Bligh at the time, and when I was in the very act of hiding my onions Bligh came in the door. "Westermark!" he cried, peering behind the chest. “What is this? Onions! You will come with me to the captain." "It is only that I thought to extend their benefits over several days, sir." "You will come with me." 

There was naught else to do. I went with him. And as I passed Wadleigh, I saw upon his face the leer which I had come to know so well. I had no doubt that he had given me away. 

Captain Cook was pacing the quarter-deck, partly for exercise but mostly to predict the weather and make his plans for the day's sailing. His mood was one of tranquillity, and I thought of him as I had seen him at the home of Mr. Banks, calm and generous. "Sir," Bligh began, "Westermark has refused to eat his onions and has hidden them behind a chest in his room," 

Then a fire came into my captain's eyes, and a look which more than once had cowed a whole band of hostile savages. "Is this true, Westermark?" he demanded. "Aye, it is, sir," I replied. "But the onions I have are in good condition and will keep a long time. I had thought only to extend their usefulness over many days." 

Only for a moment did he drop his eyes and ponder.

LOST EDEN 

"But my order was to eat them," he insisted evenly.

Yes, it had been an order, and seldom would the most resolute officer question an order of this stern commander. "Aye, aye, sir. That I know." “A midshipman takes it upon himself to alter the orders of his captain?" well?" "Aye, but with the best intentions." "I set a penalty for this disobedience. You know that as “Aye, aye, sir." "This grieves me, Westermark, but I have no choice. Two dozen lashes."

This I could not believe. I looked at Bligh, but saw no hope of help in his stern face. "Lieutenant," Cook said to Bligh, "you will have him seized up, and pipe all hands on deck to witness punishment."

Now it is a common thing for a man to faint under less than twenty lashes and to die at sixty or thereabouts, so cruel a punishment is a flogging, and I had hoped never to see a man treated so. Certainly I had never thought that I would make such a spectacle for my mates. 

I was seized up, my wrists tied to a grating at the gangway, my back bared for the cat-o'-nine-tails which the bosun took from a red bag. A cat is a devilish invention of nine whips in one, nine leathern thongs attached to a short wooden handle. Nor does man's invention end there, for its viciousness is increased by the fixing of a small leaden ball to the end of each of the nine flails. "Lay on!" Cook commanded.

The bosun laid on a stroke which forced the air from my lungs. The little balls bit deeply. “That,” I thought, "should be punishment enough for this slight offense. Surely my captain will not permit the full two dozen to be given."

But he did. At the third stroke I could feel drops of blood trickling down my back. At the ninth, so they told me, a piece of flesh came loose from my body and dangled. At the sixteenth they could see the bare bone of one of my ribs. At the seventeenth 

I lost consciousness.

LOST EDEN 

121 They took me below for Dr. Anderson to wash my wounds with water and spirits and to replace the mangled flesh as best he could. There I rested awhile, but when I stood up I could scarce walk at all. Nor was the pain of the wounds the only hurt; something had happened to destroy temporarily the proper acting of my mind and muscles together, and this must have come about when my senses left me at the gangway, under the lash. 

Midshipman Thorson gave me a steadying arm as I went to my room, where I thought to lie face down in my hammock and perhaps die. Certainly I cared little enough about life then. But 

Lord Wadleigh was waiting there for me, with a large pipkin full of onions. "You are to eat these . . . at once," he said with an evil grin. "Captain's orders. They are the ones Bligh found behind the chest."

At that moment Bligh entered, having seen me walking along the deck. "As a special consideration," he said, "you are to have some bread with your onions."

A sailor followed him in and put before me some of the ship's bread, which now was in a sorry state from the vermin which infested it. Yet all ate of it, and I did know that this was a kind gesture from Cook and for me a necessity, else I could not have made my throat swallow so many of those hated onions.

As I sat down slowly and in great pain, Wadleigh handed me a bulb which had begun to sprout, and so bitter it was that I greedily seized some bread to lessen the sting. But no matter how careful we were in picking out the little red worms from our bread at this time, there still would be a score or more in each mouthful, and they tasted hot as mustard. 

Lord Wadleigh grinned. "Could this be almost as bad, perhaps, as having a hand pinned to the mast down by the South Pole?" He rubbed the scar which his knife had made on his hand, between his thumb and forefinger.

There was little fight left in me this day, you may be sure, and I thought less of evening the score with this vile fop than of escaping the seafaring life for good and all. 

And when at last I finished that fiend's meal and got into

LOST EDEN 

my hammock, the inside of me pained and rebelled as much as the outside.

Ed Flint came as soon as his watch was finished. "I am leaving this hellish ship again at the first chance," he whispered, "and you can lay to that. When a man can't call his stomach his own, 'tis time to mend matters the best way he can, and that I will. How now, matey, are you with me?” "Yes, Flint, I am with you." "Cannibals or no, I take me chances. And we can count on 

Webb, too. I'm off at the next landing." "I am for leaving," I said, "but I must choose the place when I go. If there be a Garden of Eden in this ocean, I would not like to settle for less." But I'd ask naught better than a mate the likes of you, lad, and it may be that you will have your way. Tahiti is not far ahead." "Aye, Tahiti!"

All of us longed for Tahiti, and our hopes increased as day by day the sun arose higher in the sky and we again approached the equator.

Omai had taught me much of the Tahitian language, and now I sought him almost daily, as did many others who urged him to tell stories of his homeland and what we might expect to find there. "Not get milk underneath hog, like England,” he explained one day to Flint and me as we sat with cups in our hands awaiting the noonday ration of water. "Climb tree and get milk from coconut. Much better."

His charming English manners had worn thinner day by day. He no longer was a gentleman in replica, petted and applauded and honored with fine presents. Slowly his courtly manners disappeared and he again became an islander, interested in fish and sea life, with small heed to his clothes or to his mode of eating. Often he could be seen wolfing his meal from a bowl with his fingers, perched in the bow or the rigging and gazing out over the sea.

LOST EDEN 

123 "You will be a wealthy man on your island," I said, because

I knew that all he asked had been given to him and would be set ashore with him. "I will be king and have many wives, and if a man I don't like, I shoot him with my poo-poo. Captain Cook will build me a house upon a house. Ver' nice."

He referred to the two-story type of dwelling he had seen in England, which would be a mansion indeed, compared to the grass huts of the other Indians. "That gives me an idea,” said Flint, and he motioned me to follow him down the deck. "I have thought meself a thought." "What now?" I asked him. "Come, 'tis time for the noon ration of water, and when it wriggles in your gullet ye can think of stopping with me in Tahiti by a cool, sweet waterfall, and then I will tell me thought."

We took our cups, which we had held ready in our hands for more than an hour, and hurried to the two casks which stood near the forecastle. We were much athirst, for we could have but half a cup and that but three times a day. "White or red?" the sentry there asked each of us in turn.

He wished to know whether we preferred our drink from the wooden or the iron cask. Always this was a difficult choice. 

The "white" water came from the wooden cask, and after about a week at sea, life began to appear in it, quite varied and unattractive. Soon thereafter a green or brown scum oozed from the spigot. 

The "red" water from the iron cask contained fewer bugs and crawling things, but always it was red with rust and tasted strongly of iron.

We watched the drawing of the water for those ahead of us in line and we peered into their cups to see which water should be preferred at the present levels of the butts. "Take the red," said Flint. "It will not slack yer thirst so much, but it holds fewer animals to bite you on the way down." 

And so we took the "red" water and, moving away from the other people, sipped it slowly to make it last the longer. "I have thought meself a damn good thought," he said again, "but maybe ye'd rather not come along with old Flint. 

Maybe ye'd prefer the company of these varmints."

LOST EDEN 

He crunched three cockroaches with but one stamp of his foot. Roaches had come into the ship during her former cruise with Cook to the Pacific and they had lain dormant in the cool climate of England and of the South Pole regions, but now, in their native airs, they began to multiply at an alarming rate. When a sail was unfurled, great showers of them would cascade upon the deck.

Their numbers were incredible, and the damage they did us was very considerable. Every method devised for their destruction proved ineffectual. "Me thought is of Omai," he continued. "Now, he is a right-enough toff when he's having a bit of fun and forgetting how wealthy he is, and he is fond of you, lad." "I hope so. And I like him well enough," I agreed, “but what is the thought you thought?” ""Tis this. Cook will build a house for Omai, an English house. Also our friend will have animals and a good store of tools and all manner of gear. Now where's a better place for you and me to stop in all the islands than with him?" “A good idea. A very good idea, but Cook will find us easily there and take us back." "I thought that thought as well. We'll sail away in a fast canoe. There are many islands, and we will go from one to another. We'll sail twice as fast as the Resolution, and we'll play hare to Cook's hound until he can stay no longer if he is to reach the Arctic in summer weather. Then we'll return to Omai. You see?" 

I swallowed the last drops of my water with considerable effort, for they contained much settling of rust, and always when I tasted the settlings I had a tendency to retch and spew and so waste my water. "It is a good plan,” I said. “It may be that we can work it out." "God bless ye, lad. I can forget me Gowannahee if ye'll but stop with me. That I can." "But we must keep our secret from Omai. He is like a child and will tell all. I think he will be glad enough to see us one day after Cook is gone. We can do much for him in the care of his

LOST EDEN 

125 animals and the growing of his gardens, and much in this way for the chiefs of his island, also."

The more I talked the more enthusiastic I became, and I began to envision a happy life in a primitive Paradise, but with much of the comfort of old England, thanks to Omai's equipment and stores.

We continued to sail northward toward the equator, and one day seemed much like another except that once a marine fell overboard and was never seen thereafter. 

At last we did fetch Tahiti, and a beautiful landfall it was, on the twelfth of August. The island is formed by two distinct mountains considerably more than a mile high, connected by a low isthmus some three miles in width. The formation is volcanic, though the valleys and hillsides are of great fertility. 

This and four other main islands form the Society group, and though they lie well within the tropics, in the vicinity of 16 degrees south latitude, the temperatures noted there in the summer ranged between 68 and 86 degrees, perhaps the most perfect temperature for the comfort of the human body, day and night, that man had yet discovered. "How now?" called Flint from a snug spot on the forecastle deck, where he sat knitting a sock of woolen yarn. His great, gnarled hands plied the needles slower than some, but nonetheless accurately, and so accustomed were they to this art in long years of practice that he gave them scarcely any attention, but kept his eyes fixed upon that green shore of entrancing beauty. "A right-enough spot, if I can judge from here," I said, and the buoyancy of great expectations filled my body and my spirit was lifted as it had not been lifted since sailing from Plymouth. 

But the airs dwindled to such a lightness that the sails fell slack, and when we came within a league of the land, the breeze failed utterly and we lay becalmed. Cook made the signal for Captain Clerke of the Discovery to come aboard, and soon he was piped over the side for his first conversation with our captain in above six weeks, having communicated with us only by the hoisting of flags or an occasional hail when in fair weather he undertook to sail near us.

He reported that the flux which had brought misery to his

LOST EDEN 

men ever since sailing from New Zealand had quite left them and that the scurvy was at a stand. "You'd not enough onions," said Cook, and his eyes swept the faces of the people on deck near him with that glint which was half appreciation of their efforts and half a threat of violent measures. 

Our "Battle of the Onions" may have resulted in a partial victory over both the flux and the scurvy, for most of the people of the Resolution could yet manage their duties and only three kept to their beds this day. 

All now were most anxious to set foot on this island of their dreaming, and the day being so pleasant and mild, our captains ordered the boats swung out and told off rowing crews to tow the ships into Oati-piha Bay, the sooner to land and refresh ourselves. 

Now, in such a happy situation, every man Jack of us would have laid his last farthing that no danger of the sea could overtake us, but we headed straight into trouble. 

We knew of the coral reef which almost completely circumscribes this island as in the case of most of the other islands in these waters, and we steered for the opening in the reef to the west, which we also knew, but we did not judge the current of the sea which was carrying us with great impetuosity toward the reef, and not a sigh of a wind to blow us off.

In this situation, insensible of the danger of that calm blue sea, we watched dozens of canoes come around the reef from the bay. With no airs to fill their sails, the Indians were obliged to paddle, but such was their strength and enthusiasm upon seeing us that they came up fast.

The men paddled in rhythm, a few strokes on one side and then on the other. Some of the canoes held more than score of people, but most of these boats were small, with but three or four aboard. All had high prows like those of New Zealand, for fending off the surf as they went through it, and all were kept upright by means of that ingenious device, the outrigger, a small log pointed at the ends and held parallel to the canoe on the larboard side, usually, by two long, curved thwarts lashed to the gunwales, if a canoe may be said to have a gunwale.

The men were sturdy fellows of a light bronze color like

LOST EDEN 

127 Omai and inclined to plumpness as he was. Their dress was somewhat variegated, but most wore loincloths of beaten bark which they called kapa or tapa, and some wore a piece of this about their shoulders to protect them from the sun, and a turban of it on their heads or perhaps one of the little hats which they manufacture in a few minutes by plaiting the green fronds of a coconut tree. 

Not a few women rode in the canoes, dressed in skirts and capes of tapa, but these mostly were of the common class and their clothing was worn less for modesty than for protection from the sun, and soon they were climbing over the side of the ship, to the delight of our people. "Tute! Tute!" was the cry everywhere, for that was their name for Cook, as they could not come nearer to the correct pronunciation in their language. Almost all of them had known him on his two previous visits. They seemed to be tremendously fond of him. “Taio! Taio!" Cook called, waving to them and using their word for friend, and he seemed as pleased as any at this welcome. 

Most of our people stood on deck with presents in their hands, and the women fared better than the men. When one received a red feather or a mirror or a string of beads, she would press the gift to her forehead and then rub noses with the donor. 

They vied with one another to kiss Cook's hand and then press it to their foreheads.

Much competition arose among our men for the favors of the prettiest of the brown girls, but enough came aboard to provide sweethearts for all who desired them, and soon ardent couples were to be seen in many parts of the ship, laughing and trying to communicate their thoughts with scarce any common language to help except the universal language of love, and that not entirely platonic. Giggles could be heard coming from most of the rooms and dark corners of the ship.

A large canoe carrying above thirty Indians came alongside, making an especially loud clamor for Tute, and when he went to the rail and smiled, a comely girl about six feet tall spread her arms wide in a gesture of welcome and climbed easily over the side to the deck. “A present from King Feenou," she said, and then two

LOST EDEN 

other tall girls came upon deck and kissed Cook's hand. 

They then began a most unusual ceremony. They loosened the end of the cloth which bound the first girl from shoulders to knees, and slowly she began to turn and unwind the garment. She had appeared unduly plump at first, but now it became evident that her ampleness was caused by the winding of some eighteen yards of the cloth, a fine grade of tapa marked with brown geometrical designs, from which she began to emerge like a lovely chrysalis from an elegant cocoon.

Slowly and gracefully she turned, a charming look upon her truly beautiful face and a most enchanting sparkle in her large brown eyes. Gradually the symmetry of her well-formed figure appeared, and with a final turn the cloth came away and she stood naked and smiling on the deck before our commander. 

Even those who were rowing the small boats that towed us lay momentarily upon their oars, watching, with no small envy, the activities upon the deck. It was while their attention, as well as ours, was thus distracted that we heard a cry from the men in the bow. "Breakers! Breakers!"

The treacherous, invisible current of that seeming calm sea had carried us sideways with such speed that we now could see foam frothing about a submerged reef scarce two cable lengths away.

The men rowing the boats altered their course to pull the ship around and head her away from the reef, and the people of the Discovery imitated them. "Let go the bower anchor!" Cook commanded, and down the great iron hook plunged into the sea.

The Discovery dropped anchor also and brought up suddenly in three fathoms, but we were in deeper water and our anchor would not reach bottom. We raced on with that treacherous tide, the men in the boats rowing for their very lives. 

The roar and hiss of the surf increased, and soon one great comber broke beneath our stern, and when it passed toward the land we struck upon the reef and began to pound upon the coral rock with every fall of the sea, here at the very portals of Eden itself.

Chapter 19 

IN THIS PERILOUS POSITION, WITH THE Resolution POUNDING HER 

hull upon a reef in sight of the enchanting goal of Tahiti, our only hope of saving the ship seemed to lie in the men who strained at the oars of the small boats to pull us off. 

It was odd in this crisis that the Indians showed small concern and not the least sign of fear. It may be that our ship was so large that the noise of coral grinding her planking held little significance for them. Or it may be because all of them could swim so well that when they fell into the water they usually laughed, though great combers set them upside down and tumbled them about, the sea holding for them no terrors at all. 

But few of us could swim. I had taken every opportunity to learn this useful art from Omai, and had made considerable progress, but our people's aversion to this sport was traditional and to me incomprehensible. They put their faith in man-made ships and boats and lived every day with the dangers of the sea not farther away than the other side of some worm-eaten planking. 

A slight breeze might have blown us off the reef, but never had I seen a calmer day. At last our anchor caught and held, but it lodged so close to the reef that it afforded no help, and we continued to pound and grind against the coral rocks. 

It was the crew of the Discovery that saved us. Although she rode perilously near the breakers, her anchor held her off, and Captain Clerke lost no time in dispatching her boats to aid us. 

With almost double the number of hands now at the oars, our ship began to move seaward. Slowly we gained upon the current, but soon our anchor brought us up sharp, for it had embedded itself firmly in the coral and would not come up. 129

LOST EDEN 

"Cut away!" Cook commanded, and a sailor parted the hawser with a hand ax, and we left our valuable and important bower anchor there below on the reef. "There is enough iron in that to buy an island,” said Dr.

Anderson ruefully, and I think that may well have been true, for often the armorer cut pieces from a spare anchor to fashion hatchets and knives which in these lands were more desirable than gold. "One less anchor to save them from the next rocks," Flint remarked, "but you and I will be far away, eh, mate?" "Let us hope so," I replied, and I fully meant it, for I think all of us were upset and shaken in those critical moments, and I not the least of all.

With such unknown and treacherous currents besetting him, Cook gave up his plan of entering Oati-piha Bay and made for the better harbor of Matavai Bay, where we arrived on the evening of the second day following. Before we could drop anchor the decks were crowded with natives, the vessel besieged by friendly swimmers, and a great crowd assembled on the shore. 

In this manner we arrived safely at this legendary land. 

When Cook touched here on his first voyage, he was the third visitor from the Western world, as far as history can determine. 

Quiros, a Spaniard, discovered this place in 1606, we are told, and called it Sagittaria. Captain Wallis, the second to touch here, called the land "George the Third's Island."

Cook, the third visitor, adopted the Indian name, which was first writ Otahiti but is now more accurately spelled Tahiti.

And a very good name this is, because in southern lands such as New Zealand the word means almost the same as the Promised Land, a beautiful Paradise beyond the horizon, as far as a man may go and the best he may ever find. Wise they were, those ancient brown men.

An old chief with gray hair came over the side and I engaged him in conversation. "This is the loveliest island in the world,” I said. "Tahiti-nui marearea," he replied, and I translated his words to mean, "Great Tahiti of the golden haze."

That does not paint the picture in words, but it conveys the meaning, and rightly, that the whole atmosphere here is different

LOST EDEN 

131 from most other places. A sunny day in England can be beautiful, but no matter how charming the vistas, they lack the glow of gold found here, which is partly a magic of the mild sun and partly a balminess of the soft airs which are scented with tropical flowers. 

None will ever know the enchanting golden haze of Tahiti lest he go there and savor of it for himself. The isle is diversified with many mountains and hills, competing with one another to form the most enchanting of green valleys. Innumerable rivulets and waterfalls feed deep streams and cool, clear pools and lakes. 

Caves like pigeonholes dot some of the precipices, and in these beautiful resting places are laid the bodies of dead chiefs, where their spirits may look upon these green valleys and out upon the blue sea. And so difficult of ascent are these cliffs that only the most skillful climbers can negotiate them, and only by secret ways, to which they are trained throughout their youth. But these risks gladly are taken, that the souls of the departed may forever feast upon the beauties of this land. 

From Mahaena to Point Venus, where Cook had before observed the transit of Venus across the sun, is a succession of sunken rocks and bars disseminated over an extensive area, and irregular submerged reefs, combed by terrific breakers. "The Admiralty was wise in sending two ships instead of one," Vancouver observed. “Else the Resolution would now be breaking up on a reef and most of us drowned." 

George Vancouver had the mind of a captain, which indeed he is become, but others entertained more personal and mundane thoughts. gave away my best feathers," said Gibson, "to that wench with the cockleshell in her ear, and here we are at a different place and I must start my love-making afresh, damme!” "Now this little lady down here in the big canoe," said 

Webb, "is for me, if she owns the canoe, and you know what I have in mind. Ho, there, my little comfit!"

Rumors of desertion ran up and down the deck and must frequently have come to the ears of the officers, who thus were placed on their guard. It appeared that the crew, to a man, preferred this port to any they might hope to reach in England. "We'd best find Omai," said Flint, “and keep him in tow.

LOST EDEN 

He is our man, and he'll set us up fit as a fiddle in these islands if we play our cards aright."

We had thought to see him in the midst of Indian friends and admirers, but he stood alone and aloof in the waist, with scarce a glance at the brown people swarming aboard. "Hello, brother," said one comely woman, giving him a slight nod and passing on, but he paid no heed to her. "Is she your sister?" I asked in surprise. "Yes, my sister," he replied curtly, "but she is a commoner. 

I am waiting for the king to come." "Omai," said Cook, coming up to him through the crowd, "do you not recognize your sister and her husband? How now, have you forgot them so soon?" "They are commoners," was his sullen reply. "And so are you!" Cook reminded him with some heat. "See here, now, this attitude will be the ruination of you. A man in your position can't act like this to his people. I can see that you will have trouble on your home island of Huaheine, and I'll not establish you there. I'll take you to Maitea instead." "Oh no, sir! Not Maitea!" "Maitea it is, and you had best mend your ways." His patience with his capricious ward had worn thin. With these few words he changed Omai's whole outlook upon life, then turned away.

He endeavored to drive some of the Indians from the ship, for they clung to the landward side in such great numbers that they caused us to heel over, dangerously near to the point of capsizing. "Now listen," said Flint to Omai, as a father talking to a son, "you are a very clever fellow, and the cleverest thing to do now is to take yer relations below and give 'em some nice red feathers. Now you take old Flint's advice and be a good fellow and make friends again with Captain Cook." "Ver' good. I show them how wealthy Omai is. I give to all commoners. If I can't be chief, I give nothing to chief of king; only to commoners."

And so this native, whose head had been so turned by what

LOST EDEN 

133 had been done for him, took his sister and his brother-in-law below to his locker and gave them of his royal largess. “Mahalo! Mahalo! [Thanks! Thanks!]" they exclaimed as they saw the extent of his wealth, for at this time a quantity of red feathers not greater than what might be got from a tomtit would purchase a hog of forty or fifty pounds weight, and he gave them each a handful of the feathers of white chickens which had been none too expertly died but were beyond criticism in the flaming redness of their color.

On leaving the ship, these three exhibited the greatest friendliness. Children that they were, they quickly forgot unpleasant scenes and turned at once to the gaiety and laughter which comes so easily to those of this sunny land. "Tis a victory for us," said Flint, "but there go enough feathers to buy an island. We must not let him beggar himself, or he'll have naught to build the kingdom we plan to share with him."

And so we tried to husband Omai's wealth for him and for us, but so prodigal was he that within a few hours these precious feathers had fallen to a tenth of their original rate of exchange. 

Our own first purchases from the Indians who came aboard were confined to bananas, which we ate greedily, and green coconuts, inside of which we found cool, sweet water. It appeared that these commodities had not much more value than sticks and stones, and Flint and I had our fill for the price of one small red feather.

This day we dressed in our best finery, which is to say that we tried to rub away the mildew and the stains which damp weather had placed upon our clothes. Moths had eaten several holes in my coat and also in my hat, and heavy wrinkles showed plainly no matter how carefully we had laid away our garments. 

But such small imperfections were not noticed by the natives. 

They stood in awe of our clothes, which must have seemed to them the work of magic, for they had not learned the arts of spinning and weaving. "Now there is a boat you and me can use!" Flint exclaimed as a large double canoe came toward us, paddled by some two dozen strong men. A platform was rigged between the twin hulls,

LOST EDEN 

and upon this stood a giant of a man draped in white cloth. “That is the most seaworthy of the lot," I said, and a sense of intriguing adventure came over me. This might very well be the canoe which would carry us to freedom and the enchantment of the beckoning land which lay before us. "Look at 'im barge through the little fellows! He must be a big cabbage in these parts. He is our taio, eh?"

This regal visitor came straight through the small boats, overturning many and spilling their occupants into the water. This we did not understand at first, but later we learned that he was indeed a high chief, named Tabourai. His subjects prostrated themselves upon his approach, whether on land or water, and lying prone and helpless in their canoes, they often came under the bows of the chief's boat and capsized.

Captain Cook took little notice of the chiefs for, as always, he preferred to be greeted by no less a personage than a king, and so Flint and I welcomed this big fellow as he came over the side. 

When he dropped his two-hundred-odd pounds upon the deck, all the Indians there fell prone. There seemed to be no doubt of his importance. "Taio!" Flint and I said at the same time. "Taio!" he replied with much enthusiasm, and he accepted me at once as a friend, probably thinking himself most fortunate to become so quickly associated with a man as elegantly dressed as I. "My name Tabourai," he volunteered.

I tried out my knowledge of the Tahitian language which I had acquired in long hours with Omai and found that I could make myself quite well understood.

We "rubbed noses," as our people called it, but it was more a pressing of the nose to one cheek and then the other. He kissed my hand and then placed it to his forehead. "You stop at my house?" "That we will!" Flint cried. "You can lay to that.” "Then come with me," said Tabourai, and we accepted his invitation at once.

As we stepped aboard his canoe, the sturdy Indian warriors in her laughed and began a lively, full-throated chant, keeping ex-

LOST EDEN 

135 cellent rhythm with their paddles, striking them upon the hulls in unison. They seemed to be as highly pleased with us as we were with them. "A well-stepped mast, and never a nail anywhere," Flint observed, "but matting for a sail is jury rig. We must get some canvas for her."

The twin hulls, each about twenty-five feet long, were logs of koa, a very hard wood, hollowed by burning with small fires. 

Some of the charred surface still remained, but most had been scraped away with stone tools. Each hull had a width of about two feet, and prows about six feet high were affixed to fend off surf or heavy seas.

Thwarts of small logs were lashed to the gunwales to hold the hulls about four feet apart, and upon these thwarts a good, solid platform afforded a kind of dais for the native Chief Tabourai and the visiting Chiefs Flint and Westermark.

We skimmed through the water, overturning several canoes, negotiated the surf easily, and touched the solid land, this time a beach rather than a rocky reef. "On shore at last!" Flint exclaimed. "And I must remember to ask Dr. Anderson to make a proper prayer of thanks for me, now that I am delivered from the sea. He is a good fellow and one 

I will miss." "And I."

But we had little time to discuss this happy event, for the warriors seized us and carried us upon their shoulders in triumph. 

I, for one, appreciated this transportation, for rheumatism had stiffened my joints so that walking pained me, and also I was weakened with scurvy as with a fever. 

A crowd of many hundreds had assembled and had begun the preparation of a feast as soon as they learned of our intention to anchor here. Mats had been laid for our people to sit upon in a palm-fringed grove smelling sweetly of many flowers, their fragrance intermingled and brought to us on the gentlest of balmy breezes.

One scent stood out from the others, and so intoxicating it was that I decided I would search out its source at the first opportunity.

LOST EDEN 

But when the Indians began to open their underground ovens, such appetizing aromas assailed us that we thought only of food. Sailors who had been staring at the women now saw only the viands the women brought to us. 

I had seen Omai build such an oven on an English beach to entertain and astonish the guests of Mr. Banks. He dug a hole two feet deep or thereabouts, heated some large stones in a fire and placed them at the bottom of the pit. Over these he spread green leaves. He wrapped meat and fish in other green leaves, placed them on the stones, and covered the whole with earth. 

This method of cookery has the advantage of retaining the steam and volatile parts of the food until the oven is opened. Then wonderfully good odors are released, such as might well cause a hungry sailor man to swoon in delight.

There was succulent young pork for us here, baked with fish by wrapping the two together in a great green leaf of the ti plant. 

Heaps of plantains awaited us and large bowls of a delicious pudding. 

We thought to eat all that was offered and yet be hungry, so great were our appetites, but none of us could come up with Chief Tabourai, who sat between Flint and me and gave a most extraordinary demonstration of stowing food. 

He ate three fish the size of small carp, two joints of pork weighing the better part of a pound each, four breadfruit the size of melons, fourteen plantains which were about seven inches long and more than half that large around, and to digest the whole he consumed above a quart of a fermented paste made of taro root and called by them poi. This, I thought, must be some kind of record.

A well-fed crew we were that day, and all of us bulged visibly at the waist. "Where is Queen Oberea?" I asked my host, for I was most anxious to become acquainted with this regal personage, the better to feather the nest which I was determined to make in this group of islands. men." 137 "I have some presents for her," I said, for Mr. Banks had asked me to seek her out and make gifts in his name. "I take you there," he said, and so, when the feast was over,

And so I mounted my human steed, and with great good nature he carried me as easily as though I had been a small child. 

We followed a path through a coconut grove of cooling shade and past a bamboo thicket which rattled its tall stalks in the pleasant breeze to make a sound like the falling of many dried leaves in an autumn wind. An eerie pale green light came down through the fluttering foliage, enfolding me in the charm of this fabled land.·

Chapter 20 

THE PALACE OF THE QUEEN WAS NOT MORE THAN WHAT ENGLISH

men would call a grass hut on a large scale, but to these people it stood as a monument to a craft that they had developed through unknown centuries of Stone Age culture while Europeans had built with tools of metal.

This Buckingham Palace of Tahiti extended the distance of some forty feet and had a width of about fifteen. With evident pride in the grandeur of it, they bade me enter. 

High pillars of polished coconut boles supported large ceiling beams, all hewn with stone axes which do not cut but merely bruise the wood and wear it away bit by bit. Very crude it seemed to us, but somewhere, perhaps still living, was a brown master craftsman who had the honor to build this place because he could swing a stone at a tree better than his fellow men, better than anyone else in all the world, for aught he knew. 

The thatch of the roof was laid and lashed in a way to challenge the best of our own English cottagers. The side walls were neatly formed of bamboo strips, with some open cracks between, to be sure, but only the better to let in the balmy soft airs, and the ends of the room had been left entirely open for this purpose.

A floor of white sand had been newly smoothed, and at one end a fresh-smelling bed of grass and mats was laid, with tiny benches of wood serving as pillows. It was a large bed, because all of the immediate household slept here, including a handsome young man of about twenty years named Obadee, who obviously was the queen's favorite. 138

LOST EDEN 

139 "The queen is not here now, but at her bath in the surf," said a female attendant. "Will you wait?" "I will," I said, and seated my weary but well-fed body upon a brown mat, cleverly woven of small strippings of a long, fibrous grass. 

I had not long to wait. With a laughing train of about a dozen comely maids, the queen came in, naked and dripping sea water, but smiling and unabashed. A tall woman of six feet she was, perhaps forty years of age, but well formed and full of life. "Mr. Banks sends his greetings," I said, arising somewhat painfully, and I asked a man to bring in the chest of presents. "Ahhh. Tapane! You know him?" Her smile showed two rows of even white teeth. Tapane was their pronunciation of the word "Banks." "I lived at his house for a time." "Ahhh. Tapane. He did not come?" "No, but he asked me to thank you for the many presents you sent him by Omai, and to accept these from him." 

I then placed the chest upon the sandy floor and opened it, to the amazement of all, for it contained a gown made of cloth of gold, a smart red cape of wool, many gay ribbons, about a dozen strings of beads, and a score of bracelets. 

Oberea tried the presents on, one by one, with her servants helping and admiring. First she slipped on the golden gown and walked about, exclaiming and sighing. Then she took it off and donned the red cape. "Very fine! Very fine!” she said. “Tapane, he is well?” "Yes, very well." "But you. You have the stiffness. You have been too long at sea. Is it not so?" "Yes, I am stiff with rheumatism." "Then come with me and you shall have lomi-lomi," she answered, and with no more ado she picked me up in her arms as she might have gathered up a small child and carried me about a hundred yards to a smaller house of grass and reeds and laid me tenderly upon a thick mat. 

This kind ministration so surprised me that I could think of no way out of my embarrassing situation, but I was glad that I

LOST EDEN 

did not resist, for I knew that I had found a place in her motherly affections, and I felt that she would help me to stay here if she could. 

She clapped her hands and six girls entered. "Lomi-lomi!" she ordered. “Make good lomi-lomi."

Now this, I thought, might be a mixture of the strong drink they call ava or kava, but instead it was a kind of massage quite miraculous in its effect.

The girls removed my clothing and began to pinch and pummel all my muscles, from head to foot. Yet it was no ordinary massage, for soon I began to note that some art of their own was causing my blood and my humors to affect my sore muscles so that they relaxed their stiffness, and although I soon felt like a perfect mummy, my pains were mild ones caused by pinching and rubbing and not sharp ones of a rheumatic nature.

They let me rest about an hour and then came at me again. "Ouch!" I would cry, and they would laugh, and at the end of the second pummeling I emerged more than half cured.

A good beginning in the making of acquaintances, I thought, for in one day I had found a cordial friend with a likely canoe and the queen herself had cared for me as though I were a blood relative.

A friendly people they were indeed. Feasting continued that day almost without interruption, and with it came entertainment of surprising variety. There was a stirring chorus of some seventy men. They sang and shouted to the accompaniment of the loud and powerful drums which they call maffa, and they performed a kind of military dance, holding little wooden paddles before them. "What can you do as well as that?" the king asked Captain Cook. "Perhaps nothing, but we will gladly try," he answered, and ordered Lieutenant Phillips to drill the marines.

The natives seemed to be unimpressed with this kind of dancing, so stiff in comparison to theirs, until the marines fired a volley from their muskets, at which a large number of the brown people ran into the woods. 

The king was not to be outdone, so he ordered a dance of the women, which was performed with such grace and rhythm and

LOST EDEN 

141 abandon that none thought of leaving this feast for the eyes, and we watched dances hour after hour. It was the kind of holiday for which most of the men had sailed halfway around the world. 

In one of the dances the women wore tight black skirts, with nothing above but black feathers at their armpits like nosegays. Sometimes they danced on their hands and knees, making their fingers move incredibly fast.

But the Indians were much astonished in their turn when Cook had the two horses brought from the Resolution and he and Captain Clerke rode them about the island. None of the natives had seen animals larger than hogs and were inclined to run away at first, but soon they understood the usefulness of the beasts in carrying men across the land at a good rate of speed. 

This demonstration was for them the highest point in the day's entertainment, unless perhaps it might have been the moment when Dr. Anderson removed his powdered wig to mop perspiration from his pate. I doubt if he could have surprised them more by unscrewing a leg or an arm from his body. 

In these competitions of entertainment we passed the hours until three o'clock in the morning. Cook suggested that we retire, but the king smilingly asked if we could provide nothing of greater entertainment, and he thought the competition won by his side, when Mr. Clerke said, "What of our fireworks?" "Yes, the fireworks," Cook agreed. “We must hold up our end of the show." 

First our people fired a red rocket which burst into stars, and so many of the Indians ran away with cries of alarm that for a moment it appeared that none would stay for further entertainment.

But when some of the sailors held Italian candles in their hands and shot sparks and fireballs from them, the fireworks must have appeared less harmful, and our hosts gathered again upon the beach.

When an intricate white piece called a waterfall was displayed upon a table, the gentle beauty of it caused the crowd to press in close, and they had to be ordered back to a safe distance. 

Then a capricious wheel was fired upon the table, revolving upon a spindle and throwing off trails of white, yellow, red,

LOST EDEN 

and maroon colors in a flaming spray. By some mischance it escaped from control and left the table and charged the audience like a fire demon.

To the Indians it must have appeared to be animated by a devastating spirit of evil, for that crowd of several thousand dispersed in a moment. Even the most resolute among them fled with precipitation, nor would any inducement bring them back from the woods that night. 

Our people laughed. "We have won!" shouted Captain Clerke.

But I was far from cheerful, for my taio had gone charging through a palm grove like a great, wild bull, and I feared that I had lost his friendship and trust and that my plans for escape might have been ruined utterly, and so I sought him out. 

I found him before his hut of grass, in earnest conversation with about a dozen of his retainers. At sight of me he started, his eyes wide with terror. "There is nothing to fear," I said. “All is well.” "How many did the fire devil kill?" he asked. "None." I laughed. "It was not a devil, but a game with fire, a harmless game." 

He was not soon persuaded, but at last he smiled. "I cannot sleep this night, I think," he moaned. "I tremble. 

Will you sleep in my house?" "Yes, I am honored," I replied, and always I will be glad that I accepted his invitation, because for my first night in Tahiti I could have hoped for nothing more novel or more stimulating to an artist's sense of primitive charm and grace of living. 

Servants spread the petals of sweet-smelling flowers upon the floor mat used for a bed, and over these they laid a clean sheet of tapa. A girl of about seventeen came and kissed my host's big toe and then went away. "The bed is ready," said Tabourai. “Let us sleep. I am very happy to have you here."

He stretched out with a loud sigh, and I took a place beside him, weary and well fed, and was surprised at the comfort of that soft, clean sheet beneath me. I laid my cheek upon it, and the

LOST EDEN 

143 fragrance of many flowers, as yet unknown to me, came through the cloth.

Soon I heard the music of flutes outside in serenade. Soft and elfinlike the melodies were played, and I could have drifted off to sleep most peacefully but for Tabourai, who must have been severely shaken by the fire devil, for he tossed about and sighed. 

The girl who kissed his toe had also another tender ministration which she revealed at this time. She came into the room quietly and covered him with another sheet. Then she sat down and patted him on the back and legs and buttocks with her palms, and sometimes with her small fists, quite rhythmically for almost an hour, at which time he was fast asleep and snoring.

This sedative, called tooge-tooge, she applied several times, at the least sign of the chief's awakening. A most effective sleeping potion this was for him, but quite the opposite for me, because for a long time I was kept awake either by his snoring or by the pata-pat of the girl's hands upon his body.

I sighed, and I must have been heard outside the chamber, for another girl entered with a white sheet, laid it upon me, and began at once to play the tattoo of tooge-tooge upon my tired body. I found it to be most soothing. It was so enjoyable that I hoped to remain awake a long time, but soon I also fell asleep beneath the patting of the girl's gentle hands. 

When the pain of my rheumatism awoke me a short time later, she was lying beside me in the darkness which was lightly scattered by the moon. My hand was upon her shoulder, the skin of which had been made soft by the application of scented coconut oil. 

This gave me a start. "Oh!" I exclaimed, withdrawing my hand in haste, an act which caused her to sit upright upon our bed of petals so suddenly that she awakened Tabourai. "I am sorry," I made haste to say, with great regret at my unconscious familiarity with a member of his household. "You like my Pinau?" he asked with a warm smile, as he stroked her hair. Then he patted me on the shoulder and turned away again to sleep, as one who felt more complimented than

LOST EDEN 

offended at his guest's intimacy with the charming girl called

Pinau. My attitude seemed to give her grave concern, and at once she began again her soothing pat-a-pat upon my tired body. 

In my half-dreaming state I told myself that all that I could wish for was here, and a young fellow might do worse than to marry Pinau. My good taio had in effect given me his blessing, but first I wished to fall in love. "That might be quite easy," I thought as she patted me, and in my half-awake state I thought that here at last was a portion of the Garden of Eden which had not got into the books or the legends. 

But when I awoke in the warm, balmy dawn, Pinau was gone. Perhaps more disconcerting, my breeches were gone also, and with them my musket and my sailor's knife. Into Eden, then, had crept the sin of covetousness whose fruit is theft. 

I sat bolt upright, and this movement awoke my host. "Ahhh," he sighed sleepily. "You are yet here. It is well, my taio." "A thief has stolen my musket and knife, and my breeches as well." "It is to be expected. Come, let us have food."

Here was a new attitude toward theft. I think it sprang from the nature of the country, which furnished so much of the pleasures of life that possessions were not greatly cherished. If food or implements disappeared, they could be replaced readily and with but little effort. I do believe Tabourai thought no more of the matter than I would think of my brother's borrowing my knife and forgetting to return it for the moment. "I must find the thief," I protested. "Very good. We find him," my host agreed, and got slowly to his feet and donned a freshly laundered skirt and cape of pure white cloth which had been laid ready to his hand in the night. 

We had not gone far when we encountered a smiling brown man with my breeches wrapped about his waist and tied between his legs in the custom of his country. "They are mine," I said, and he gave them back in the manner of one who thought I would neither care about my loss nor

I will teach you how to do it."

"Hono!" That was as near as he could come to the correct pronunciation, and the way he rolled the vowels made me think that my name sounded better in his tongue.

I knew that with the exchange of names I had become a fast friend, and I lost no time in furthering my plans to stop in these islands.

LOST EDEN 

"I would like to stay here when my ship sails away," I began. "Would you like that?" "Good! Be very good! You stop my house." "That I will, and bring another friend, also, if I may." If you can take us off in your canoe for a few days, we will hide from Cook and he will leave without us." “Good. Good. I am very happy, Tabourai.” "And I, Hono."

And so my plan was laid, and my spirits were high as the sun came booming up out of the east and painted the clouds a flaming red and brought the land to life. 

I could see the brown people venturing again slowly toward the ships, like wild birds which are frightened away by a shot but come back singly and in pairs to see again what the mysteries of life may offer.

And I was as eager and curious as any to explore this new life and plan my future in this Paradise.

THIS BEING A SABBATH MORN, A CHURCH SERVICE WAS HELD UPON 

the shore for such as might be inclined to attend, and this included the greater part of our company, for the spirit of thankfulness was upon them, oddly mixed, perhaps, with some thought of atonement for their transgressions. 

Many of our people came with their ladies of the evening before, smiling and happy although a bit rheumatic. Nearly all were decked in strings of flowers.

The charming Pinau, who had slept beside me, placed a wreath of fragrant white ginger blooms upon my head, and she wore a necklace of red ginger which enhanced the loveliness of her bare young breasts as she sat beside me. For the girls this churchgoing was a pleasurable experience, though no doubt puzzling.

As Dr. Anderson began to read from his prayer book, hundreds of curious natives gathered around, maintaining a hushed silence in imitation of us. "Where are your idols?" Pinau asked. "We have none," I whispered, and her brown eyes opened wide in wonderment. "We thank Thee for our safe deliverance," the doctor prayed, "and for the many blessings of this bountiful land." see."
