LOST EDEN 

When the service came to an end I helped her to her feet. "We are finished now," I explained, "and the remainder of the day is ours to do with as we please." "Good!" she exclaimed, and placed her arms about me.

So began this Sunday, but the rest of the day was given over to such nudity and amorous dalliance as must have fulfilled the wildest expectations of our men, and certain I am that in many instances it went far beyond their imaginings. 

Dr. Anderson turned his eyes from these entertainments and came to me as I walked with Pinau through a shady bower where the white clustering flowers called tamanu rioted in profusion, accented by the red atae which clamors for attention in nearly all the landscape here.

When he saw us arm in arm I thought I noticed his eyebrows raise a bit. "Oh, John," he said, "would you care to walk with me toward that mountain yonder?"

He pointed to a tall central peak. No doubt the good doctor had my welfare in mind, for often he would discourse with me upon the beauties of life which included not only the charms of the land and the living plants and animals upon it, but also the nobility of the human body, to which he had devoted much of his life in the practice of medicine. "I would, sir," I replied, and to Pinau, in her language, "Will you come with us for a walk?” "Yes. Yes." Her eyes sparkled.

We were fortunate to have her for a guide, because she knew the growing things with the intimacy of one who believes them to have spirits and to be related to the diverse family of 

Tahitian gods.

We were attracted first by a tree bedecked with large flowers tinted a delicate canary yellow which seemed intensified by a blackish-red center. "Hau," she said, giving the name of the tree. "Flowers change color in day and at evening fall off. Very sad, such a short life. They are here to tell us life is not long and we must enjoy it today, for we too may go soon."

She chanted a little prayer which I could not understand

LOST EDEN 

149 because it originated long ago when language was different here. 

But from that day onward I seldom passed a hau tree without looking into its flowers, for soon after they opened in the morning they began to change from yellow to a rich salmon shade and then were dead by evening. “Hutu is same spirit in nighttime," she said, pointing to a tree with large and beautiful flowers like the magnolia. Its white blossoms, tipped with magenta, open only in the evening. It adds to the perfume of the night air, and it loves only the night, for in the morning it withers and dies and bows to the hau blossoms of the coming day, the two working together to assure that Tahitian airs shall not go unperfumed by day or by night. "Do the hau and the hutu make you sad?" I asked her. "Yes, sad and happy," she replied paradoxically. "Is that why you sing sad songs when you are happy?" "Yes, maybe. Tahitians always sing sad; always happy." "What do you make of that?" I asked the doctor. "Not a very strange philosophy," he answered. "We sing sad songs very often when we make merry. But perhaps we are neither as sad nor as merry as these people.' 

We laughed at a tree like an umbrella, called autataa, whose trunk stood straight and free to a height of twelve or fifteen feet, its branches growing from the top and curving down for all the world like a parasol.

A strange monster of a banyan tree called ora, with a trunk resembling a mass of tentacles and numerous pendulous roots reaching from the limbs to the ground, seemed a fabled remora or sucking fish walking upon the land. 

With Pinau laughing and talking to the forest gods, we walked through ferns the height of trees and examined a pandanus or screw pine with leaves six feet long. In a beautiful coconut grove we stood still, entranced by a divine fragrance. "What do you smell?" asked Dr. Anderson. "Could it be jasmine?” "Yes, that is it."

Soon we found a profusion of the tiny white starflowers much like our English jasmine. It was the scent which had haunted me since first I came ashore.

Nails were highly valued as tools because they would hold an edge better than the native implements of stone or shell or bone. These "scrapers," as the brown people called them, were the gold of the islands.

We found this a lively topic of conversation, but we had taken scarce two dozen pulls upon the oars when the boat began to fill and sink. "Damme," cried Flint, "she is a bloomin' sieve, she is." And so I thought our secret might have leaked out or that these others had devised a plan of escape similar to ours. Those were typical remarks that day. If a leader were to arise from among the men and urge them to it, I doubted not that some manner of wholesale desertion or mutiny would ensue.

About ten of the morning it was discovered that many nails had been drawn from the ships themselves, and upon seeking for a culprit of whom to make an example, the officers found that Alfred Hornby had been seen removing a cleat and taking the nails from it. He had gone farther than most in that he had thrown the cleat overboard, thus willfully destroying the King's property. 

The punishment might have been a flogging with the cat, but much merriment was heard in the cabin when the officers discussed the matter and, when they had finished, a boat was dispatched down the shore. It returned loaded with nettles, which were barbed with thousands of small points which entered the skin when touched and remained there, like the stingers of bees, to cause a painful irritation. 

The people were piped aft, and Cook began to speak. "Nails are of great value now," he said.

LOST EDEN 

153 "Hear! Hear!" the people shouted, and they laughed uproariously, for they knew what he meant. "And they are well guarded," Cook continued, "except for those which hold the ship together. It should not be necessary to teach English seamen that it is imprudent to destroy the ship which must take them home." "Hear! Hear!" shouted some, in pretended sincerity, but I am sure that a vote taken at this time would have shown that nearly all were for knocking the ships apart and using the nails for the special kind of trading at which they excelled. "Alfred Hornby," said Cook, "you are found guilty of the theft and destruction of a cleat. You will disrobe."

A paleness came into Hornby's face. He took off his shirt and waited to be trussed up for a flogging at the gangway. "Strip off everything," Cook commanded, and so Hornby took off his breeches as well, completely denuding himself, at which the brown men and women watching in the rigging made much merriment. "Bosun, pass out a bundle of nettles to each man. Mr. Bligh, space the men evenly around the deck." 

It was ever Captain Cook's way to lighten punishment if he could accomplish his ends thereby, and humiliation was one of his weapons; but of all such experiments, this was the drollest. "Hornby," Cook ordered, "you will run thrice around the deck. Mr. Bligh, you will see to't that each man strikes with his nettles, and make sure that they are laid on with a will.” "Aye, aye, sir,” Bligh replied, and by the tightness of his lips across his teeth we knew that he was of a mind to see the order carried out. a will. new device of punishment proved to be most mild because most were as guilty as Hornby. It was a case of "Let he who is without guilt among you cast the first stone." There will be hell to pay." "A whole keg gone?" “Aye, and mostly given to the women. John, this tub will fall apart and founder in the next blow, and no nails to mend her with. We must stay here, fair or foul."

And those who knew about the nails disappearing from the stores as well as from all parts of the ship continued to watch the attitude of our captain. It may be said that Cook's downfall began this day, for, better than any other, he knew that nails were worth more to us than gold, and to the natives as well, and he pitted himself against this encroaching evil of theft and fought a losing battle.

Soon Mr. Bligh caught an Indian in the act of handing a small iron chisel over the side of the ship and seized him and led him to the armorer. "Is this your chisel?" he asked. "Aye, aye, sir. "Tis that." "Have you given it away?" "No, sir. That I have not. 'Tis but freshly stolen and I have much need of it this day. Let me run him through, sir!"

The armorer drew his sword and made to dispatch the thief. "Hold!" Bligh commanded. “It is what he deserves, but he shall have justice. Bring him to the captain." 

And when Cook heard the charge he yet could smile, but with difficulty. "They are but children," he said. "I have punished one of our own people first, to show what we think of thieving. Now let us punish this man in some similar manner. How would it be to shave the hair from his head and so make him a laughingstock?" ""Tis done, if you say so." "Very well." Cook smiled and watched the shaving and noted with satisfaction the laughter of the other Indians.

LOST EDEN 

155 "But look out for a squall," said Flint. “The storekeeper has discovered the theft of the keg of nails."

Soon the storm broke. Cook issued harsh orders and all of us were piped on deck and searched. Alas for Arthur McCoy. He had seven new, shiny nails in his pocket, all of the same size as those which had been stolen. Red-handed he was, for there was no excuse for him to have a nail about his person. "We will have another demonstration now," said Cook, and his manner was one of stern justice. "Two dozen lashes. Omai, call all the Indians you can and have them watch. Tell them why we punish this man. Tell them they will be treated the same way unless they stop their thieving."

And so the unfortunate McCoy was stripped to the waist and trussed up while Omai addressed the natives from the quarterdeck with great pomp and show of oratory. "This man, he steal toas!" he declaimed. "And now we cut his back many times with our cutting branches. Ver' painful. Ver' bad. You steal and we cut you too, ver' bad."

At the first stroke of the cat the natives screamed and wailed and begged the captain to desist, for never had they seen one man cut another man's back in this fashion, but the full twenty-four strokes were given. 

That was the price McCoy paid for the theft of seven nails no longer than his little finger; but thieving was the very root from which stemmed most of our troubles and disasters among these people, and our hard-put captain met the situation as best he could. 

Cook had not long to wait for the next offense. An Indian made off with a sextant from under the very noses of the officers and leaped overboard with it. Five marines fired at him as he swam for the shore, but most of his swimming was under water, performed most expertly, and none of the balls took effect. 

It was my own friend, Chief Tabourai, who brought him back, for, as always, the man had taken his treasure directly to his chief. "You shoot him now," said Tabourai, much humiliated, suggesting the type of punishment which he himself would mete out to such an offender.

LOST EDEN 

"No," said Cook. "We will treat him as we treat our own people. It is what we call justice." 

And so he had the man seized up and twenty-four lashes laid upon his bare back. The man groaned, but his groanings were more in anger than in pain, and when the twenty-fourth was done and his wrists untied, he stood sullen and angry, staring at the captain in defiance. "Lash him off the ship!" Cook ordered, in considerable heat, for insubordination he could not tolerate in brown or white. 

And so the bosun continued to flail the man while he walked slowly to the rail and dived over, unconquered and unbroken in spirit. "He is not chastised," Bligh observed. "I doubt not that we will hear from him again."

And so, as often happened, Bligh was right in his gauging of a man. Soon this Indian was caught tearing up the grapevines which Cook had planted on his previous visit. All knew what store he set by these growing things, and perhaps no greater insult could have been offered him.

When the man, still bleeding, was again brought before Cook with this new charge, our captain began to pace up and down, and the two familiar pink spots of anger came to his cheeks. 

We could scarce believe his command when it came. "Cut off his ears!" Cook's fists clenched. "His ears?" The bosun stood with his mouth open. "Yes, his ears! Do as I say!"

And so, while four men held the Indian, the bosun drew his knife and sliced off an ear. He stood holding it and looking at it for a moment, as one who knew not what to do with another man's ear in his hand. Then at last, realizing the utter worthlessness of an ear detached from a body, he threw the strange pruning into the sea.

The Indian made not a sound, but glared fiercely as his other ear left him in the same manner. It appeared that this punishment might make him a more dangerous enemy than ever, and when his own countrymen began to laugh at a man without ears, he again dived into the sea and swam ashore. It was an idyllic two weeks we spent there, Flint and I, with the light hearts of anticipation. I worked hard with my pencil and paints, for often I felt a twinge of guilt at deserting because of the many kindnesses Mr. Banks had showed me, and I resolved to come even with him if I could.

With feasting and entertainment every day, the days passed, and we began to wonder when Cook would sail, for our health was now recovered and our ships stocked with provisions. "I will be hard to find on sailing day," said Webb. “And I,” Gibson said, for they and many others thought 158

LOST EDEN 

159 that Cook would make known his sailing date and that he would put to sea without them if they were well hidden.

But our captain revealed continuously the qualities of an astute commander who knew his men as well as he knew his ships. 

Although he held himself in check when women offered their charms, his eyes were not closed to the conduct of his men, and what he learned of their plans he kept to himself. 

Accordingly he maneuvered all hands aboard on the evening of the sixth of December, and early on the morning of the seventh quite unexpectedly gave orders to set sail. ing." "Wenches over the side!" Bligh commanded. "We are sail 

When this order was put into effect the greatest of clamoring and wailing went up, for we had above fifty women aboard. 

Many of our people were despondent, for they had planned to stop ashore with their sweethearts when Cook sailed, but he had outwitted them to the last man.

But the surprising aspect of this situation was that even the most casual lover amongst us was saddened and deeply moved by the tears and desolate cries of the women, for they seated themselves upon the deck and joined in the most plaintive of singing. 

Not a few took out from their clothing small instruments made of a short stick with a very sharp shark's tooth affixed to the end, and with these they beat themselves about the head and breasts, drawing blood at each stroke, so that soon trickles ran from them and splashed upon the deck.

These instruments they passed, one to another, and in a few moments all were bleeding and wailing most mournfully, and some were such lovely lasses that I think the most hardened among us was moved to pity.

The women begged to go with us, to go anywhere, even to Pritane, as they pronounced the name of Britain, and they forced our officers to drive them from the ships with spanks upon their buttocks or by forcibly throwing them overboard.

Good swimmers all, they followed us until we gathered too much way, then some got into the canoes which began to come up to us, and all continued their lamentations until we stood some

LOST EDEN 

two miles offshore and the dangers of the open sea forced them to return to land.

My good friend Tabourai came up fast in his big double canoe and sailed around us for some time, waving and laughing. "What makes that fellow so cheerful when all the others are sad?" Bligh asked. "He seems glad to be shed of us.” "He's pupule," answered Flint, using the native word meaning crazy, and, throwing a dark look toward Bligh, he spat into the sea.

A sad crew it was as that green gem of an island faded into purple and grew small in the distance. At each unfurling of a sail new showers of cockroaches fell upon the men, and they took cover, venting curses and striking the vermin from their clothing and stamping upon them as they scuttled about the deck.

Chapter 24 

"WE HAD BEST FIND OMAI," I SAID TO ED FLINT, “AND SET HIM IN 

a better frame of mind, if we are to stop with him on the island of Maitea. He is in no mood to become a king, but rather an outcast, unless he mends his ways." "He gave away more than half his wealth at Tahiti and got naught but enemies in return," Flint observed. “He is like a brat who has not felt the rod. Aye, we must straighten him out.”

And so, as we sailed that sunny sea, only one day before we reached Maitea, we sought out Omai to see how we might reform him and so make him the kind of sponsor we wished to have when we would at last make our home on the island. 

We found him in his cabin with two chests open and all manner of gear spread about. Two sailors were there with him. "I trade you ten plates of china for one knife," Omai was saying. "Make it all the plates and cups you've got and 'tis a deal," said the sailor named Hayworth. "Ver' good. English things not wanted here. Knives make the dam' commoners fear me,” Omai replied, handing over a good set of china. "Now how many cartouches you give for this flagon? 

Many cartouches for my poo-poo and I kill many men. Give Omai ver' big handful.” "Damme!" Flint whispered. "He has well-nigh beggared himself a'ready!"

"Give me both the flagons, and also the big blackjack to hold me beer," the other sailor suggested, holding out two cartouches. "Take all flagons," said Omai. "Coconut shell much better.

We were not then to have much of the benefit of this produce of England in our future home unless we stopped this wasteful trading, nor would we have a strong leader to protect us unless we could change his attitude. "Omai, you'll not have many presents for your people unless you put an end to this trading," I said. "No presents; no matter," he replied sullenly. "I not stop here long. I go on next ship to England and I tell King George to punish Captain Cook. King George my friend.” “Come, it is no use," Flint whispered. “We must find him in a better mood than this."

But I could not give up so easily. "With these presents you can make many friends among your own people,” I began. "No. They not be my people. My people live Huaheine. 

People on Maitea not good." "But you will not be a chief there unless you give many presents." "They will know I am king when I ride my great hog, and they will fear me when I fire my poo-poo. I shoot them dead."

This was bad news indeed. His manner now was that of a native of a savage island who would have more of war than of peace. However, there remained considerable wealth in his chests, which gave us some hope of his acceptance on the island we planned to share with him, and he would have also several animals and a house of wood.

The next day, when we stood in for Maitea, Omai's attitude had not changed. He had become a primitive Indian, with a smoldering fire in his dark eyes where often I had seen much gentleness and fun.

When we were yet some two leagues from shore, canoes began to come off to us and soon swimmers appeared.

LOST EDEN 

163 "Tute! Tute!" was the cry of welcome they gave to Cook, and he waved pleasantly in response; but Omai ignored the brown people, holding his nose high in the air, nor would he have aught to do with the king or any of the chiefs. "I am more a king than any here," he insisted, but the chiefs would not kowtow to this lowborn man of arrogant ways.

Accordingly, he gave presents only to the commoners, and so lavish was he that some of them acquired more wealth than the king of Maitea.

Nor did Omai have the power of a chief to prevent theft, and to this vice we found the natives of Maitea addicted in a most discouraging manner. He had some basis for his objection to this land, for the people here were neither so large nor so light in color as others we had seen, nor the women so pleasing, and always when we found a personable girl we discovered that she came from another island. "We must build Omai's house without nails," said Cook, "or it will be torn down in a few hours after we sail." 

Slowly our captain's wrath was rising, but his method of punishment ran its usual course, first seeking one of our own company to punish as an example, and he had not long to wait. 

Thievery was the order of the day with the Indians, and soon our men began to reciprocate. "He took my plantains!" an ugly, squat native complained, pointing at Fred Conway. "Is this true, Conway?" Cook demanded, and a cloudiness came upon his broad brow. "Only two, sir," Conway answered, "but this Indian and others have stolen everything within their reach." "And so you thought his conduct a good pattern for an 

Englishman! My orders are that you are never, never to steal. You know that!" His voice was rising, and his fists clenched. “A dozen lashes!" "But, sir! For two bananas? A dozen- "Seize him up! Call the bosun. Gather the Indians to witness."

And so we moored to the sound of the cat upon Conway's back and Omai delivering his usual speech of explanation to the

LOST EDEN 

brown people, and I do believe that he made enemies of all who listened. He walked back and forth upon the quarter-deck with his double-barreled musket, which he brandished at frequent intervals. "Now you know who rules here," was the essence of his speech.

Derision could be seen in the brown faces, and to many the harangue of Omai served only to stimulate their cunning and challenge their ability to steal, for while he talked and most of our people stood on deck to witness punishment, several Indians roamed over the ship in search of loot. "My quadrant!” cried Mr. King, pointing, and we saw a man leap from the rail with this instrument in his hands. "Fire over his head!"

Seven marines fired and splashed the water about the man's head, which action cowed him at once, and he began meekly to swim back to the ship. "The half-hour glass!" shouted a hand at the other side of the deck, and another native dived from the rail with the glass in his hand.

Omai took deliberate aim and, when the man's head showed above the water, fired and killed him instantly. "Omai!" Cook shouted. "Go to your quarters and stay there, you bungling idiot. The man has dropped the glass and it is lost." "Not a good beginning," said Flint, "but here we must stay. Are ye still of my opinion?" "Yes, I am," I answered, for now the ship was close to shore and the man in the chains heaved the lead more frequently, and each time he called the depth he seemed to touch some sensitive nerve in my body. I had disliked this sound day and night since we had gone upon the rocks the first time, and often in my hammock at night I would be taken with a nightmare and wake up shouting, "By the mark three!" Yes, I would stay.

The chief of the island came aboard and presented Cook with a large war canoe, and in his turn Cook gave a shirt frayed at the cuffs, a well-worn and discarded hat, and some beads to the value of scarce two farthings, so angry was he with the course of events here.

LOST EDEN 

165 But when the chief donned the shirt and flipped the tails about with pleasure, Cook's strain of generosity and forgiveness came again to the fore and he unbuckled his hangar and fastened it about the chief's waist.

At this gesture there was a fond rubbing of noses, and the chief was our friend, but much ill feeling had been caused already by Omai, and our difficulties rapidly increased when we landed. 

Our greatest trouble centered about a goat which Cook had left here on his previous visit. "Where is the goat?” he asked almost as soon as he set foot upon the shore.

But the Indians showed no desire to answer his question or to assist him in any way. “Tell me, where is the goat?" he asked again, his temper beginning to rise, for he brooked no opposition in any man. "It has gone fishing," said one, and all began to laugh. "It is mated with a turtle," said another. "Have you killed and eaten it?" Cook demanded, and began to fix them with that look of his eyes which boded ill. Here again was a matter most likely to arouse him. He had given them the animal in the name of humanity-a small gift of Europe, perhaps, but for him who had carried the goat over thousands of leagues of sea and nurtured it and shared water with it the animal held considerable importance. "The goat lives in the high tree on the high hill," said another Indian, and all laughed again, perhaps the most dangerous conduct for them. Making fun of our captain usually was the greatest goad to his anger. "If I do not find the goat, I will burn all your huts," Cook shouted, "and all your canoes!"

I think he meant it only as a threat, but Omai seemed delighted at the prospect. "Aye, that we will!" he cried.

He left us then, and we saw no more of him for above an hour, at which time we heard the cry of "Fire!"

About a dozen of the grass huts of the village were aflame. "To the boats!" Cook ordered. "Back to the ship."

As we got into the boats and pulled for the ship, a breeze

LOST EDEN 

from the sea caught the flames and fanned them through the village so that it appeared that hundreds of dwellings were burning. On the beach a group of canoes also had been set afire. 

I thought of the long hours required for the building of those precious boats, the tedious scraping with shells and sharp stones to hollow out the hulls, and I believe more damage was done to the Indians that day than could be repaired in many years. 

When we stepped aboard the Resolution, Cook gave orders to muster the people, and so I knew he meant to weigh and sail. "We are sailing," I said to Flint. "Aye. Omai has fouled our plans. What d'ye think now? 

Is it off to the North Pole with us?" "It may well be," I admitted. "We cannot stop here now." 

Nor would any others risk staying ashore at this place now. 

Fighting had broken out, and it was said that some Indians had been shot.

In this unpleasant manner we left Maitea, another island of our dreams. Long after dark we could see a glow of fire where the village had been, and of all our people who wished to desert, not one expressed a desire to be ashore, nor could one have lived the night through, in our opinion.

I lay awake long in my hammock, flicking a cockroach from my face now and then with an automatic gesture, as one brushes away the flies upon a summer's day. The last I remember hearing was the call of "By the deep twenty," coming faintly to me above the creak and groan of the rigging. And when I awoke I heard the selfsame sound and thought I had closed my eyes only a moment, but I saw the faint gray of dawn through the open port and knew that morn had come.

Soon after the sun flashed up in the east, Cook called Omai to account. "Now, my strutting young fellow," he began, "was it you who arranged the fire and the fighting so you would not be set ashore at Maitea?" "Oh no, sir!" Omai answered, with a show of innocence. "Many men fight, break canoes, burn them. Many spears come at us. Many stones fly. Ver' nasty, these Indians.” "If it were not against King George's wishes, I would swing you from a yardarm this moment!"

LOST EDEN 

167 "King George my friend," Omai replied with considerable guile. "Now you take me back to England?" "No! By Jehoshaphat, no!" "But you say not good to stop at Huaheine. Omai not like stop there, either. We go back to England now?" "No! Good or bad, you go to Huaheine." 

Huaheine! Another chance to get away! "And there you stay," Cook continued, "no matter what the outcome, and you had best behave yourself. You understand?" “Aye, sir, but Omai not like it." He hung his head in the manner of a spanked baby boy and departed. 

Thoughts of escape again came seething into my head, and

I had to check my high spirits, because they did not match the dismal mood of Cook. Wearily he turned to Bligh, whose moving eyes seemed to be everywhere upon the rigging and the sails. “Lieutenant," he said with a sigh, “I would rather command ten thousand Englishmen than ten Indians." “Aye, sir." "I can cope with my own kind, but the Indians defeat me." "It is because of your desire to serve your King, sir," Bligh answered, "and I would do the same, no matter how it might humble me in some other eyes, if I may be so bold as to say it, sir.” 

At this the captain's attitude softened. "You are a stern man, Bligh. I hope your firmness gives you less pain than mine has given me." He glanced over the stern rail and saw for the first time that we towed a large canoe. "Why was this boat not cast off?" "Your present from the chief, sir." "Yes, so it is. A pity we could not have left it behind, where we burned so many."

He regarded it for a moment, and an idea came into his versatile mind which utilized everything possible, down to the last thong of sealskin. “Omai!" he shouted, and I think his good humor had by now been restored almost completely by Bligh's talk of service to the King. again.

The petulant Omai came bowing upon the quarter-deck “Aye, aye, sir,” he said sadly.

LOST EDEN 

"I have a present for you." Cook pointed to the canoe in tow. "When you are a big chief on Huaheine, you will need a war canoe. This one is yours.' "Good! Ver' good!" Omai's eyes sparkled, and his boyish humor returned, for this was something he could comprehend. "I dress him. I dress me. I go to Huaheine like king. I make big show for those whoresons." "Not too big a show, and mind your language now," Cook admonished, but, as usual, his words rolled off the savage like sea water from a booby.

Omai began to decorate his boat in a manner never before seen on any sea. He had collected the flags of ten different countries, and these he flew from the stays of his crude but high mast. 

Every inch of the gunwales and thwarts he covered with gewgaws of the gaudiest description-bright-colored cotton, beads, mirrors, and feathers. 

And to my joy this day a canoe caught up with us, and in the stern stood my smiling, fat taio, Chief Tabourai. Making full twice our speed, he sailed close under our windward bow. 

His mighty warriors beat the gunwales with their paddles in rhythm and sang a full-throated war chant. His white garments fluttered in the breeze. He shouted, "Where to?" "To Huaheine!" Flint and I sang out together, but Flint's voice alone could have carried ten times the distance. "Now what is this?" asked Cook. "We are far from land.

They must be lost. It is best that they follow us to Huaheine." "Aye, sir," Flint answered, much pleased, and roared again, "To Huaheine! Follow us!" "Huaheine," the chief called back, and smiled.

However, he did not follow us, but continued on his course. "It is no use," said Cook. “If he chooses to go his way in that small chip of a boat, there is nothing we can do about it.” 

But Flint and I understood the smile of our rotund friend and felt sure that we would meet him in Huaheine, and that quite likely he would be there ahead of us. "There is our passage to freedom," I whispered. “Aye," Flint replied, “and a jolly little craft she be. Look at her skim the water!"

Chapter 25 

OUR HEARTS WERE LIGHT WHEN WE RAISED THE ISLAND OF HUAHEINE, some twenty miles east of Raiatea and Tahaa and eighty miles northwest of Tahiti.

Like Tahiti, it was composed of two volcanic islands, Huaheine-nui (great) and Huaheine-iti (small), to the south, united in a beautiful strait by a narrow isthmus which was submerged much of the time but showed above water at low tide. "It seems," said Dr. Anderson, "that each island is more beautiful than those we have seen before."

Deep blue lagoons border much of this land, formed by outer strips of low country connected with reefs here and there and densely wooded like the mainland. 

And to our delight, the first sail we saw was that of Tabourai's big canoe. It came up rapidly and soon skimmed past us. "Hono!" cried the jolly chief as his ponderous body bounced and jiggled with the motion of the boat. 

And we left him no doubt that we were glad to see him.

But Omai held the center of the stage that day. He had forgotten his loathing of this place in his desire to impress the natives with his own greatness. "I must go first, in my own ship," he said to Cook. "I will show them what a king should be. Have I not supped with King

George and lived with Sand'ich who is king of the English ships?” "Very well. It is your show," Cook replied, in good spirits at the thought of at last being rid of Omai and the troublesome animals still aboard. He and all the people laughed at the antics of this brown child of Nature who could be most entertaining and was this day at his best. 169

LOST EDEN 

Omai invited Flint and me to sail in his canoe, and I am sure that never did such a fantastic hero return to his native land. 

He got himself into a full suit of armor which Lord Sandwich had given him, as indeed he gave everything else this childish heart could desire. The armor was complete with helmet and visor, and a long white plume flowed from the crest of it, but that was not enough. To this Omai added as many large red ostrich feathers as could be made to stay upon it.

About his shoulders he fastened a bright red cape of velvet, and at his side he carried the jeweled sword which King George gave him. One pistol hung from a belt at his waist and another he held in his hand, and this he loaded and fired as rapidly as he could. 

He would not land at once, but sailed up and down the water front, declaiming his greatness and punctuating his remarks with pistol shots. "Omai returns from Pretane!" he intoned. "Omai (bang!) the master of the poo-poo and the seer of great things. Omai (bang!) the taio of Kilhargo, the great chief of all Pretane. Omai comes with wealth of a hundred kings and will ride his great hogs (bang!) to show that he is master of many new and wonderful affairs."

But I think his friends were less impressed than puzzled to see this strange apparition come upon their beach. They gathered around, hoping for presents, and many were delighted to receive generous largess from this prodigal. He also paid his respects to the heathen idols of the place, and I doubted no longer that he had returned to his primitive state-a good thing, perhaps, as he was to be cast ashore here.

He astounded the other Indians when his mare and stallion were brought from the ship. Many withdrew to a safe distance and made ready to run for the woods. 

He tried to mount, but the weight of his armor held him fast to the ground. "Help me up," he asked, and so Flint and I together managed to heave him into the saddle.

Cook mounted also, and the two began to ride—an act which was as awe-inspiring here as in Tahiti.

LOST EDEN 

171 But Omai, desirous of a greater effect, drew his two pistols and fired them simultaneously into the air. The stallion reared and threw him heavily to the ground with a loud clattering of the metal parts with which he had adorned himself. "Hiyah! Hiyah!" shouted the populace, in vast merriment to see the great chief from Pretane thus rolling in the dust. "This clown will spoil everything!" said Flint. "We can hope for little help from him. 'Tis yer fat friend must turn the trick for us." "Right you are, and a good stanch friend he is. His canoe is readied and will take us off at the beck of a finger." "Then we'll bide our time till Cook's business is finished here and he is anxious to be off to the Nor' Pole where he can freeze his bloomin' ears off."

The chief of this island was Orea, and it was obvious from the first that he did not care to share his power with a former commoner like Omai; but he was swayed by presents from our commander, by red feathers and hatchets, those magic articles of trade which spoke so eloquently. "If you will give me a piece of land," said Cook to him, "I will build a house for Omai, and I will leave tools with him and animals and garden seeds, and all your people will be the richer for it." "The land is not mine to give," Orea replied courteously. "It belongs to you, all of it, and you can give what you wish to Omai." "Ver' good!" Omai cried delightedly. "I will own all the land and give some of it to my friends." "You will do nothing as foolish as that," said the captain. "You will have no more land than you need."

And so Cook accepted a plot extending about two hundred yards along the shore and some three hundred yards inland, and our joiners set to work building a house of wood with as few nails as possible.

But when it was finished, Omai began to pout again. "I want a house upon a house!" he insisted, having in mind the two-story houses of London.

LOST EDEN 

"You will have this or nothing!" Cook replied. "And you had best mend your ways or your friends will not let you keep this long." "A cozy little place," Flint whispered. "A better home than

I thought to find for us in these islands." 

Planks had been sawn from the timber of the island. These formed the walls and floor. A roof of thatch appeared to be the best these islands had ever known. A door was swung upon wooden dowels in lieu of hinges, as were the shutters for the three windows. 

Many of our people admired the work as it progressed, and I knew that at least eleven planned to stop here. Most had found sweethearts to their liking, and, as usual, many Indians wished us to stay.

Always it seemed remarkable to me that these brown men so loved the whites. Most inconsistent with this feeling was their thieving from us, and usually the stolen articles were given to the chiefs, who were even more hospitable than the others. 

When Flint and I had determined that Chief Orea might conspire with us to stay here, we went to him and I took as a present about three yards of what we called cloth of gold but which was really made of base metal, and two shirts and a good knife, all highly prized articles. "That should buy our freedom," said Flint. "A proper mate y'are, and a jolly life we will have here." "Yes," I agreed. “Here we stay if we can."

Chief Orea, almost as fat as my own Tabourai, greeted us warmly. "My taios!" he exclaimed, rubbing noses with us. “Orea is happy. Orea ask many to stay here, and now ask you. Can you stay?"

It was as easy as that, and I doubt not that many others had talked with him of desertion. It appeared that we might have the company of white men in considerable number. "We will stay if you can keep our secret from Captain 

Cook," I said. "Yes. Yes. Very good." He donned a shirt, which could not be made to gird his ample waist but pleased him nonetheless, and he continued to feel the soft material of the tails while he talked.

LOST EDEN 

173 "We will sail away with Tabourai," I explained, "“far away to other islands, and hide until the ships are gone. Then we will return." "Good!" He laughed loudly and called for food, which seemed always to stand ready to his hand. 

I think he resented Omai's attitude and thought to gain ascendancy over him by helping us. It was obvious that Omai was not a chief over any white man, for he gave us no orders nor would we have thought of obeying his commands.

And so we readied Tabourai's canoe for a long voyage, and on the twenty-third of October, in the night, set forth upon our adventure. We went into a small cabin of thatch erected between the twin hulls, and twenty picked warriors pushed the boat into the surf and paddled it through the breakers. They set the big, triangular sail of matting without attracting undue attention from those on shore, and we glided swiftly out to sea. 

We flew with the wind, and soon I saw the island darken in the moonlight and grow small in the distance. "Good-by, my friends," I said as I watched the land fade into the night. I felt an odd twinge inside me at the thought of leaving all I had known of civilized life, perhaps never to return to England, and starting my life anew at this far end of the earth. 

But long ago I had made my decision, and now was the time to go. "And good riddance," Flint remarked as he leaned back peacefully and smoked his pipe. "A clean break it is. Me dream is come true."

In this manner we bobbed upon the sea all night, far away on the dark Pacific Ocean. At dawn we made out the island of 

Otaha and went ashore to stretch our legs. Flint spread his arms and smiled. "All ours," he proclaimed. "An ocean full of islands. A happy man I am this day." 

I too felt a new freedom, and the balmy breeze of the place stirred me deeply. It was the greatest of adventures, I thought. 

We had intended to continue eastward, where many islands lay, or so we were told, but a contrary wind prevailed, so that we could not have made much speed in that direction, and so we proceeded to Bolabola, and thence to the small island of Toobaee.

LOST EDEN 

The Indians here knew our taio well and received us with kindness, and so we decided to stop awhile with them and rest, keeping a sharp lookout for the sails of our ships. 

The chief of this place shared his meal with us, and all had plenty; but, feeling that we should have a greater feast, he immediately ordered his best dog to be killed and cooked.

When the dog was called to us for inspection before being butchered, our host smacked his lips and rubbed his enormous wen in approval, for he had eaten no more than four or five pounds of food and probably felt quite empty. 

An Indian closed the dog's throat with his strong hands and let it strangle to death, a proper preparation, they believed, to render their favorite dish more savory, and soon the animal was stowed piecemeal beneath the ground upon hot stones. 

Flint and I could not eat dog flesh, although many of our people did, and once at sea Captain Cook ate of one of the ship's dogs to cure his bilious colic. 

We stretched upon the finest and whitest sand I had ever seen, like white flour almost. Small crabs the size of acorns came out of little holes and peered at us. And when they saw they had naught to fear, they busied themselves with digging their homes deeper, bringing up armfuls of white, moist powder and flinging it from the holes.

A green-brown crab the size of my hat climbed a tree for a coconut and now and again a malolo, or flying fish, would break the surface of the sea and take to the air like a bird for the distance of a few yards. 

And as I gazed at that limitless blue, blue sea my heart was light indeed for the first time in many long months. I believed then that Cook would not much care if we left the ship, for he had not previously been angry with any who deserted. So far all had been returned, and their punishment had been light or, in the case of Flint, none at all.

But George Vancouver later, told me of Cook's wrath. "If we had not known him well, we might have feared for his sanity," said George. "Orea!" Cook had shouted to the chief of Huaheine, “you must find these deserters for us, or you will have much trouble

LOST EDEN 

175 on your hands. Come along in my boat. Now, what do you know of their plans?" "Oh, they tell me nothing," the chief answered artfully, "but I will help you hunt for them."

And so all the day Orea led the searchers from one place to another on the island of Huaheine, thus giving us a full day to outdistance our pursuers. But in the late afternoon Cook became suspicious of the Indians who directed him hither and yon, for they began to laugh at him, a most dangerous course to pursue. "Orea, you are lying!" Cook declared, and he fixed the chief with those terrifying, flashing eyes of his. "Lead me to these men or I will punish you and your people!"

With that vein of talk he soon convinced the chief that he must give some genuine help in the search. "They went to Otaha,” Orea said at last, telling a truth but only a part truth, for he knew we would sail on from there. "Then you will come with me to Otaha and give orders to seize them," Cook commanded, and with two boats they went directly to the middle of the east side of that island. 

Orea, our true friend, dispatched men to find us on Otaha, knowing that this further delay would assist us in escaping. A game like this always pleased the simple hearts of the Indians, and they had a pleasant day of being masters of the situation; but in the end 

Cook found a native of the island of Otaha who admitted that he had seen us and that we had sailed in the direction of Bolabola. 

Vancouver, who commanded one of the boatloads of marines, said that he thought Cook angry enough to shoot the chief dead on the spot, but the clever brain of our captain worked quickly and formed another plan. He brought Chief Orea back to the Resolution, and also his son, daughter, and son-in-law. "Now, Orea," said Cook, "your children are prisoners on my ship. Here they stay until you find the deserters. If you are not successful, I will carry your children away with me.” 

As easy as a chess game! In order to win, you place the king in check. "Your people," Cook explained, "have not only assisted these men in their escape but are at this very moment enticing others to follow them."

LOST EDEN 

Orea's daughter Poedooa began to weep and call out to her friends in the canoes alongside, "I am a prisoner! They will take me away and nevermore will I see you!"

Loyal subjects began to wail and sing sad songs and make loud exclamations. Often came the name of Poedooa in great sadness of tone, and the women seemed to vie with one another in mourning her fate with more significant expressions of their grief than tears and cries, there being many heads bloodied by the striking of their little shark-tooth implements against their faces. 

Orea did not give way to unavailing lamentations, but fearfully dispatched a canoe of warriors to Bolabola with a message to Opoony, the sovereign of that island, requesting him to seize us and return us to Captain Cook. "Remember this," said Cook to the Indian warriors as they started away. "You must not come back without the deserters. Tell 

Opoony for me that if the men have left Bolabola he must send canoes after them, for I suspect that they will not remain long in one place."

The canoe put off, but the natives on the island did not have much faith in the success of this mission, and in order to rescue the family of the chief from the Resolution, they prepared for war upon our people. 

First they planned to capture Captain Cook and hold him until their own people were freed. 

They lay in wait for him at a little stream of fresh water where he bathed every evening. Often he went alone to this place and without arms. He was such a capable leader, however, that he foresaw this danger and did not go that night to bathe. 

But when Captain Clerke and Mr. Gore went ashore, the 

Indians made plans to seize them instead, and quickly formed an ambush, assembling all available warriors, armed with spears and clubs and stones.

It was at this moment that a girl from Maitea came swimming out to the Resolution with those long overhand strokes which the natives use so well. One of our officers had brought her in the ship with him, and her loyalty proved to be most valuable to Cook, for she told of the ambush and of the hostile spirit of the warriors. 

At the moment of hearing this news, about six in the eve-

LOST EDEN 

177 ning, all the canoes in the bay began to disperse at once, as if a sudden panic had seized those who manned them. 

Our people armed immediately, and a strong party under the command of Mr. King was sent to rescue our two gentlemen who were in danger on the shore. At the same time two armed boats and a party under Mr. Williamson went after the fleeing canoes, to cut off their retreat.

Several shots were fired over the canoes, which caused them to check their speed. And facing the muzzles of so many muskets, the Indians soon were reconciled. 

Captain Clerke and Mr. Gore had not yet been seized and they were allowed to return without harm. 

But the hostile attitude of the Indians persisted, and so, unknowingly, Flint and I had precipitated a state of war, and death faced our friends on the ships as well as our friends ashore, so strongly was our captain resolved to get us back.

We had left the night of the twenty-third of October, and three days later, on the twenty-sixth, we feasted with our new friends far away on the island of Toobaee. Canoes were coming and going through the surf, and we paid little attention to one which brought eight strong men. All carried clubs or spears, and they came directly toward us. "I am father of Pootoe, who is married to Chief Orea's daughter," said an elderly giant, "and Opoony, who is king of

Bolabola, sends me a message that my son is a prisoner on one of your ships." Flint.

He struggled, and I think I also struggled a little, but the hands of those big brown men held us tight. I knew the tune was finished and now we would have to pay the piper. "We be friends?" the spokesman asked, and all smiled but none relaxed his grip. "We not fight. We just go home."

LOST EDEN 

And home we went, as peaceful as any pair of doves, all because of the clever brain of Captain Cook, who could stand on his deck and stretch a long arm to a faraway island and pick us neatly off the beach. "Damme!" Flint roared. "What bloody rotten luck!”

When we came into the bay at Huaheine, Cook was making ready to sail to Bolabola in search of us, taking his weeping hostages with him.

As soon as Poedooa caught sight of our canoe, with her father-in-law standing proudly in the stern, she took to weeping. 

Chief Orea also wept, but part of his sadness stemmed from his disappointment in not being able to aid us or other of our people in escaping so that we could make our home with him. 

Cook gave us the merest glance. He was in the midst of parting with Omai, and I am sure his sorrow was mingled with pleasure at being rid of the troublesome first ambassador of Polynesia to the Court of St. James. 

Omai wept copiously. "My good frien' Tute," he sobbed. "My good frien' King 

George will never see me again. My good frien' Sand'ich. I will go back to them if ever another ship comes here to take me.” "God bless you, Omai,” said Cook less warmly. “And now over the side with you. Good-by. Good-by." "Good-by."

We then weighed and sailed. Scores of canoes followed us out of the bay. Many of the Indians sang sad songs like dirges and shed tears, even those who had been at war with us but an hour before. And I think we responded to the last man, in some measure, to the genuine warmth of friendship and the sadness of this parting.

As our sails began to draw, we left the canoes behind, and 

Cook called all hands on deck. "Midshipman Westermark," he began, "you are hereby reduced to the rank of ordinary seaman and you will report to Mr. 

Bligh for duty as keeper of the pigs. You will swing your hammock above them and you will clean their stalls every day or oftener if need be. Remove your uniform. Winston, bring some slops for 

Westermark to wear."

LOST EDEN 

179 I stood amazed for a moment, because Cook had not dealt so severely with desertion before, and he had met with it many times and in many forms. But I did not know then that Flint and 

I had precipitated a state of war and that our captain's anger had been with him for four days.

Scarcely believing, I undressed there and donned some illfitting breeches and a cotton shirt and stood with the sailors. "Edward Flint," said Cook, "you are to receive two dozen lashes." 

A flogging for old Flint! It did not seem possible, but they stripped off his shirt and seized him up. 

I think never did a bosun have a more difficult chore, for he as well as the rest of us had grown fond of Flint and his salty yarns and his willingness ever to use his big, capable hands to pull on a line or to go aloft in a storm when others faltered.

A stout fellow he was, but age had begun to show upon him, and the cat took so much out of him that never again was he the same. "Flogging makes a bad man worse," Vancouver observed, "and it breaks the spirit of a good one." 

As the flails fell I hoped that Flint would soon faint, but he bore all twenty-four lashes with no more show of pain than a whistling of his breath through his teeth and a writhing of the heavy muscles of his back, which tore apart and bled profusely. 

As to my lot, I found that Society Island pigs smelled no sweeter than English pigs. We had set most of our other troublesome animals ashore, but swine we now had in all the stalls, to be carried as long as they maintained their health and to be eaten sparingly in order to extend our supply of fresh meat as far as possible.

My chores were enough to cast a continued gloom over my days and nights, but worse punishment than this was the loss of my captain's friendship and company and that of the other officers.

I could almost wish that I had not been taken alive, and I resolved to desert again at the first likely place, even though it be a lonely shore near the Arctic ice. 

A sad day that was when we left those charming islands

astern. Never had I heard such grumbling on the Resolution, and there was much covert talk about mutiny. "Put the men to work!" said Lieutenant Bligh to the bosun. “What a dismal lot of wastrels! I will have neither long faces nor idle hands about me." "There is naught for them to do, sir," the bosun answered. "We rode so long at anchor that all's in shipshape. The odd jobs are done and there is no work to do." "Then do some of the work over again." “Aye, aye, sir,” he answered, but he began to go about the deck like a lost soul, for he could not think of any work which might be at all useful. "Bosun," Bligh cried in impatience, “have each idle man turn out his gear and swab the bulkheads and decks with vinegar and smoke them with gunpowder. Another dosing will do no harm."
