And since the airs are light, put some others in the longboat and see that they tow us with a will. Let them bend their backs at the sweeps until they are too tired to complain. They will sleep the better for't."

In the evening I contrived to go to Flint, who lay face down in his hammock. "Ed," I said, "we must not be taken the next time.”

But he did not share my enthusiasm for another try. "I am beat," he murmured. "They gave me the cat, they did. Old Flint that's worked his life out for the King Old 

Flint, he gets the cat at the end of his life, he does." "Is there aught I can do?" ... “No, lad. No . . . no.” His voice trailed off and at last he fell into a fitful sleep.

A freshening breeze made the blocks squeak and the timbers groan as we reached for the icy Northern Passage, and I went forward and crawled into my hammock above the pigs.

Chapter 26 

ON THE EIGHTEENTH OF JANUARY, SOME THREE MONTHS AFTER 

leaving Tahiti, I heard the cry of "Land!" and, looking from the pigsties, saw a high island, which could mean fresh water at last, and food. It was the first of what Captain Cook was to call the 

Sandwich Islands, in honor of his benefactor, the first Lord of the Admiralty.

Now known as Owahoo or Oahu, this island had upon it the populous village of Honolulu. But to our disappointment, this beckoning shore appeared too far to windward to interest our captain more than to estimate its position for his log. He was not of a mind to waste time sailing slowly against the wind to a land of unknown value, and he knew also that with such a stiff breeze blowing and with such a foul bottom, the ship might not fetch the land at all.

Accordingly, we took the fresh, reliable wind on our starboard quarter and sailed steadily northeast. In this direction we soon saw another island, which we came up with at noon of the nineteenth. Like the other, it also stood high out of the water. "Very green. Very interesting," said Dr. Anderson, looking through his glass. "And there is a village! John, we may have some fun ashore." "It is forbidden that I talk to officers," I said with much regret, but Cook's eyes were upon us, and I was reminded that I was but an ordinary seaman and keeper of the pigs. "Aye, something should be done about that. But there is no harm in listening to me talk to myself. Now what I am thinking aloud is that these islands lie about as far from the equator as the Society Islands, these to the north and the others to the south. 

And I am wondering if that gorgeous climate of Tahiti may not be 181

LOST EDEN 

duplicated here. If it is... by Jove! We have made a find!" 

And I think he must have talked with the captain, for Cook surprised me by calling me into his quarters. He had scarce spoken to me in all the weeks since Flint and I had deserted and thrown our people into a state of war. 

He stood erect, towering above me, for none of us was tall enough to meet the level of his piercing eyes.

I had a good look at the scar on his hand, and it did appear that it might have been made by his own knife, driven into a mast after a fight in the days when he was a forecastle hand. He had lived hard and seen much, this resolute Englishman who left no doubt as to who commanded here during every second of the long years ticked off by the Hanley watch. "If this land provides proper refreshment," he said, “we will eat the last of our sickly hogs or salt them down, and then we will not need a keeper for them." 

Slowly a smile came to his lips. "Yes sir," I answered, nothing better coming into my head. "Has your work been hard?" "It has, sir." "There will be work for an artist if we go ashore. I think you had best put on your uniform again and turn to." "Thank you, sir. I am very grateful, sir." My heart bounded. "But remember: I will fight a war, if need be, to get you back aboard, and all the others. That you must know by now." "I do indeed, sir," I answered, but I made no promises concerning deserting, for already I was hoping that I might stay ashore here. "Very good, Mr. Westermark.” Cook smiled broadly.

And so I was no longer a forecastleman but again a midshipman, and I rated the salutation of Mister. I shook the roaches out of my silks and broadcloth, and after some airing and mending I once more stood with the officers and watched the land come up out of the sea.

Soon we were coasting this lush green island, looking for a likely landing place, and upon seeing some canoes put off from shore, Cook immediately brought to and we waited to see what manner of men we had found.

LOST EDEN 

183 The canoes contained three to six men each and were of the usual outrigger type. To our surprise, the natives addressed us in a tongue almost identical with that of the Society Islands. Similarly, they wore the tapa malo or patterned loincloth. "We are friends," we shouted. "Come closer. Come aboard. 

Can we get fresh water here? Have you any drinking nuts? What place is this?"

They paddled alongside, but no entreaties could get them to come aboard at first. They were of a brown color and, though of ordinary size, were stoutly made. The hair of most of them was cropped short, but some wore it flowing loose and a few had tied it in a bunch on the crown of the head. In all it seemed naturally black, but most had stained it, as is the practice of the Friendly Islanders, with some stuff which gave it a brown or burned color. 

In general they wore beards, but some were shaved, presumably by the usual method of two shells used as scissors, for they had no metal with them nor indeed any weapons save stones, which they seemed to have brought for their protection, as they threw them overboard soon after we began to converse in their language. "Atooi" seemed to be the name of the place, but the first letter of the word soon appeared to have no more meaning than the O which often is placed before the word Tahiti to make it Otahiti. "Kauai" might be a better way to represent the word they spoke, for the sailors laughingly took it up at once, thinking it to be spelled "Cow Eye."

Cook gave these Indians some of the brass medals which had been struck for him in England, and which he distributed freely in unknown lands, hoping that they might be instrumental someday in proving that he was first to see these lands and that they belonged to England by right of discovery. 

He tied the medals to a rope and let it down to one of the canoes, and in return the brown men tied some fish like mackerel to the rope as an equivalent gift. One offered his only garment, a small piece of tapa cloth stained with red, black, and yellow. 

Seeing no signs of an anchorage on the eastern extremity of the island, Cook bore away to leeward and ranged the southeast

LOST EDEN 

shore at a distance of half a league from the surf, and soon other natives came off with produce almost identical with that of Tahiti. 

A canoe full of small pigs could be purchased for a fourpenny nail or, indeed, almost anything else we chose to offer. A land of plenty it was, and of great generosity.

At length the native men began to come aboard, expressing wonderment at our ship and the things in it. They appeared to be awe-stricken, and each would lay a lock of his hair upon the deck and mutter a prayer. "Lono" or "Arono" was a word often mentioned, and later I learned that it was the name of one of their four most important gods. He had lived among them long ago, or so they were told by their priests, and had killed his wife in a jealous rage. In remorse, he had gone about the islands wrestling with all who would oppose him. Then he had gone away but had promised to return upon a floating island, bearing swine. They believed that Cook was this god, at last come back to honor their island with his presence and beneficence.

But Cook did not know of these matters. His chief concern was for fresh water. "Wai? Wai?" he would say, pronouncing their word for water and making the motions of drinking. 

In answer they pointed always to the west, and so we continued in that direction.

The sailors called out a different word. "Wahine? Wahine?" they shouted, meaning, "Woman? Woman?"

As yet we had seen no women, but soon the most hopeful among us was rewarded by the sight of a large canoe carrying six of the loveliest brown girls one might imagine. 

These charming creatures stood up in the canoe, entirely naked, and sang a pleasant but somewhat melancholy song. They danced by moving their hips rhythmically while their torsos remained almost motionless. It was as though their hips were hung upon a swivel. 

They gestured most beautifully with their hands and fingers, and they finished their song to the accompaniment of a lively slapping of their breasts in unison. The melody rose and ended in a high pitch of beauty and emotion.

LOST EDEN 

185 There was much yearning and muttering among our people. "Here we stay for sure this time," I heard one say, and others: "That young wench on the left end for me! Who is for the ice islands again? Our last chance, mates."

Other women and men came out to us, all vigorous, active, and most expert swimmers. Leaving their canoes upon the most trifling pretext, they dived under them and swam to others at some distance, seeming never to grow tired. 

It was amusing to see women with infants at the breast leap overboard and, without endangering their little ones, swim through heavy breakers to the shore.

These Indians seemed to be blessed with a frank, cheerful disposition, and except for their propensity to thieving, which seems innate in most of the people we visited in this ocean, they were exceedingly friendly toward us and on all occasions appeared to be deeply affected with a consciousness of their own inferiority. 

Cook was much impressed. "It was a pleasure," he wrote in the third of his books, which I read years later, "to see with how much affection the women managed their infants and how readily the men lent their assistance to such a tender office, thus sufficiently distinguishing themselves from those savages who esteem a wife and a child as things rather necessary than desirable or worthy of their notice." 

After coasting some thirty or forty leagues, we came to a bay called Waimea, and here we anchored. And when it became known that Cook would go ashore, a crowd of several hundred assembled.

I went in the pinnace with him and the other officers, and we met with a most fantastic reception. As we approached the land an elderly priest began a kind of prayerful chant similar to those uttered by natives who had come aboard and, like them, he frequently shouted, "Lono!"

The priest held up a small scepter or wand covered with the hair of a dog, which appeared to be the nearest thing to fur that these people knew, and in their eyes it may well have been as impressive as the ermine on King George's robe. 

Certainly there was much significance in the priest's gestures, for the very instant Captain Cook leaped upon the shore the whole multitude fell flat upon the ground.

Chapter 27 

THE BROWN PEOPLE OF KAUAI REMAINED PRONE BEFORE THE 

stately, elegantly dressed Captain Cook until, by expressive signs, he prevailed upon them to arise. 

I think he still did not know that he was believed to be a god. However, he was of a mind always to act as he deemed best and to let others think as they chose. The wildness of the looks and gestures of the natives expressed their ignorance of everything they saw and suggested that, until now, they never had been visited by Europeans.

Our people, almost to the last man, were intent upon finding sweethearts, and I will confess that this matter was not farthest from my thoughts.

At first we saw only the common women and not those of the ruling class. But these commoners in general possessed more charm than those of any other island we had visited. Few were dark-skinned. Many had complexions light enough to show the pink bloom of health in their cheeks.

We walked among them, looking into their eyes, as men will do everywhere, knowing that the soul of a woman shows in her eyes. And always there was the hope that some spark or some inner flame would be revealed at a glance, and that here would be found a mate with the charm which haunts the dreams of men awake and asleep.

The women wore skirts called pa-us, which were prettier and more elaborately designed than the malos of the men and usually larger, about a yard wide and three or four yards long, wrapped about them like skirts. This was their only covering for 186

LOST EDEN 

187 the most part, but, sitting in the cool of the shadows, some wore a kind of cape or shawl over their shoulders. 

A colorful lot they were, and a feast for the eyes of lonely sailors. In those primitive garments all the hues of the rainbow seemed to be represented, mostly in delicate tints. 

And for many of our company these charms were sufficient to distract them from the feast being prepared and even from the thought of clear, fresh water which spread out before us in an enchanting lake bordered with tropical verdure. 

As it was in Tahiti, here a man or woman might have as many wives or sweethearts as desired, and charming women vied for the favors of our men, even to the ugliest. Weber, who had but one eye, could still manage a woman on each arm, and Armstrong of the spiky red beard gathered four unto himself. "Ah, look at that brilliant red cape on the man coming yonder!" Dr. Anderson exclaimed, with intention, no doubt, of helping me to keep my feet upon the ground in this atmosphere of intoxicating beauty and feminine cordiality. "It shimmers in the sunlight like red gold. No fabric of our making ever had the lively color of that.”

The scintillating brilliance of the garment truly was astonishing. The man who wore it was a chief, or alii, and he approached in a dignified manner with a retinue of servants. We were greeted by the usual pressing of noses against cheeks and fell at once to an examination of his raiment.

It was made of feathers. Such small, soft feathers were used, and they were so expertly laid upon one another like shingles, that they presented a surface like the finest velvet, but softer and more brilliant.

The cloak fell loosely from his shoulders to his knees, and on his head the chief wore a kind of Grecian helmet of the same material, fitting close upon his head but with a high crest. He passed on to greet Captain Cook, and soon others came with longer or shorter cloaks. Many had simple but striking designs worked in black, red, and white.

We discovered that the choicest of these garments were made from the feathers of the o-o bird, but those of the iwi were more common. These feathers were gathered just before the molt-

LOST EDEN 

ing season, and in order not to destroy the birds, the hunters caught them by placing sticky bird lime or resinous gum upon the trees deep in the forest. After carefully plucking the feathers, the 

Indians would release the birds to grow more of their brilliant plumage and to enliven the forests with their song. 

The o-o bird had a body of brilliant black and under each wing a tuft of beautiful yellow feathers. The bright red iwi flew about the woods in great abundance. 

No less remarkable than the feathers was the netting into which they were woven. It was made of the fiber of the olona plant, one of the nettle family, and the fine, strong strands we examined seemed to be as good as anything of the kind which 

England could produce.

We feasted that day as we had in Tahiti and almost in the same manner, with similar foods cooked in underground ovens. I thought their sugar cane the sweetest and their sweet potatoes the tastiest I had ever encountered. There was pork, chicken, and also dog for those who desired it. The seaweed called limu was good, and they gave us a delicious confection prepared of ti root by baking it beneath the ground for two days. 

And this was another night like our first in Tahiti, but more enjoyable because of the greater friendliness of the Indians, at which I marveled, for I did not know until the next day that all of us were considered to be gods, or at least demigods of high rank. 

I slept well that night in spite of the hilarity and lovemaking in most quarters of the ship, and my rheumatism and scurvy seemed to pain me less because of the thought that here I would make myself well again. Also visions of adventure in these islands opened pleasant vistas and lulled me to sleep.

It was well after sunrise the next morning when I opened my eyes to gaze at this charming land framed by the port of my room. No brighter blue for sea and sky, no deeper green of land could ever exist, I knew, and up the valley a little rainbow added the last fantastic touch of a painter's dream. 

Dr. Anderson shared my enthusiasm. "You must paint the feather cloaks of the chiefs and the colorful skirts of the women," he suggested.

LOST EDEN 

189 "It will take all the pigments in my box and more. I can't half do justice." 

"Nor I, with the feeble words I write. A fantastic land!"

We were ashore before Captain Cook that morning, and were met by the elderly man who had conducted the ceremonies attendant upon our landing the day before. His name was Kahele and his office that of high priest, a most powerful man in the community and as wise and charming as he was powerful. 

As he seemed most solicitous of our welfare, I asked him to arrange some of his people for a picture, seating near me the women with the most colorful tapa skirts and three chiefs with excellent cloaks of feathers. "What do you call the cloak?" I asked. “Ahuula,” he replied, which word means no more than “red cloak." But in New Zealand it is spelled kahukura, and it is the name of the god who is supposed to be seen in the rainbow. "Is this cloak to honor the god of the rainbow?" I asked. "Yes," Kahele replied. "When you see the rainbow, this god is present or coming soon. The cloak is made to look like a rainbow because all who wear it are high chiefs and they are sacred and are related to the gods."

Then here also the people believed in the divine right of rulers, even as the Europeans, and I was beginning to understand their prostration before us, for in our fancy clothes we must have appeared to be great god-chiefs of another land.

As I painted, with much pleasure in the riot of color before me, Cook came ashore, and again all the Indians fell prone before him.

A handsome figure he made, tall and full of spirit in spite of the rheumatism which gave to his firm stride a slight limp. His powdered white wig contrasted sharply with the black hair of the natives and with that which had been dyed reddish brown. He chose to wear a red coat, but its brilliance was shamed by the red feather dress of the native chiefs. However, his claim to elegance was established by his gold braid and by the extravagant cascades of Belgian lace at his cuffs.

He bade the Indians arise from the ground, impatiently, as one who desired no such homage and was anxious to savor of the

LOST EDEN 

delights of this enchanting island as well as to be about the business of his King and seek what he could find. "Westermark," he called as he approached, "you can paint that picture another day. Come with me to explore the white obelisk yonder. We must have your record of it on canvas. And, Dr. Anderson, will you come along to describe it with your pen?" "Yes indeed, sir,” the doctor answered. "England will have new marvels to talk about."

The obelisk was one of many we had seen from the Resolution as we sailed along the coast. This one stood about fifty feet high, in a beautiful setting beside the lake. 

For a guide Cook chose the friendly Kahele, and a good choice it was, for this man had been a chief before he had become a high priest of the island. His name, Kahele, means in their language "the goer," and I soon acquired a genuine fondness for him and he for me.

A large retinue followed us, and we were joined by many others who fell upon the ground at our approach. Kahele often held up his fur-covered wand and uttered a prayer and pronounced the name of Lono.

At every pause in our walk a man with a small pig under each arm would walk solemnly around us in a circle. "This is the most interesting place I have visited in this ocean," said Cook, "and the perfume from these flowering shrubs and trees is beyond man's imagination. Dr. Anderson, you will do well to describe this accurately for the people at home." "Aye, sir, were that possible," the doctor replied.

There seems to be little doubt that we were the first Europeans to come here. If the Spaniards knew of these islands, they doubtless would have used them for refreshment in their annual sailing from Acapulco to Manila, because there is no other known land between those ports but Guam. Here they would have found plenty, and they would have been within a month's sail of the ports of California. How happy Lord Anson would have been, and what hardships he would have avoided, had he known that there was a group of islands halfway between America and Tinian. asked. "Do you feel like an explorer by now, Westermark?" Cook

LOST EDEN 

191 "I do, sir," I replied. "It is past all dreaming." "You will be well wenched before nightfall," he smiled, "unless you can run faster than I think you can." "My heart is still my own," I ventured, but I did not know what was in store for me at the obelisk.

We found this structure to mark a center of religious activity, but the obelisk itself had neither art nor permanence. About ten feet square at the base, it tapered toward the top, a good fifty feet above, and was composed of small poles, interwoven with twigs and branches, thus forming an indifferent wickerwork, hollow within from top to bottom. 

This was a place for prayer and meditation. Here a priest would commune with the gods and receive their commands, as from an oracle. We found it in a rather ruinous state, but originally it had been covered neatly with the thin, light gray cloth called hoho, which these people seem to consecrate to religious purposes. More like paper than cloth, this material is made here, as in other islands, by beating bark until it spreads out to the desired thickness. 

In the vicinity of the obelisk stood several strange figures carved of wood, many with terrorizing faces, and all with their loins covered by pieces of hoho. There was a house or temple some forty feet long and ten broad, built of poles and thatch, and mostly open at the sides.

Cook left me alone to work while he continued his explorations, and I was not disturbed, because none of the Indians would enter this hallowed ground. On a platform at one corner of the compound, which they called a morai, were some plantains which appeared to be offerings to the gods. Opposite the entrance were two wooden images, cut out of one piece, with pedestals, in all about three feet high, indifferently executed, which were said to be goddesses. It appeared that some of their objects of worship then were females, although no living women were allowed in these places.

Other carved images must have been designed to frighten prowlers, they were so hideous. One glared at me with large eyes of pearl shell.

I finished with the idols and was searching about for an-

LOST EDEN 

other view of the morai when I became conscious of a figure in white, sitting in the shade of a giant koa tree, just outside the stone wall of the temple.

It was a girl. Her large brown eyes were upon me, watching every move I made. She may have been there for some time while my canvas came to life with pigments unknown in this part of the world. Often, when I painted, the spell of a magician seemed to be cast upon my audience, and I could see now that the wonders of my paints and brushes had scored again.

This girl who watched me so intently had dressed herself differently from the other women I had seen. A cape surmounted the usual skirt, and both garments were of the purest white. Over her left ear she had placed a bloom of red hibiscus. This, I learned later, was to signify that she was amenable to courtship should an acceptable male come her way.

When she saw my eyes upon her, she dropped her glance and took up a native mirror, made of half a coconut shell blackened inside with fire and filled with water. The surface of the water supplied quite a good reflection, and with the aid of it she began to arrange her hair and the hibiscus flower above her ear.

Chapter 28 

FILLING MY PIPE WITH TOBACCO, I WALKED TOWARD THIS LOVELY 

apparition, and when I reached the edge of the hallowed ground she sprang to her feet with a smile of warm friendliness mingled with wonder. "Aloha nui," I said, which means something like "very large greetings." 

I doffed my three-cornered hat. "Oh!" she exclaimed. "Your moon comes off." "My moon?" "I had thought your head was shaped like a crescent because you came from the moon." "It is only a covering for my head," I replied, but she did not fully understand, because they had neither hats nor names for hats.

I struck a match to light my pipe, and she sprang back from the sputtering sulphur. And when she saw smoke issue from my mouth, a look of terror came into her pretty face. "Auwe!" she cried. "You are afire!"

Quickly she seized her coconut mirror and threw its contents of water full in my face. laughed loudly, and when she saw that I was unhurt from the fire and smoke, she also laughed. "It is harmless," I explained, and, drawing deeply on my pipe, I blew a large ring of smoke. "Ahhh!" she sighed. “I understand. You help Lono to make the clouds in heaven, the bending clouds and the round clouds. 

Very wonderful." 193

LOST EDEN 

"It is only a smoke ring," I said. "Most anyone can blow a smoke ring." "More! Make more clouds."

And so I continued to make rings and to watch the sparkle of her eyes, which was more wondrous than all the clouds in heaven. "We saw some of your people eating large pieces of red flesh," she said. "Were you eating your enemies?"

This I did not understand until much later. The "flesh" turned out to be some melons which remained of our supply gathered at Tahiti. "No. I do not eat my enemies," I told her. "That is good," she replied. "My fathers do not eat their enemies. Do you spit brown?"

This was easier to understand. She referred to the chewing of tobacco. "No," I said. "I have no magic like that for you."

By now we were laughing together, and I thought her the loveliest vision a man ever could behold in all these islands. There seemed to be an understanding between us, although literally we were a world apart in our background. "I want to see the magic things you carry inside your skin," she said, and she reached her hand into my breeches pocket and drew out my clasp knife. "Oooooh! It really is true. You carry things inside your skin!"

How strange we must have seemed to these people. They had never seen woven cloth or sewed garments, and so at first they believed that our breeches and shirts were loose folds of our white skin. "No," I said. "This is not my skin. It is only a covering. I am made the same as you.” 

I drew back my sleeve to show that it was only cloth. "Oh, very fine tapa!" she exclaimed, but what seemed to please her most was that she found me to be the same as she. 

With that she came close and pressed her nose against my cheek in their usual method of greeting. "Aloha," she whispered, and because that word has many

LOST EDEN 

195 meanings, such as "good morrow" and "good-by," I did not at once understand that this charming creature in her frankness meant, "I love you."

But when I looked into the depths of her wide brown eyes I know that here, at the ends of the earth, I had found a girl whom 

I fervently wanted. "Would you like to learn our custom of greeting when we say aloha to a lovely lass?" I asked, and she was willing, so I kissed her. "Oh!" she cried. "It is much better that way. What do you call this greeting?" "A kiss." "Keete," she repeated as best she could, having not the sound of "s" in her language. "No. Kiss. Kee-ssss." I prolonged the hissing sound and showed her that it came through my teeth. "Keeee-ssssss!" She laughed. "And why not?" I said, repeating the embrace, and I thought to claim her then and there for my own, but she drew away, suddenly fearful. "You have no women in your big family," she said with a puzzled look, "but you act like a man. Come, we will go where there is dancing, and you will learn about us of the islands. Perhaps you will be displeased with us, but I hope not." 

She laughed again and skipped along merrily in front of me, while I wrestled awkwardly with my paints and easel, for my head was in a whirl. I thought perhaps I had lost her in my haste to possess her, but soon I learned that love-making here was carried to a high art, and like all women everywhere, she longed for singing and dancing and would surrender her heart only when filled with the delights of courting. 

We had not far to go before we saw a dance, for our people had quickly wandered inland in search of such pleasures. 

We heard bursts of laughter coming from a large group of tapa-clad brown people and the beating of those little drums made by stretching a skin across a half coconut shell. Rhythm also was produced by a row of men seated upon the ground with large

LOST EDEN 

gourds, more than a foot long, which they beat with their hands and pounded upon the ground, the seeds inside making a pleasant rattle. As usual, they chanted, now soft, now loud. 

As we made our way to them, eight comely maidens emerged from the crowd and stood in two rows facing each other and began a dance such as one might not come across in a whole lifetime, even in the water-front houses of Liverpool. 

The girls wore leis, or necklaces of flowers, their only other garments being girdles of leaves and grass loosely put together and hanging down only a few inches.

Their movements and gestures appeared wanton in the extreme. The Indians laughed and slapped each other on the back and cheered the dancers on to such emotion that perspiration stood upon their bodies. "Ahoy there, Westermark!" called Webb, who sat with 

Gibson, laughing and cheering, each with an arm around a girl. "Just like Tahiti, eh?” 1 

And so it was. Here, unashamed in the balmy sunshine, were paraded such variety of sybaritic pleasures that few of our company, I am sure, could add to them, although I think we had among us some of the most profligate of earth. 

Here Nature was stripped of all shams. Here were observed all the impulses of the human animal which had contributed to its growth and multiplication, aye, and to its destruction. 

My companion watched me closely, and I think she was glad to see that this show of the commoners embarrassed me. She did not applaud until two graceful girls in long skirts danced with most expressive movements of their hands and arms, and when she noticed that I also was charmed by the beauty of this semireligious exercise, she took my hand in hers. "It is time to bathe," she said.

I smiled, because apparently it was always time to bathe in this land. All seemed to plunge into the water several times a day, preferring fresh pools to the surf unless it ran unusually high. 

She led me along a little path and over a hill, and when we passed under a koa tree I caught my breath at the beauty of the scene before us.

A small, shady pool had been reserved for royalty, as well

LOST EDEN 

197 it might, for its cool, clear water reflected a riot of flowering trees which stood upon its banks. It mirrored a patch of blue sky with fleecy white clouds voyaging slowly across it. And at the far end a high waterfall tumbled riotously down and broke into spray which caught the glint of the mild sun to form a perfect rainbow. "The god of the rainbow must come here to bathe," I said. "Yes, he is here now," she replied with conviction.

With a quick gesture she disrobed, ran to a ledge, and dived into the pool. Making only a slight splash, she went far down, and 

I could see her graceful form moving about under the water like a lovely mermaid.

I soon followed, with much less grace, I am sure. We swam to the rainbow in the falls. We laughed. We splashed.

I found floating near me the red hibiscus which she had worn in her hair. I caught it up and placed it over one ear in fun, not knowing that by this sign I showed that I also had courtship in mind.

She laughed and came to me. She took my face in her two hands and looked into my eyes. Then she pushed my head far under water, so far that I thought I could not again reach the surface without strangling.

When finally I did come up, she was nowhere in sight. "Ho, there!" I called. "Where are you?" 

I heard her merry laughter and then saw her peering from behind a kind of lily plant which bore leaves almost half as large as she.

When I went to her she laughed again, ran a little way, and threw herself upon a grassy place to dry. I followed, and found the lush growth warm and pleasant and dotted with little white starry flowers and a few of brilliant blue. 

With a devastating smile she turned her face to mine. "Keeee-ssss?" she said softly.

That was her name for a ship, and it still is the word these people use. "It is forbidden to take women away with us," I told her. “Ah yes, tabu, like our temples here," she sighed. “I had hoped it would be otherwise. Then tell me, can you stay here and let me make your house for you?" wife." "That," I agreed, "is a very good idea. I want you for my "Oh, very good! And will I be your favorite wife?" she asked, much concerned. "You will be my only wife." "The only one? Have you no others?” "No, I am not married." "It is very strange, but very nice. Sometimes we have only one wife, only one husband, and when that happens it is because we love much, and that is a blessing of the gods.' "I think so too," I agreed, and as I looked at her loveliness there beside that charming pool, I had no doubt of it. "You will be a very high chief and have many servants to feed you. My father will honor you. He was a great chief first, and now he is the priest Kahele." 198

LOST EDEN 

199 "Kahele? The old man who commands your people to fall upon the ground when Lono approaches?" "Yes, Kahele. He is very powerful. He talks to the gods in the temples and tells our people what the gods say to him. You will stay?" "I must make a plan," I explained. “Do you think you could hide me until my island floats away?" "Yes! Yes! We have many secret places known only to the gods of these islands. Even Lono does not know of them. These places have been made since he last was here." "Was he here before?" 

She laughed. "You must know that. Were you not with him in the long, long ago when he promised to return?" "No, not that I can recall." "I suppose you go so many places you do not remember, but Lono does. That is why he came at the time of the Lono festival, as he said he would."

There was indeed a festival for Lono now in its fourth month, and this coincidence accounts in large part for the fantastic welcome we received here.

Once a year an image of Lono, the swine god, was paraded around the island for the purpose of collecting taxes of coconuts and hogs and tapa cloth, and we had landed at the very time that this procession was completing its circuit. "See!" She pointed to the first of the cavalcade, which I had been watching for several minutes as it came into view from beyond a hill. "They will be very glad to see Lono himself come to honor them."

I saw this colorful troupe file along a trail which had been worn smooth by many generations of bare feet. All carried heavy burdens of coconuts, yams, plantains, pigs, or cloth. "Will they give some of this food to Lono?" I asked, beginning to feel very hungry. "Yes. All of it, if he will accept it. It is a wonderful day on this island, a day we prayed for always but never hoped to see in our lifetime." "What are the men in front carrying?" "It is an image which honors Lono. You should know that."

LOST EDEN 

The object they carried was shaped very much like a mast and sail. It consisted of a long pole topped by a small carved figure which had a crosspiece like a yard tied to its neck, and from this piece hung a white banner for all the world like a spritsail, although somewhat longer.

Upon these primitive people our huge sails must indeed have had a profound effect, and the tall, elegantly dressed Captain Cook most likely overwhelmed them when he walked upon their land. "I am not a god," I said, thinking that the truth had better come from me at once, so that we would have no misunderstanding. "You must be one of the lesser gods, anyway," she insisted. "You have the red of the sun in your face and the white of the moon on the skin of your body, and every day I have prayed to the sun and the moon since I was a small girl. Now you have answered my prayers and I am the happiest of women."

I was beginning to understand that when these people claimed divinity for their men it was perhaps no more than seeing gods in flowers and trees, which indeed was not an uncommon practice among our own poets. And since she insisted upon some kind of deification, I thought I might not be too blasphemous if

I classed myself with some of the lesser saints, like St. Patrick or maybe St. Vitus. "Perhaps I am a little one," I agreed, "but a very little one." "What is your name?” she asked. "John." "Hono," she repeated after me, pronouncing the name as best she could in her language, which has but twelve letters as against our twenty-six. Hono means a sheltered place beside the sea, a cave, a snug harbor protected from the wind. "Hono comes with Lono. Hono will make a shelter from the wind of Lono.” "That I will, if ever I can!" One of Lono's many powers,

I believe, was control of the winds, and indeed I would now get to leeward of his blasts if I could. "And what is your name?" "It is Iolana." That word conveyed the idea of floating in the air, as a bird, as a lovely cloud. "A good name for you, darling. Iolana!"

LOST EDEN 

201 

How well my visit to Paradise began! What an answer to an artist's dream! "We will ask your father to say the words of marriage we use in my land, and we will promise to love only each other as long as we live." 

She looked at me unbelievingly. "Now," she whispered, “I know we are blessed of the gods." "Yes, we are blessed of the gods, and I am sure of that.”

The sun being now low in the western sky, I thought I had best return to the ship. And so, with my arm about my Iolana, I strolled along the flower-scented path to the shore, and I told her good night as the sun crimsoned the clouds. "You will stay here?" she whispered. "Yes," I replied, but I did not know yet how it might be managed. 

Suddenly I discovered Bligh's eyes upon me. "You too, Westermark?" he growled.

It probably did not take great intelligence to read my mind to the extent of discovering that I had formed an attachment and that I was affected profoundly.

We were not permitted ashore that night, and a sleepless one it was for me, what with the flashing brilliance of the heavenly bodies and the soft, perfume-laden breeze coming off the shore to me.

My thoughts were mostly of Iolana. I had surrendered entirely to her charms, and there upon her heavenly island beside her I would stay if I could. I was determined upon that, and I thought long hours about it. A good sailing canoe seemed to me again the most likely means of escape. And this time, I resolved, I would not be caught.

As we could not go ashore to meet with women, they came to us, and there was much singing and dancing and love-making that night for nearly all but me, because only the women of the lower classes came thus and gave freely of their affection. Iolana did not come.

She later told me of a grand assembly called by her father, Kahele, to discuss the events of the day, which all the high chiefs attended.

LOST EDEN 

"Do you think these are floating islands or are they turtles?" he asked, for some of the people held that all islands were turtles. "Most likely they are turtles," said one, "because they are told where to go and when to stop and they obey." "The people on them may be Pele's children,” Kahele offered, mentioning the goddess of volcanoes. "They have small volcanoes in their mouths and they command fire by touching a twig to the side of their island. They also have volcano sticks which erupt with a loud thunder and shoot out flame and smoke. 

You must not approach these sticks within a spear's length or they may burn you." "There are no women on those islands, but only men,” said another, "and so they must be gods who are born of men." "Yet there are no babies among them." "That is true, but if they had women they would live with them on their islands." "Some are not gods but devils, I think, because their saliva is brown, and they are continually throwing it from their mouths. 

Some of them can throw it far, especially if the wind is with them." "It may be," said Kahele, "that they are really Pele's children, and that their saliva is burned brown by the volcanoes in their mouths." "No," said another, "I have listened much to the way they address one another, and they speak of this whoreson and that whoreson, and so I think they must be the Whoresons." 

And so when Kahele welcomed us ashore the next morning by throwing down a green branch of plantain, he extended his arms and said, "Aloha, Whoresons," whereupon there was much merriment, and he was beloved as a man who indeed spoke our language.

I was one of the first ashore, carrying my paints but looking first for the vision which had haunted me the night through, a charming figure in white. I found Iolana sitting apart, beneath a hau tree. She had arisen and breakfasted in the dark, as their custom was, and already had taken her first bath of the day in the royal pool and had prayed to the god of the rainbow when he appeared at the waterfall with the rising of the sun.

LOST EDEN 

203 "Hono!" she sighed. “You are real, and not a dream. I feared all night that I had but dreamed of you and the kee-ss.”

She laughed merrily, and her spritelike form seemed to dance inside the fresh white robe she wore as, hand in hand, we returned to the throng upon the beach. "My father will help you to stay here,” she whispered. “He is pleased. Hono, you will stay always?" "Yes, always," I told her, and we sought out Kahele. "I am honored," he said, and fell upon his knees and would have kissed my foot according to their custom of obeisance, but this I would not permit. I bade him rise and shook his hand. instead, and looked into his wrinkled face and saw there a promise of friendliness that deeply moved me. "Taio!" he proclaimed. "Yes," I assented with all my heart, "Taio!"

I asked him to arrange a scene for me to paint, with their neatly thatched grass houses, men and women in colorful tapa, and three chiefs in feather cloaks. This he did, and I settled down to my canvas, but what I mostly wanted was talk with him about escape. "Will you keep my secret?" I began. “Kahele has many secrets," he smiled, “and they are well guarded." "It is very important to me," I insisted. "I know which man is to die today when the sun is highest. 

He will be sacrificed to the gods, and only one other knows this secret. Is that not proof that I can hold my tongue?"

This was startling news, and I thought he must indeed be a powerful man and perhaps the best friend I could have here. "If I am to stay here, we must tell no others, not even the king, because Lono will force the king to capture me and bring me back. Can we keep our secret from the king?" "Yes, Hono. The king rules the land, but I rule the temples, and among us kahunas we have many secrets that chiefs and kings do not know. There are many places here to hide you. I will make one of them tabu and no one will come near it. There you can stay."

LOST EDEN 

"It would be better to go away," I said, “far away in a canoe in the night. How many islands are here?" "Five." "And how far away are they?" "You can see one from another." "We saw one somewhat farther in this direction," I said, pointing to the east. “Do people live there?" "Yes. It is called Oahu and many people live there, more than live here. There are four other islands beyond, in the path of the rising sun, but they are far away and the voyage is difficult. If the god of the wind is not pleased, he may blow you away where you cannot see the land, and then you must study the stars to find your way." boat?" “Does any man here know how the stars may guide his "Only I and the other priests. This wisdom is kept among us kahunas. My father taught it to me when I was a boy, and he took me to the islands which lie near the eye of the rising sun, and it is part of his fame and mine. That is why they call me Kahele, the goer." "How many days must we sail to reach the farthest island?" 

He held up the fingers of both hands. "Maybe this many." "Is it a large island?” "Yes, the largest. It is very big around and so high that the gods cover the top of it with water which is cold and solid so that the land will not be melted by the nearness of the sun. It is called Hawaii." "Kahele, will you take us to Hawaii?"

He gave me a look of astonishment, and then a warmth came into his eyes and they wrinkled at the corners as he smiled. "Yes, Hono, I will do that." "But we must touch at no islands before we reach Hawaii." 

Again the same astonishment, and then, "Yes, Hono."

At last I had a plan. If I could sail over the horizon before daylight, Cook would not know which point of the compass to choose in coming after me, nor could he send any of the natives, even if he captured the king, because none would know.

LOST EDEN 

205 Cook would have to go from one island to another until he came to the farthest, and all the time sailing against a head wind which would make progress all but impossible. The Resolution would of necessity wear ship to alter course and turn about in a wide, backward circle, each time losing almost as much as she had gained or perhaps more.

With our little sail of matting we could tack back and forth, continually sailing into the wind. This matting usually was in the shape of an inverted triangle, and not a bad design, for the wide part reached up into the air so that the boat would seldom lie becalmed in the trough of an ordinary sea. "If you are blown out of sight of land, how do you find your way over the water to such a great distance?" I asked. "I have the pattern of crossed sticks which my father gave me. It has the position of the stars upon it, and I know which stars the gods have set in the sky to guide us. This I have treasured all my life and kept in a sacred place. It is part of the wisdom of our fathers of olden times."

It would be a wild kind of gamble, I knew, but I was convinced that the Resolution would not come back from the ice islands of the north. I preferred to take my chance with Kahele. 

And above all I chose not to abandon the joy that Fate had brought to me-Iolana. 

When I told Flint, he was delighted. "Will you come along?" I asked needlessly. "That I will, matey!" he cried. "Show me the canoe and 

I'll make her shipshape and sea'orthy in no time at all."

We had our choice, from the smallest to the largest, and we selected one which would hold the four of us and our supplies but yet was small enough to be managed easily. Flint looked for wormholes, of which there were none, and ran his hand over the polished hull in appreciation of its excellent workmanship. He hesitated when he examined the mast and sail. "I am thinkin'," he said, "that a sail of matting the likes of this catches scarce half the wind. Now, if she had proper cloth sails, she could go twice as fast as any other. D'ye follow me?" "I do," I replied idly, thinking this to be only sailor's talk.

LOST EDEN "I'd rig her fore 'n' aft, I would. She'd need but a small boom and gaff for a mains'l. No, not a gaff; a sprit. That's the easier managed." 

I thought no more of this conversation at the time but later was reminded of it in a tragic manner, for Flint planned to steal canvas and make sails for us.

"Do you think we will find more lands such as this?” I asked, because I thought he would know Cook's plans if any of our company did. "I doubt that very much," he answered. He is anxious to be off to the North for his great adventure. He considers finding a Northern Passage to England the most important part of this voyage. I think he will sail in a day or two, in order to take advantage of the summer months in the North, and then return to England by the new passage or south past Japan and 

China, and back around the Cape of Good Hope."

That was good news indeed. Cook could afford to waste little time looking for me, and if the king himself knew not where to search, my chances of escape might be very good. "I hope he finds the Northern Passage," I said, and I meant that, because I knew in such case he would sail on through the north seas to England with the news of his discovery and leave me to my island life.

And so we readied the canoe, which stood upon its wooden standards high on the beach under an algaroba tree. Kahele saw to the provisioning of our little bark. "Here is dried taro," he said, “and here dried sweet potato, dried banana, dried fish. All keep many, many days. My fathers have this knowledge from ancient times.' 

He also brought fermented breadfruit, which was very good, and food made from the pandanus tree, like a yellow dough with the consistency of putty, made by scraping the starch from the base of the leaf, mixing it with coconut milk, and baking it. This was as good as any sailor's hard biscuit, quite nourishing and durable. 

Flint and I then went aboard the Resolution to fetch spare clothing and articles of trade. We found Captain Cook in the act of leaving the ship—a favorable circumstance, we thought, but it turned out to be quite the opposite. 

Cook had decided to go upon a trip of discovery into the interior of the island with Captain Clerke and Mr. King. He thus left Lieutenant Bligh in command. "Be particularly watchful against theft," he cautioned Bligh, "among our own people as well as the Indians. It would be best, I think, to punish one of our own men first, if there be an offender.

This I have always done, to set an example for the natives and to show them that we bear them no ill will." "Aye, aye, sir. It shall be done." Bligh never hesitated to assume responsibility and never shirked a duty. He accepted this temporary command of the ship as one who himself could bear the most rigid discipline and would in all conscience inflict it upon others.

When Cook left, Bligh turned upon those of us who were on deck, and I liked not his stern mien nor the sudden stiffening of his spine. "You heard the captain," he said in an acid voice, "and you will do well to remember his words. If any of you think you can

209 take liberties with a junior officer in command, I'll . . . by Jehoshaphat, I'll keelhaul you!"

At that I think all other spines stiffened. None doubted that this harsh officer meant exactly what he said. Bligh's eyes were everywhere, especially on the canoes coming and going. Frequently the Indians brought bundles of tapa to trade, and one of these bundles attracted his attention, perhaps because it was being taken away from the ship. It was larger than most, but otherwise I saw no cause for suspicion. "Stop that canoe!" Bligh shouted. "Bring it back. I'll have a look at that bundle."

Our men in the longboat caught up with the canoe and brought bundle and paddler aboard. "Where were you taking this?" Bligh asked the Indian. "To Kahele," the brown man replied. "What is in it?" "I do not know. I am not to open it, but to give it to Kahele." “Then I will open it. Weber, give me your knife.” Viciously

Bligh cut the cords which bound the bundle, and out fell two pieces of the ship's canvas. "I've a mind to cut your ears off, you skulking thief!"

The man began to tremble. "I do not know about this," he stammered.

Bligh shook out the sailcloth with the interest of a thorough sailing master who knew every piece aboard, patched or unpatched, and seemed to love them like a fond parent loves his children. "Damme!" he cried. "If it isn't a neat little mains❜l. And a jib, too. Just about the right size for a canoe. Now who in hell put you up to this? Tell me, who gave it you?"

There were many of our people who hoped to escape in canoes, but my first thought was of Ed Flint.

Bligh's voice struck terror to the heart of the poor Indian, who looked about and shook his head. "Not here," he said. "Then come with me," Bligh commanded, and went below with the man.

LOST EDEN 

I hurried down the aft companionway, in hopes of warning Ed, but could not find him, and soon I heard the bosun piping all hands on deck. When I obeyed his whistle, I saw Bligh pacing the quarter-deck with his hands behind him and our company assembled, with Flint standing foremost. It was what I had feared: Flint was caught. "It is the ship's canvas you were sending ashore, is it not?” 

Bligh asked, pretending calmness.

Flint glared in silence, bewildered, his shoulders somewhat bent forward and his head raised as though already he could feel the sting of the cat upon his back. "If you'll not answer, I'll answer for you," Bligh continued. "There is no other canvas in these islands. And would you be planning another escape, perhaps?" 

No answer. 

I stepped toward him and half opened my lips to speak, but he stopped me with a glance, as much as to say, "There is no need for two to suffer. You had best let me take it alone." "The Indian has identified you, and your silence further proves your guilt." Bligh's temper, which had been rising, now exploded. "Bosun! Keelhaul this damned whoreson!"

Murmurs ran through the people assembled there. Though not more than one or two had seen a keelhauling, all sensed the horror of this rare and diabolical punishment. "It's murder!" they exclaimed. "The barnacles! He'll be cut to shreds! He'll drown! He can't swim!" "Look alive!" Bligh shouted. "Lash a block to the end of the main boom and reeve a line through it. Mr. Peckersgill, start that bosun!" 

Peckersgill struck the man with a knotted rope's end called a colt, which he carried to use upon the Indians as well as upon our own people when their actions displeased him. 

Sullenly the men went about their preparations. With the line rove through the block, the boom was swung outboard and secured. One end of the rope was carried forward and under the bowsprit and then aft to the waist, and in this way passed beneath the ship to the opposite side. Its end then was tied to Flint's ankles.

LOST EDEN 

211 A half dozen men were told off to take up the slack of the line and to stand by to pull upon it and so haul Flint underneath the ship and across the keel. "Over the side with him!" Bligh barked. 

Two sailors seized the unfortunate Flint and threw him over the rail head foremost. The look in the poor man's eyes was terrifying. He made no outcry but splashed clumsily into the water. 

His capable hands reached for something to grasp, but, useful as they were at many tasks, they helped him none at all when he tried to swim. "Now whip!" Bligh ordered, and those who manned the line whipped it through the block, pulling quickly hand over hand.

As Flint was drawn under, his face was the last of him to disappear, and he looked at Bligh in the manner of a whipped dog.. 

With mouth open wide, my good friend went down, and bubbles came up.

I shuddered. We on deck could feel a slight bumping as the body of our shipmate struck the bottom and then a greater bump as he came against the heavy keel. All knew the sharpness of the barnacles which encrusted the ship below the water line, for most had been cut by them when, in one of the boats, they attempted to shove off or come aboard.

Like men entranced, we turned our eyes to the taut line running through the squeaking block. One more bump and a slight but rasping shudder of the deck, and Flint came up to the boom feet foremost and hung there limp and dripping not only sea water but blood as well.

Flint's shirt was all but torn away in front, for he had gone across the keel face uppermost. Long red gashes appeared upon his muscular chest, and his face as well had felt the sting of the sharpedged mollusks. A piece of flesh hung from his left cheek. His hands were gashed from grasping at those devilish barnacles. 

The seamen swung their shipmate inboard and dropped him heavily upon the deck, where we saw that he was unconscious. "Flint! Are you drowned?" I said, my lips beside his ear.

My wits must have been half gone, or I would not have asked a drowning man if he were drowned.

LOST EDEN 

"A wine cask!" I cried. Willing hands brought one.

It was bitter to see Flint's bleeding chest laid upon the cask and his heavy body rolled back and forth, but this was the best of all methods which might bring a drowning man back to life. 

We pushed and pulled, and the cask reddened, and we despaired. Then suddenly the body convulsed and out of the bloodied mouth flowed near a pint of water from the lungs. Then we heard a sigh and we knew that life was returning to the tortured body.

We laid him upon his back, and his eyes roved about, looking from one face to another, but he could not speak. He breathed and his heart pumped, and that much we had done for him, with a wine cask and many hands anxious to administer what they could. "How is it with you, Ed?" I asked.

My friend's lips moved slightly, though no words came out. 

But in his eyes I saw a look of comradeship which one man seldom sees in another. Many was the time I sought to express my appreciation to this rugged fellow who, in the hard little world of our life at sea, had become my best friend, but never did we come closer to an understanding than that day when he lay half drowned and bleeding upon the deck and smiled at me.

Now it was the Indian's turn. In fear of death, he stood trembling while they tied his feet and threw him overboard. He took no water in his lungs, but his bare flesh suffered more than 

Flint's, and he bled profusely. When released, he jumped overboard immediately and swam away, leaving a reddish trail in the water behind him. "That," Bligh observed, "is fit treatment for thieves. Who'll be the next?" "Not I," was my thought, but I felt none too sure of myself.

When Flint's wounds were washed and he lay alone upon his back in his hammock, I went to him. "I am most sorry, Ed. It was a brave try." ""Tis me own stupidity together with me hard luck, and there's a combination to beat the devil himself," he sighed. "A blessing 'tis our last day aboard this cursed ship. Tonight we'll be free."

213 "No, no, lad. You must go without me. I'm near drowned and I've lost that much blood that I'm too weak to move." "We can wait another day." ""Tis too risky," he objected, "and I'll yet be a sick man the morrow and many a day to come. Again 'tis good-by, and God bless ye, Westermark."

"Good-by," I said, and replaced his ditty box on the deck beside some drying spots of blood. 

It was not easy to part from such a friend, and I knew that I would miss him more than any of the others. 

Iolana and Kahele were waiting for me when, with a wedding ring in my pocket, I again stepped upon the shore. "We will leave tonight," I told them, and their eyes brightened. "And now we will have a marriage, according to the customs

LOST EDEN 

of my country. None must know but we three. Come to the pool where the rainbow god shows himself in the waterfall.” "A wedding? My Hono!" exclaimed Iolana, comprehending little but radiant with affection. "How do you make the wedding in your country?" "We promise to be husband and wife all our lives. We will stand together while Kahele asks the greatest of the gods to give us his blessing." "I will like that. Very good."

Together we went again to the sacred pool. The rainbow god was there. Golden sunshine touched the many flowering trees and shrubs. I chose a most enchanting bower for our chapel and asked Kahele to stand before us, while I tried to recall the wedding ceremony. I asked him to say, "Do you take this woman to be your wedded wife?" said. "I do," I answered with those words which so many have 

Then Iolana plighted her troth with "I do." "With this ring I thee wed," I said, placing Flint's ring upon her finger.

And so Flint had a large part in the wedding, after all. I doubt not that he would have thought his pearl properly bestowed, and almost I could feel his hand clapping me upon the back. 

Kahele, wishing to please me, asked, “What now, Hono?" "Please pray for a blessing," I said, "from the highest of the gods."

And so he prayed in ancient language to Kane, the creator of the universe, an invocation so long that Mrs. John Westermark grew impatient, and perhaps her husband, too. "Now it is time to swim?" she whispered. "Almost."

The pool was the favored place of love-making in the Sandwich Islands. It was in such a haven that youth met and became enamored and found romance. And there at the pool reserved to royalty was such an invitation to a nuptial swim as might never elsewhere be encountered in all the world. 

My bride unfastened a beautiful band of plaited hair which had been wound about her head. It was made of one long strand of

LOST EDEN 

215 human hairs tied one to another and woven in an intricate pattern. 

With the hairband laid aside, Iolana took off the snowy mantle which her serving maids had washed in sea water and bleached upon the sand to immaculate whiteness. Further disrobed, she turned and dived into the pool like a lovely water sprite, leaving her indulgent father with a prayer upon his lips.

But a wise old man he was. Smiling and raising his hands to the sky, he withdrew and left me to follow my bride into that crystal-clear water which held the body in a caress and seemed to say: “Here is a haven of ultimate delight. Stay, good friend, stay here." 

Aye, that I would do, and forevermore.

Together, side by side, we floated in the warm embrace of that pool in Paradise upon which smiled the rainbow god. And at the end we walked out upon the shore to our couch of soft warm grass, star-sprinkled with tiny white blooms. Aye, that was Eden.

Chapter 31 

THE SUN SWUNG LOW IN THE WEST AND THE DAY, OF ALL DAYS THE 

best, approached its end when we left our enchanted nuptial bower and went to the house of Kahele. He had prepared a wedding feast. It was tabu for women to eat with men, but to this I now would not consent. I would have my wife by my side. Kahele shook his head in amazement at such a strange foreign custom, but he followed my wishes as always. "Our last hot meal for many days," Kahele observed, without sadness but in the manner of one chosen of the gods to go upon an important though dangerous mission. "Tomorrow I will be free," I said, mostly to convince myself that this time my escape from the Resolution would be successful. I wished very much that the competent Flint could come with me, but Fate had intervened, and I knew that perhaps I never would see another white man in all my life. That was a saddening thought, but when I looked at Iolana I was content. "Tomorrow!" She placed her hand in mine. "Aye, my precious."

It was my plan to swim from the ship in the night, and so I stowed my coat and hat with the spare sail of matting in the canoe. At sunset we parted. "I will come to you in the dark of the night, when the people sleep," I whispered. "We will be waiting," Iolana answered, and I could see no trace of fear in her lovely face, but only radiant happiness. “Tonight it is!" I said as the sun disappeared in the west, and I got aboard the longboat and plowed through the breakers. 

I watched Kahele and my white-robed sweetheart grow 216

LOST EDEN 

217 smaller and disappear in the fading sunset, and so fast does darkness fall in these latitudes that I had scarce stepped onto the deck of the Resolution when the stars came out. "These stars! These sunsets! These balmy breezes!" I said to myself. “All mine now, for all my life perhaps, and so is Iolana." 

A strange sad feeling came over me as I walked to my room, taking a last look at the ship and the people. I noticed the black patch over Weber's blind eye and how the patch had frayed with wear. The tapa upon the loins of the men made strange white 

Indians of them. The scuttle butts stood ominously waiting for the future noonday line, when they would give out their slimy, stinking water in half portions. The mainmast creaked as with a weariness, and the deck rolled slovenly in boredom. 

In my little room, scarce large enough to swing our three hammocks, I bent down to my locker, to see what of my small stock of worldly goods I might take with me against the making of new friends far away over the horizon. A cockroach upon the lock waved his feelers at me. "Good-by to you too," I thought as I flicked him off. 

My pockets I stuffed with red feathers and trinkets. Two good knives remained, and I seized upon them like an Indian who knew that iron was more valuable in these lands than gold. Aye, and Indian I was to become, and I had best prepare to lead an 

Indian life.

Once more I looked through the sketches I had made in many long months at sea. Most were of beautiful lands and flowers, and of new and graceful trees with vivid colors of the tropics all about them. Few paintings suggested shipwreck or the brutal hard life of the sea, from which so many of us wished to escape. 

My plan of escape was a good one if I could but get ashore. 

The dark of the moon would help us to slip away unnoticed. In the morning, when Captain Cook would discover my absence, we would be far over the horizon and no one would know in which direction to pursue us.

There were many of our people on deck that night, and many native women, and it was past midnight before I had a chance to go over the side. I had tied lines to the rail in three

LOST EDEN 

different places and dropped them into the water. Finally the space about one of these was clear, and over I went. 

I took a deep breath and swam under water until my blood pounded in my temples. The success of the whole venture depended upon this moment. When I could stand the strain no longer, I turned upon my back and gently floated to the surface. 

For a brief moment I listened intently, but could hear no sound coming from the ship. I gulped more air and went under again. Twice more I came to the surface and then I rested. I was well out of sight of any who might be watching from the deck. "Good-by, Resolution," I said to myself. "You have given me the worst of torture in the past, but the greatest of hopes for the future. Farewell, mates. You will be green with envy when you learn of this." 

I swam slowly away, without a splash. "That," I told myself for the second time on this voyage, "is the last bit of England I may ever see."

I confess that this thought gave me a bit of a twinge, but I did not dwell long in this mood, for not far away I saw a man in the water swimming toward me. "Westermark," he called in a loud whisper. "Wait. Wait for me." can't 

It was Lord Wadleigh. "What do you want?" I demanded, considerably irritated. "The stolen sailcloth was for you and Flint, wasn't it? He go and so you are going alone. Right?" "Keep your mouth shut!" "You've got a good thing with the high priest . . . and his daughter, too. Now how about you and I being friends and going together?" "Never a chance!" "But supposing I cry out. You'll go into the brig."

I seized him by the throat and carried him under the water where he could make no noise. He struggled. He kicked and he bit me on the right forearm. He was clever with a sword, but at swimming I was his master. I got his head into the crook of my left elbow and, thus sure of closing his jaws, I brought him to the

LOST EDEN 

219 surface to breathe and in this manner swam with him through the breakers to the beach. "Hono!" called Kahele from the shadows, and, coming quickly, he asked, "What have you there?" “A drowning pig,” I panted, flopping my ugly burden upon the sand. "He would stop me from going with you." "Auwe!" "Can you have him guarded this night so that he can't return to the ship or cry out?" "Yes. Yes, Hono."

I saw Wadleigh carried inland by four warriors, and he was so spent that he could scarce moan. I thought, "That must surely be the end of the tortures which he has brought upon me." 

What a moment that was for me! The little boat stood in readiness, and beside it Iolana, my bride, my princess. "See," said Kahele proudly. "I have the sticks with the stars upon them."

He showed me a rectangle about a foot square, made of some ten or twelve crossing sticks. At one end was a large knot of grass, and when I pointed to the North Star in the sky, he nodded in understanding. Two large knots near the center I recognized as 

Castor and Pollux, "the heavenly twins" of the constellation of

Gemini, which he called Makalii. They are arranged in the sky approximately north and south. His name for these useful stars were Nanamua, the one going ahead, and Nanahope, the one following.

He appeared to know considerable about the stars, which surprised and pleased me, but he had no time to tell me more, and I comforted myself with the thought of Gemini for a guide, for I knew the sun would not enter this constellation until about May, and this was January. "This," Kahele said, indicating a small stick which ran diagonally across the chart, "is the path to Hawaii. Here we pass the island of Oahu to the south, and here Molokye and here Mowee, and then we come to this big one which is farthest away and is called Hawaii." "Yes, I see. And we must not stop until we reach Hawaii, and we must not sail near´any other canoes. In order to keep our

LOST EDEN 

voyage a secret, we must go far out to sea, where we cannot be seen from the shore. Are these islands far apart?” “No, not far. In fair weather you can see one while standing upon another. It will be easy to find our way once we reach 

Oahu, unless the winds turn against us." "And do they sometimes turn against you?" I asked, for day after day I had felt the warm breeze of these latitudes always coming from the northeast and never changing. stop.❞ "Sometimes. Then we blow far away where no more islands

I knew what he meant, because an outrigger canoe cannot beat its way against a strong wind, nor could his sail of matting be reefed to give steerageway, but must be furled or placed at the mercy of the wind.

I was amazed at this brown stranger who had grown so fond of me in a short time that he would risk his life for me. These were to be my people, and I felt a great warmth of friendship for them. Prince and princess they were indeed, and few anywhere to match them. "With this breeze we will go fastest by sailing to the south,"

I observed, and he nodded assent. Also we would be sooner over the horizon, as Waimea Bay, our starting point, is on the southern shore of Kauai. "But if we sail to the south, can you still find your way?" "Yes. The stars are my friends." "When we turn east we will be far from Oahu." We would be, I estimated, about one hundred miles from Oahu, for Cook had taken a day and a half to make the distance, and he sailed about fifty miles a day. In addition he had come some thirty miles along the coast to Waimea. A good one hundred miles it would be, plus the southing we made this night, and all in an open canoe. And I know now that to reach Hawaii from Oahu would mean another two hundred miles, about three hundred in all. 

Iolana was softly chanting, and since most chants have a religious meaning, I wished that I knew the words so that I could join her. "E kii i ka waa!" 

That much I could understand: "Get the canoe!"

221 now?" "Good," I said, gathering her in my arms. "Shall we go "Yes. We go now." And with these words she committed herself to the elements of wind and sea, relying only upon her lover and a brave old man with some crossed sticks who considered the gods not only his friends but his relatives as well. "I should have made a human sacrifice today," said Kahele, "to the god of the winds, but that would have betrayed our plans. 

I am worried about this matter. Perhaps I should go back and make one now." "It is not our custom in England,” I said, “and I have sailed many months without hurt." "Then it is well. I think I will like your gods when I come to know them better. We kill only the unfit when we sacrifice, the cripples and the sick and the feeble-minded, but always it is unpleasant business, and if I am a god in heaven someday, I will not require it."

Such was our churchgoing and our praying before we embarked, primitive and faltering, but somehow it moved me more deeply than any service I ever before had attended. 

Kahele and I hooked our forearms over the outrigger thwarts, one arm inside the canoe and one arm outside, and with some straining we got the heavily burdened boat into the wash of the surf. The rest was easy. A little sturdy paddling as each comber met us, and we were soon out upon the sea. 

We hoisted sail and immediately began to fly like a bird, or so it seemed to me. These boats make eight or nine miles an hour, and I thought ours must be as fast as any other. At this rate we would be well over the horizon by dawn. 

The hulls and spars of our ships loomed dark in the starlight, and soon they grew small and faded into the night. When they disappeared I ceased to think of England and my friends, but lay back on the spare sail, filled my lungs with the soft breeze which now was the breath of freedom. "If I really were a god," I thought, "what more could I desire or provide than this, so charming an adventure into Eden?"

Kahele steered his little bark for two or three hours. The black shape of Kauai sank low and disappeared in a mist.

LOST EDEN 

"Kauai is gone,” I said. “We are safe. Soon we must turn east toward Oahu." "Yes, soon, but not now. We will not go fast when we turn into the wind, and we are not yet out of sight of men on the hills of Kauai." "How will you know when to turn?" "The stars,” he replied with confidence. “I will watch this one in the east and that one in the west, and sail the line between them. When one sinks in the west, another rises in the east. Each one travels under the earth in its own pit and then floats true across the sky and down into its own pit again." 

How wise! He had explained the method of determining latitude from the stars, and now I felt sure that he could find his way to the other islands.

We turned eastward at last, on his judgment, and soon the dawn came booming up over the sea, followed by a brilliant sunrise. 

I looked about and could see neither land nor ships. We sailed all alone, on the broad, curving surface of the sea, my taio, my Iolana, and I.
