I held our course while he concerned himself with breakfast. He gave me water from a joint of bamboo about six inches in diameter. Then we ate of our fresh plantains and sweet potatoes and some cold roast pork, reserving our dried fish and vegetables for the days to come.

All morning we watched for land but saw only the gently swelling blue sea. Now and then a shark would come to explore us in the hope of filling his belly, and sometimes one would cut the surface of the water with the large fin which grew from his back, and we could see his greedy pig eyes. fully:

About the middle of the afternoon Kahele shouted joy- "Oahu! Oahu!" "I see nothing but a cloud." "Yes. That is the cloud above Oahu. Almost always an island has a cloud above it. The gods of sailing boats set the clouds there to guide us."

It was true that almost all the high islands had clouds above them, for the steady breezes, laden with moisture, would strike the peaks and be deflected upward into cooler atmosphere and condense into cloud forms. We watched intently. "I see it!" Iolana cried, and when we topped a large swell 223

LOST EDEN 

I also saw a purple form upon the horizon beneath the cloud, and a cheering sight it was. "We had best keep some distance away from the land,” I said, “so that no canoe from the shore can come up with us. Then if Cook comes here, no one will know we passed this way." "Good. Good. Also it is the custom to have a man on the peak watching, and he is chosen for his keen eyesight.' "Why does he watch there?" "For the fish," Kahele explained. "He is trained to see schools of fish, knowing them by the colors they make and by the disturbance of the water, and he signals to the fishermen. Also he watches for enemy flotillas of war canoes." "Is Oahu at war?" "Yes, always. And all the other islands. It is never known what day four thousand canoes will come to murder and pillage," he continued. He said four thousand as we would say one thousand because these people counted by fours instead of by tens, as we do. "And sometimes the people of an island begin to fight among themselves." "Is it that way in Hawaii?" "I think so. Perhaps never has it been any other way. 

But he steered calmly over the heaving sea, resigned to his fate with me, arriving at this state of calm fortitude by some reasoning I did not understand.

Toward evening we saw the silhouette of Oahu more clearly. "There to the left," said Kahele, "would be the Queen's 

Surf, I think. Very fine. Much fun to swim on boards there. It is called the Tumbling Water Beach."

Soon we saw another cloud, and from under it arose a second island which Kahele called Molokye. We sailed past this one in the night and next morning came up with the island of Laneye. 

This we passed, steering well out to sea. On the third morning we sighted Mowee, and the following day about noon we saw a snowcovered peak standing up from the horizon. "Hawaii it is!" Kahele shouted. "Taio, we have come over the far sea to Hawaii.” "Good. We are safe at last."

LOST EDEN 

225 "Yes, it is good, but I like not the wind today. It is too strong and also from a bad direction. It may be the sick wind, the kona wind." He took a carved stick from the bottom of the canoe, held it up to the sky in reverence, and uttered a prayer in dismal, resounding tones.

Soon the waves increased in height and white combers rushed down upon us. A dark cloud climbed into the heavens from the south and blotted out the sun. A storm burst upon us, so heavy with rain that the first downfall felt as though vast tubs of water had been cast down upon the sea.

I tried to lower the sail, but did not work fast enough. The spread of matting took the full force of a strong gust which drove the rain blindingly in our faces. As the water from the surface of the sea was lifted by the gale to mingle with the water the clouds launched down, our little bark dug her nose into the sudden cauldron and overturned. The mast snapped and with the sail went flying down-wind.

In the act of overturning, I grasped Iolana's hand and managed to retain a grip on one of the outrigger thwarts. Kahele was thrown clear and was drifting away, but with sure, strong strokes he came back to us. "The coconuts!" he shouted into the wind. "All gone!"

The nuts were floating away, but, worse than that, other stores had fallen into the sea and sunk. The bamboo water butt bobbed on the waves just out of reach for a moment and then hurried on after the coconuts. 

The canoe began to pitch and toss so that it took all our strength to hold to it, and so I removed my shirt and with it lashed Iolana to the outrigger. With other clothing Kahele and 

I made ourselves fast and waited. It was all we could do. "It is very bad," he said. "Do you think Lono has discovered our plan and is displeased so that he makes the storm?” "No, I think not." "Then it is well." "But our mast is gone, and the paddles also. We have another sail, but no mast." "That is not good."

LOST EDEN In truth, it was not good. 

All afternoon and all night the sea and wind buffeted us until we almost lost the will to live. "Are you afraid?" I asked Iolana. "No. Not afraid with you here. If we die we go to heaven together. Very nice there. Very good times with you." 

Hers was indeed a fine faith to share. 

When morning came the storm abated and the sun shone again, but there was no land to be seen. 

We righted the canoe and bailed it out. "We must swim and tow the canoe," said Kahele. "If we come to the land in two days, all will be well. It is the third day that the thirst lays hold of you." "Which direction shall we take?" "I think this way," he said, pointing, "but I do not know for sure. We must try this way first." 

Here was a stanch man indeed. He fastened a bit of broken rigging to the bow, looped it over one shoulder, went overboard, and began to swim. I rigged the spare sail to the high prow of the boat as best I could and, hungry and thirsty, stared ahead at the cloudy horizon, hoping for a snow-covered mountain to reappear. 

A crew of three was not too large for our predicament, because it required one to swim, one to hold the flimsy sail, and one to watch for sharks.

We took turns at these tasks, and it was surprising how strong a swimmer Iolana proved to be. I think she made more headway than I.

All morning we spent our strength and rested by turns. 

The sun being now nearly overhead, our thirst became almost unbearable. It was at this time that my weary eyes began to watch a bobbing object some yards to starboard, and at first I thought of it only as so much flotsam. Then suddenly I understood that this was a coconut. "Kahele!" I shouted to that tired sea horse who labored at the towline. "Coconut! This way! Coconut!"

We kept a sharp lookout thereafter for other nuts, and during the afternoon picked up two more, but at nightfall we still were lost at sea. We continued our efforts with but little hope,

LOST EDEN 

227 and it was some hours after the stars came out when Kahele, standing in the bow, suddenly shouted: "This way! Over this way is the land." "Can you see land in this darkness?" I asked. "No. Not see. I smell. It is the maile. I smell the maile on the breeze from the land."

This I could scarce believe, because I had made a sketch of maile and had been able to distinguish scarce any odor at all. But it was true that he could identify it. Perhaps the slightly broader noses of the Indians have something to do with this phenomenon, for they smell this plant strongly when we do not. "It is as good a course to steer by as any I know,” I answered. Both of us could judge our latitude approximately, but the stars would not tell us whether the land lay east or west. "It is good," this child of Nature continued. "And in the morning the wind changes. In the night it blows from the shore, but in the day it blows from the sea upon the land. It will blow us to the land." 

And he was right, for at sunrise we again saw our snowy peak, almost dead ahead, and soon after a breeze came up and blew us toward the shore.

All day, in light airs, we moved slowly but steadily, and I watched the high peak of Mauna Loa broaden out into a tremendous green island. We would have welcomed the sight of another canoe at this time, but none came, and we saw no village upon the shore. We doubted that we could reach the beach before the wind turned against us, but slowly we drew nearer to it, and about an hour before sunset we heard breakers. "In we go!" shouted Kahele, and both of us now began to tow the boat with a will, using our last remnants of strength. 

And in we went! The canoe crashed through a high surf and grounded heavily upon the beach, while the three of us dragged our weary bodies onto the dry sand. 

Safe we were at last, in my Eden. But so weary were we that when we had drawn the canoe up on the sand we all lay down and soon were fast asleep. 

I awoke with the sun shining on my face. What had happened was not a dream, after all, for rising from the beach where

LOST EDEN 

we lay was the big green island with a tall, snowy peak in the center. A balmy breeze came along the shore.

I was free at last, and safe on land. My bones ached from lying on the sand, which seems soft enough for a good bed but in reality is most punishing to the muscles. 

Turning my head, I saw my Iolana asleep beside me, her head upon her arm and the breeze moving her tousled hair. In that aspect of repose I thought she was the loveliest vision a man ever beheld. 

Beyond her was my faithful old taio, asleep on his back, with his arms and legs outstretched. His eatooas in heaven had been good to us. They had given us a safe, snug haven. 

Slowly I got to my feet, and when I did so, the surrounding country became filled with hostile cries. Natives had gathered in large numbers to peer at us. They may have been there some hours, because it is their custom to arise before dawn and breakfast heartily and bathe themselves in streams or in the surf. 

Often had our ship's people been the first white men to set foot on the shore of an island, and we thought little of the dangers because of our numbers and the effectiveness of our arms, but now I was alone, armed only with a hatchet and a sailor's knife. 

Brandishing spears of wood and large clubs, the Indians began to advance, their cries becoming fierce and loud, so that they awakened my companions. With vicious screams they hurled a volley of stones at us, some as large as a man's head.

Let the greatest chief among you take Hono to your king."

The leader then came toward us. He stood a good six feet high, well built, but with a vicious face. His heavy lips curled in a cruel, insolent expression. "I am Nooah," he said curtly. "Who are you?" try to rule us by lies. If you are such, you will not live to see the sun go to rest this night."

Here was the age-old struggle between religion and government, between church and state, which has filled so much of our history. My only hope was that these people might render unto 

God what was God's and unto Caesar what was Caesar's. 

Nooah came to me with mingled awe and contempt. He touched my white skin. "You have white paint on your island of Kauai?" he asked. "It is not paint. It is my skin," I answered, but he was not convinced.

He felt of my white breeches and said, "Your skin is loose. 

Are you sick with the drinking of too much kava?” "This is cloth like your tapa," I explained. "Mmmm," he muttered to another chief, bidding him come and feel the fine tapa and the texture of my white skin. "It may be that the old fool of a priest is right," said the second chief.

This was the moment at which Cook would have made a lavish present, and I realized with much regret that I had brought so few gifts with me. I resolved to give a knife to Nooah and a hatchet to the king if I lived long enough to meet him. 

I took my bundle of clothes and trinkets from the canoe, where I had stowed them with enough care to preserve them. My coat and hat, wet and wrinkled, I smoothed out as best I could and arrayed myself in this shabby attire to the astonishment of all. 

Then I took a knife from my bundle. I picked up a piece of driftwood from the beach, and with the knife I cut a large chip quite easily, which action drew gasps, of admiration from the natives.

I offered it to Nooah, and he seized it eagerly, his eyes wide. 

I doubt not that he foresaw its use in battle. "Come with me,” he said. “The king will be pleased with this new wonder, and perhaps he will believe your story, but I am not sure." "We are hungry and thirsty," I replied. "Coconuts!" he called, pointing to a tall tree with a good cluster of nuts, and he looked about the group.

LOST EDEN 

231 A man stepped forth carrying a piece of hibiscus bark about three feet long, the ends tied together to form a tiaa, a climbing loop. Going quickly to the tree, he stepped into the loop and adjusted it to his ankles, as though he were holding a skein of yarn. 

Springing onto the tree, he encircled it with his arms, and the tiaa enabled him to grip it with his bare feet. Using his arms and feet alternately, he climbed about one hundred fifty feet to the top with surprising speed and quite fearlessly. He tapped the nuts for ripeness, an act called toto, and twisted a few off with his hands. 

Then, going higher, he began to break them away with his feet so expertly that he sent every one down to fall upon its pointed end so that it would not crack.

The sweet, cool water of the nuts refreshed us mightily, and again I was thankful for this boon of Nature which forms so important a support of life in these lands. 

I gathered up the few feathers and the two hatchets which remained in the boat when most other supplies were lost, and we started inland. I took a last look out upon the ocean for the sails of our ships, but saw none, nor were any visible from a hill we climbed.

Soon we beheld below us the beautiful bay of Kealakakua, and in a grove near the shore a village which they called Kaulaua. "We are free," I said to Iolana, and took her in my arms. "Yes, free." She had lost her cape, and her skirt was wrinkled and untidy whereas always before it had been immaculately clean and fresh. Her hair fell riotously about her shoulders. In this charming disarray she seemed dearer than ever before. "I am a lucky fellow," I said. "You are more desirable than any other chief I have ever seen," she whispered. "I am very happy with you this day, my Hono."

Such was her cheerful faith, even though she knew not what lay ahead for us nor what tragedy I might bring upon her. 

We heard conches being blown, the sound coming from the direction of the village, and soon saw a throng of people hurrying to meet us, the news of our arrival having preceded us. 

Soon we were surrounded by excited people, in looks and stature and clothing almost identical with those of Kauai.

LOST EDEN 

"Hono!" they shouted, and, "Lono!”

I now made entry into the town in a grand manner, with Kahele holding up his wand and chanting. Many of the Indians carried stones and spears and clubs, but all fell prone at my approach, some surly and reluctant and others in fear which in some cases amounted to terror.

As we neared the house of the king, an elderly priest approached, looking intently at my taio. "Yes, it is Kahele!" he cried. "There is the scar on your neck made by my spear when we played together as boys in the long ago. I am Turi." "Turi! Yes, it is Turi!" Kahele pressed noses with the man, and tears came to the eyes of both. These Indians are deeply affected when reunited with old friends as well as by parting. I had not understood this well until that day, when I knew of my own experience that sailing away in a canoe could very well mean good-by forever.

Kahele proclaimed that I was the God Hono who had come with Lono on a floating island and had much knowledge of magic. 

Such an introduction displeased me, and I thought to correct him at once, but it came to me that I had taken possession of this island when my feet touched it, according to their custom, and if I were not accepted as a god, even survival would be dubious. 

And so I had no choice but to pose as a god for the time being, but I resolved to give them the truth later on, if I could with safety, and were it possible to make them understand the truth.

King Terreeoboo awaited us in a large building open on three sides, and what first caught my eye was a glow of the most brilliant orange coloring I had ever seen. It came from the feather capes of near a dozen chiefs who stood to receive me, and from that of their king, who sat upon a mat in front of them. 

I had thought the red cloaks of Kauai the most dazzling raiment in the world, but these of orange were brighter by far and more beautiful. Some were variegated with designs in black, red, and white. Helmets of the same material with high crests transformed these tall, stalwart warriors into bronzed Greek gods who

LOST EDEN 

233 might have stepped from the pages of a storybook. An artist could scarce imagine a more colorful pageant. 

The king was small and quite old, and he remained seated, a man holding above his head a stick with a knob of long feathers at the top which designated and honored the sovereign. "See the magical hamaite he gave me," said Nooah, who had whittled several sticks as he walked in our procession and now cut a large shaving. "Auwe!" the crowd murmured. "And this is better yet," Kahele announced, taking one of my hatchets and cutting the stick in two with a single blow.

I then presented the somewhat imperfectly made implement to the king, who looked indeed as though he felt himself to be in the presence of a god. “It is a gift from Earee rahie no Pretane-from the Great 

Chief of Britain," I explained. “Mahalo! Mahalo! [Thanks! Thanks!]” he exclaimed. “What name this Great Chief?" "King George." "Kilhargo," he repeated, as best he could.

Then he removed his golden feather cloak, the longest and most beautiful of the lot, and ordered that it be placed upon my shoulders. With that act I became one of the chiefs of Hawaii and shared their claim to divinity. 

He ordered kava prepared, but I could not bring myself to taste of this liquor. To prepare it, men sat around a bowl and chewed the roots of the ti plant, spitting the juice into the bowl. 

When it was filled, it was passed around among the chiefs, all of whom relished it for the elation it produced, something like intoxication but more like a mild opiate. 

The king ordered food, and all the women began to retire, for it was tabu for them to eat with the men, and when Iolana hesitated, two of the others took hold of her in a most unfriendly manner. "My wife," I began to explain to the king, but Kahele, wise in such matters, told me with his eyes to let her go and to follow the customs of the land.

LOST EDEN 

"Will you come to me soon?" she pleaded, and I think she knew intuitively that the people here were going to dislike her for a malahine, a stranger from another island, who immediately achieved high rank among them but appeared to be an unimportant, disheveled castaway saved from the sea. "Yes, as soon as ever I can," I assured her, and I found myself annoyed at this custom which separated us even for a moment.

Food appeared in prodigious quantities, and for once I think I ate as much as the capacious brown men about me. The meal and manner of eating were the same as on Kauai, except that here they had salt of a good quality and did not dip their viands in sea water at each bite, as was the custom on the other island. "Why does not Lono come to the makahiki?" the king asked, for this was the fourth and last month of the Lono festival of that name, dedicated to sports of all kinds—a long festival, to be sure, but always in these islands there is time for sport. "Lono must go far away at the bidding of King George,"

I said, expressing more a hope than a certainty. "Then you will compete in the sports?" Nooah asked truculently, and upon his face appeared the same look of viciousness which I had observed when first I saw him upon the beach. "Gladly," I replied, but I did not know that their sports that day would result in many bruises and wounds and cracked heads and that three would die.

And when it became known that the newly arrived god would enter the competitions with the other chiefs, there was much singing and shouting and dancing, and heavy bets were made. 

It is an odd circumstance that here, where no medium of exchange existed and barter was but little understood, the custom of betting nevertheless was well known. Some would wager their total wealth upon the outcome of a single contest. 

The chiefs were eager, for their power and importance depended in part upon their showing in these sports, especially the war games. They were in some measure bidding for the rule of the island, for kingship was not a direct inheritance from father to eldest son, but a competition. When a king died, as soon Terreeoboo must, there would ensue a struggle among the chiefs. The

LOST EDEN 

235 wisest or most powerful, the most skillful in the use of weapons, or the one who otherwise could enlist the greatest number of followers then would become king and would redistribute the lands to his own liking. 

Accordingly, each chief was anxious to excel at wrestling, boxing, spear throwing, and a score of other sports, and they boldly challenged me, a mysterious newcomer, to show what I could do.

Thus on my first day upon this island I became a high chief, unwillingly competing in some measure for a throne which I did not want and would not accept. 

First came spear throwing, and excitement ran high. Spears were the most formidable weapons these people knew, and the most skillful contestant would be the most likely leader in battle. 

Two sides or teams of twenty men each were chosen and formed in line of battle. Blunt spears sometimes were used, but this day, near the end of the makahiki festival, sharp ones were employed. After a brief chant to the gods for protection and strength, the two sides advanced with shouts of defiance, and I watched a very real battle.

The weapons measured about six feet in length, and when they were thrown they would vibrate and sometimes sing in the air. The art of dodging had been developed to a high degree. Some would catch spears in the air and throw them back, and others would retrieve spears which had passed them. 

Two men fell, one mortally wounded with a thrust in his abdomen, and I thought the battle to be at its highest pitch; but one chief was displeased at the lack of vigor shown and, joining one side, ran out in front with loud cries of challenge.

This man was Maiha-Maiha, whose name is now written Kamehameha, and he has become king of that island and of all the other Sandwich Islands. He was then about thirty years old, and an uglier fellow scarcely could be imagined. His fierce eyes were set close together, and his large, disdainful mouth was accentuated by the scowling wrinkles of his face. Yet, oddly enough, when in a pleasant mood, he had the benign look of all chiefs bred in the belief that they are divine rulers, and his intelligence and his patience in trying to understand his people marked him as a true leader.

LOST EDEN 

As he advanced toward the "enemy," six spears came at him almost simultaneously, all being in the air at the same time. 

With unbelievable cunning he dodged three, seized a fourth as its sharp point passed, and with it parried the other two. 

The crowd shouted appreciation and the men behind him rallied to his support, so that soon their opponents were routed. 

That was the end of the game. Seven lay upon the ground, five wounded and two dead. "Can you do better than that?" Nooah asked me as Kamehameha returned to us, breathing heavily but maintaining his poise. "We do not use spears in my land, and I have little knowledge of them," I replied, trying to excuse myself from participation in this sport, because I knew that my first game might very well be my last. "Then how do you defend yourself in battle?"

I wished that I had brought a musket or a pistol with which to win fame among these people, but my only weapons were one knife and one hatchet, which I resolved to retain for my own use. 

Flint had taught me the skill of throwing them, and I decided to try a cast of the hatchet. I selected a palm tree at a distance of about twenty yards and managed to strike it squarely. The sharp edge cut deeply with a satisfying thud. "Auwe!" the people cried, running to the tree to view the damage. They seemed to understand at once that I could split a skull at twenty paces in a way that none could match with spear or stone. I had gained some fame, but the sports of the day had only begun. 

Nooah challenged me to a game of bowls, which the commoners played with a round green breadfruit, but we used a highly polished discus of hard black stone. We rolled it upon earth which had been leveled and packed firmly for this purpose, endeavoring to send the bowl through a small wicket at a distance of about forty yards.

In this I was quite successful, due partly to my knowledge of the English game of bowls but perhaps more to the skill my hands had acquired in painting. I bested Nooah and won the applause of the throng of people, but this served only to increase his jealousy.

LOST EDEN 

237 "We will try the slide,” he said.

It appeared that they had learned to coast upon sleds in this tropical land, and this was one of their most thrilling sports and sometimes one of the most dangerous.

They had prepared a new track or runway which extended down a steep and rocky hillside for some four hundred yards. 

About three fathoms wide, it was paved with stones, upon which earth had been placed to make it smooth, and on top of this lay a quantity of slippery grass. "Is it possible to stay on the track at that curve?" I asked, because, about halfway down, the runway turned rather abruptly and sloped outward to the left, so that a sled likely would run over the edge and throw the rider down a hill of sharp volcanic stone. tried." "That we do not know," Nooah answered. "No one yet has

This sport called holua, then, would come nearer to being a suicide leap, and the winner would be the one who survived the plunge into the sharp rocks, if any did survive, and I thought none could.

With some four thousand people now gathered about, shouting and chanting, a man brought a small wooden sled, for all the world like an English sled, with wooden runners, except that the runners converged toward the front somewhat. It is my belief that this arrangement enables the rider to hold more firmly to the track and that it might well be adopted in our country, but of such mechanical designs I know very little. Certain I am that the grass was so slippery that nothing could be of much assistance in guiding the headlong flight of a man on this odd conveyance. 

The object of the holua is to establish a mark of distance greater than that of the other competitors. To aid in this, the runners of the sled were highly polished and lubricated with kukui-nut oil. "Nooah! Hono!" the people shouted as we appeared at the top of the precipitous slope. 

With pompous disdain and a show of self-confidence,

Nooah took the sled and walked some fifty paces up the hill, so that he could run at full tilt toward the slide and gain extra speed.

LOST EDEN 

"Auwe!" cried the people as he charged, holding the sled with his right hand upon the bottom of the right runner.

He ran so fast down the slope that I felt sure he would reach the bottom, sled or no sled. When he got to the top of the slide he swung the sled up, seizing the left runner with his left hand at the moment of throwing himself prone upon his belly with the sled underneath.

The grass squeaked. It screamed as he flew downward, and 

I watched to see how he would make the curve. He reached it in a trice, and he squirmed and wriggled. The crowd let out a mighty roar. He seemed to turn the sled beneath him, but to no avail. Off the track to the left he went, tumbling over and over down the rocky slope. 

Shouts of delight changed to cries of dismay. He lay still, as though dead, and I could see blood running from him in several places. "They must alter the curve in the slide," I thought, feeling very fortunate that this sport must now be ended, but I was quite wrong.

When the people ran to help Nooah, he would not be lifted until the chief who presided over the sport placed a stick in the rocks to mark the distance covered. My opponent had set the mark in fair competition, and now I must take my turn. 

Dripping with blood, Nooah managed to walk again to the head of the slide while another carried the sled. "You go now," he said with a look of triumph. "Would it not be better to improve the curve and try again another day?" I asked. "No. You go now." The sneer came again to his face.

I wished to explain to him that I was not competing for the kingship here and wanted but a small hut and my Iolana, but the look of the other chiefs told me that I, who had received the king's high favor, must now meet this challenge.

Chapter 34 

"WHAT A SILLY PERFORMANCE!" I THOUGHT. "I MUST RISK MY LIFE 

to gain nothing." 

I took the sled in my right hand, as Nooah had, and made an indifferent run down the hill toward the slide. If they wished to see me broken and bleeding, then so be it, but I would not try to best Nooah. I would save my neck if I could, and let him win and so end this foolishness.

I made the fall as easily as I had in my youth on the snowclad hills and streets of my native land. The sled seemed to fly like a bird, and I reached the curve before I had prepared myself for the tumble down the rocks. The grass screeched in my ears. I half closed my eyes, but, to my astonishment, the sled began to turn rather nicely.

I think few would have been so inattentive this day as to overlook the difference between Nooah and me in our steering abilities. I had thought, of course, that he dug his right foot into the grass when he wanted to turn right, but only now it came to me that he had no boots but was barefoot! The poor fellow could do little with his bare toes.

My sturdy right shoe bit into the grass and on through into the soft earth, and I yawed beautifully and turned to the right, slipping easily around the curve and so on to the bottom of the slide, a good two hundred yards beyond Nooah's mark. "Hono!" the crowd roared, shouting my name as often I had hoped it would be shouted in a cricket match. 

I laughed. My tense nerves relaxed, as with this ridiculous performance I established my greatness. 

They took me to sit beside the king, who was proud and 239

LOST EDEN 

delighted. Then others went to the slide to try, but all flew out over the rocks at the curve, and my prestige increased with each broken head. "Westermark," I told myself, “you really should loan your shoes and save these men from hurt, but you have enemies here, and you had best keep what advantage you have. You must treasure those boots and rub them often with fine coconut oil."

As I sat with King Terreeoboo, I began to talk with him of my future. "If you will let me have a small piece of land," I said, "I will build a house for my wife, Iolana, and for Kahele, and live peacefully among you." "All the land is yours. Take what you wish," he answered, for I, a foreign chief, had set my foot upon the land and so claimed it for my own. “Then I will return it all to you and save only a small piece for myself."

This pleased him, and I feel sure that he hoped I would say this but yet stood in fear of me because I appeared to have magic beyond his knowledge and might, after all, be closer related to the gods than he.

And so he called his kalaimoku, his island carver or cutter, and bade him apportion me an ahupuaa, or large slice, as compared with an ili or small slice, and to let me choose any of the lands which were not then occupied by chiefs. 

This news cheered me so mightily that I longed to see Iolana at once and tell her of it, and so I took leave of the king. 

I found her sitting with some other princesses, dressed as before, in immaculate white cape and skirt, her hair neatly arrayed. 

She had been feasted, and handmaidens had anointed her skin with flower-scented coconut oil. "Hono!" she cried, running to me and throwing her arms about me. house." “We are safe," I whispered. "We will have land and a

Her eyes grew moist as we stood with hundreds regarding us, she a lovely vision in white and I, through no virtue of my own, a favored chief in an orange feather cloak and a three-cornered hat.

LOST EDEN 

241 

Never had I known such a moment of triumph. Never had I looked to the future with a greater joy of living. "How have the women behaved toward you?" I asked. "Some good. Some not good,” she answered with an anxious look. "But we will think only of the good ones today.' 

All my wishes were granted here at Kealakekua Bay. The “island cutter" gave me a piece of land which measured more than five hundred paces along the beach and extended far up the hillside, where it ended in a point, having thus the shape of a piece of pie, according to their custom of apportioning land. 

My property extended out into the sea as far as a tall man could wade with the water coming no higher than his chin. Here 

I could fish as I chose, but I must share the fishing rights with others, who were obliged to give me either one third of their catch or all of one kind of fish. This supplied more than I needed, usually, for the Indians were very clever in throwing their circular nets. 

At first I planned to build an English house and teach the Indians what I knew of the trades and arts of my country, but the constant fear of Captain Cook led me to abandon such ideas. If he were to touch at these islands and observe traces of European culture, I doubted not that his first thought would be to look for me, and the end would be a keelhauling or a flogging, if nothing worse.

And so I let these good people build me a house in their own way, with walls of thatch to fend against the rain. They also built a room called a lanai, having but three walls, being entirely open on the seaward side, the better to enjoy our leisure moments which now were without end. 

How well I remember that first night in our new home.

Iolana had gathered an abundance of flower petals for our bed upon the floor and over these laid a sheet of purest white. We had a coverlet of five other sheets of tapa bound together at the head, the top sheet being exquisitely patterned in lavender and old rose by means of bamboo stamps, so that each small design was accurately repeated. It was the custom to take the lower sheet to the sea each day and rinse it and dry it in the sun, for these people were most cleanly in their personal habits.

LOST EDEN 

And so we retired upon a bed of sweet-scented flowers, with the moon, now at the full, pouring its golden light over us as only a tropical moon can. The coconut oil with which Iolana had softened her skin and dressed her hair was scented with jasmine, for this flower she knew to be my favorite, and she would use none other. "Are you happy?" she whispered. "Yes," I assured her, "this is the best a man may hope for in all the world." "You will never leave and go back to Pritane?" "I hope never,” I answered, and this I meant with all my heart. I thought Captain Cook must be well on his way to the 

Arctic by now, and I envisioned him discovering a Northern Passage and sailing through it triumphantly to England. Then no 

Englishman would know my whereabouts nor, indeed, the existence of the charming island of Hawaii. “Auau?” Iolana would say each morning as the sun arose to paint the eastern sky.

Auau means swim, and never did we tire of this refreshing sport, nor did she ever seem like anything but a lovely water sprite to me.

I learned to use the long, highly polished surfboard my men made for me, and with other chiefs I would ride the crest of a breaker with the speed of a galloping horse. The water almost always was of a pleasant warmth, and so clear that I could see the brilliantly colored fish below me and now and then a crab upon the bottom disturbing the sand as he walked, so that he left a little cloud of it behind him like dust.

When once I decided that I must not develop the arts and skills of the white man here, I became absorbed in the life of the Indians and their methods of using what Nature provided. They knew more of the art of living than any other islanders I had met. 

I had a score of servants of the class called kauwa, and they seemed to be no more than outcasts. I could not determine their origin, but they had less of intelligence than the ruling class and perhaps had been conquered and subjugated long ago. I grew fond of them, and they willingly supplied all our needs and catered to my slightest whim.

LOST EDEN 

243 Life was indeed bountiful, but one day when I left Iolana upon the beach and went to our house, I was startled to find there the woman called Ilima, who was the wife of Nooah. In many gatherings I had seen her dark, smoldering eyes fixed upon me in a covetous manner, and so I had taken considerable care to avoid her. 

But now she stood in my home, leaning languorously against the bole of a palm tree which had been cut and polished to form a main support of the roof. She wore one very small garment, loosely draped. "You like Ilima?" She smiled.

Chapter 35 

"WHY ARE YOU HERE?" I ASKED CHIEF NOOAH'S WIFE WHO HAD 

thus invaded my home. "Ilima has much olapa. You will see."

Omai had taught me this word olapa. It meant the joys and glories of a woman's body. "You must go. I have a wife.” "I want to be your wife, too," said Ilima. "In my country we have only one wife," I protested, scarce able to think of a way out of this situation which I had tried so diligently to prevent. "Here we can have many wives, many husbands." She advanced and threw her arms about me. "I love you, Hono. You are the greatest of all the chiefs. Someday you will be king." 

In desperation, I pushed her from me. "You belong to Nooah. You must go."

She now assumed an attitude as vicious as any I had ever seen in a woman. Her features contorted in hate and her dark eyes flamed in defiance. "What if I tell Nooah that I was here alone with you?" "You must not do that!" “I will do it and you will have to fight him!" she cried angrily, and then her attitude softened as she added, "But you can kill him because you have magic." "No. You must leave. Go now." 

She laughed. "It is too late. Nooah is on his way here. You should see him coming up the hill, I think. Shall we look?”

LOST EDEN 

245 hill. It was true. Nooah was coming toward my house. In his hand he carried the hatchet I gave to the king. Ilima gasped. "Auwe! He will kill you! I love you and I have killed you," she cried. "Be quiet!" I said, casting about for some kind of defense.

If I had brought but one pistol with me, I would have been master here. How foolish I had been to build a house alone upon a hill and to think that I was in Paradise! Here were the same problems of love and hate and jealousy which plagued the sons of man in all other lands of the earth.

My hatchet and my knife were my only weapons. There was some consolidation in the fact that I could throw both of them with some degree of accuracy and, as far as I knew, Nooah could not. But if I should miss, and come to grapples with that giant, I would have not a chance. 

Slowly he came up my path, scowling in the bright sunlight. I stepped out to meet him, hatchet in hand. "Good morning,” I said. "Good morning." "What brings you here?" "I am angry with you." He came closer and closer, and soon 

I would have to retreat if I were to make a proper cast of my weapon. "Why is that?" “This hatchet. It has lost its magic. It is not better than a stone. It is a fraud."

Then I laughed, much relieved, not only because apparently he did not know his wife was within, but because of the appearance of the hatchet. Its edge was gone entirely, nicked and mashed. "What caused this?" I asked. "We tested it against our stone hatchets, and now it no longer cuts and it is not as good as ours. It is a fraud."

Now I thought he must be trying overhard to make trouble for me, because these Indians knew the use of a whetstone in both shaping and sharpening their implements. But possibly his simple mind did not think that a new edge could be restored to hamaite with a stone. Accordingly, I patiently sharpened the hatchet for him and educated the childish giant in its proper use.

LOST EDEN 

All the while I could hear little sounds coming from the house as Ilima moved about, but love and hate mingled in her to my temporary advantage, and she did not come out. It was with much relief that I sent the vicious Nooah down the path to the sea with his sharpened hamaite, again alive with magic. "Go now," I said to Ilima, “and never come back." 

She laughed. "I stay," she said, and lay down upon the mat.

Nothing would induce her to move. Soon Iolana would return. In desperation, I went to the near-by house of Kahele and asked him what to do. "It is easy," he said. "Very easy.”

Presently he came with two poles about six feet high, each with a knob of wood at the top. He started to erect these at each side of my door, and as he did so Ilima ran out screaming. They were tabu sticks, and to enter the house uninvited would now mean death.

Such is the power of the priests. The tabu is such a forceful edict that to break it is fatal, whether the culprit is caught in the act or not. The Indians shy from a tabu stick as though it were a ghost or a demon of fire. If they violate the tabu, they wither and die of their own volition, a definite proof of the control of the mind over the body.

The look of terror upon Ilima's face was dreadful to see, but when she had run a few paces from the house, the look changed to one of contempt. "I hate you!" she screamed, and left me little doubt of it. "I will make you pay for this. You will see." 

With that she flounced down the trail to the sea, leaving me much disturbed. Through no fault of my own I had made another enemy, and a vicious one. Life in Paradise that day was not to my liking, and I thought of the England I had left, as often I let my mind wander back home when I became puzzled with the childish minds which here were coupled with stalwart and sometimes murderous bodies.

In this mood I reached up into the thatch of the roof and brought out an object which often consoled me. It was the oval of pearl shell upon which I had painted the face of Lorna.

247 Still fresh and clear it was, and Lorna smiled at me as of old. "How different my life might have been,” I was thinking as Iolana appeared in the room. “Ilima said she was with you. Is that true?" she asked.

Chapter 36 

THIS IDYLLIC EXISTENCE FLOWED ON, MONTH AFTER MONTH, WITH 

feasting and playing. In this land of perpetual summer the seasons were marked more by the coming and going of the flowers than by change of temperature or the disposition of the elements. 

Then came the sixteenth of January. It was a year, lacking only two days, since Captain Cook had first sighted these far Pacific islands, the first being Oahu and the second Kauai, where 

I found my wife. 

The sun that January morning had traveled but halfway up the sky when a breathless runner came from his home up in the hills. "Lono! Lono comes!" With eyes wide and hand trembling, he pointed to the southern horizon.

All the Indians now knew the story of Lono coming to Kauai, for his visit became the subject of a legend, or mele, which was chanted and diligently taught to children that they might pass it on to their descendants. 

I strained my eyes but at first could see only the usual golden haze upon the water. The man pointed more carefully then, and I made out the sails of two ships hull down. "It can't be Cook," I thought, but without much conviction, because the uncanny wisdom of that unbelievably capable commander had given most of his men the feeling that always he would have his way, always he would command, and that no opposition could succeed against him.

At once the Indians became aroused almost to a frenzy, as 

Englishmen might upon Resurrection Day if they were to see the 

Lord coming with His host of angels. 248

249 In my role of high chief, I had a large war canoe of my own, and often I had wondered if someday it might be a means of escape from Cook. This thought indeed had little relish. I had tempted Providence once in this manner and might not again be so fortunate.

Only four times in their history, then, were canoes successful in navigating that vast expanse of water. "Do you know the way?" "No, but the gods will tell us." However, Terreeoboo's absence created some advantages. 

For one thing, the king would not be at hand for Cook to capture and so force my surrender. More important, the power of government in the king's absence was divided between the chiefs and the priests, and this day the priests took precedence, as they had in Kauai. "A tabu of death it is!" they agreed when they had heard

Kahele's suggestions, and they sent runners about the island with conches to spread the news like town criers. War canoes were paddled speedily to overtake the smaller craft which had put out to meet Lono, and all were informed of the tabu. "It is well," said Kahele with confidence.

Indeed, it seemed probable that our secret might be kept. 

Almost at once I could see a change on the faces of the brown people. It was as though the Angel of Death hovered over them to strike down any who should but speak the name of Hono.

251 time, I knew, she had a vicious weapon. Accordingly, I looked to the provisioning of my canoe for a long voyage, I knew not where. 

My servants brought sea supplies such as Kahele had put in our canoe at Kauai-dried sweet potato, banana, and fish, a good store of hard biscuit made from the pandanus tree, and many of the life-giving drinking nuts. These stores I battened down with the aid of my hatchet so securely that they would not likely be lost unless the boat were dashed to pieces.

Canoes put off from the beach by the hundreds, and the wildest excitement reigned. "How now? Do you think Lono is going to stop here?"

Iolana's voice was filled with melancholy. "Perhaps not." I tried to allay her fears, but I believed this to be the best bay upon the whole southwestern coast of Hawaii. 

In my swimming and fishing I had discovered deep water close to the shore at the north of the bay where we watched. Here, I knew, the ships might anchor in the lee of the hill upon which we sat and safely ride out any ordinary storm. But Cook did not know the depth of the water here, and I hoped he might not learn of it. "Lono and the other gods must be very hungry, for they have been gone a year," Iolana observed quite correctly, "and no food grows on the tall trees of his islands. Will he not come ashore to feast?" "It may be that the canoes will take him plenty to eat and he will sail on in search of a better harbor," I said hopefully. Most of the canoes were laden with produce, which was exchanged for nails and red feathers.

Soon, however, I saw a small craft rippling the waters in a manner foreign to an outrigger canoe. It came up fast toward the land. Aye, it was the Resolution's cutter, and it made directly for the lee of the headland beneath my feet.

So good was Bligh at this charting that a large number of the Resolution's maps and records were made entirely by him. Indeed, he was so diligent that at the end of the voyage he demanded and received one eighth of the profits of the published journal which set forth the official account. "By the mark three!"

The men in the boat showed their despair at having come so close to this fair land that they could feel it with a heaving lead and smell the flowers of it, only to be told to put about and return to the ship.

So we went to the large house where more than a score of the nobility sat, both priests and chiefs, and the choicest feather capes lent color to the assembly on the greatest day yet known in their history. 

I made my obeisance to the absent king by kissing a wooden bowl which was believed to contain his spirit and was prominently displayed, that it might be watchful of his realm while he was away.

Then I bowed to Koah, a little, old, emaciated man like the king, his eyes sore and red and his body spotted with the leprous scurf which comes of drinking kava. He had been a chief first, like 

Kahele, and a distinguished warrior, so that he commanded respect from the other chiefs on this score, and now he was become in addition a powerful priest. "It is well that you have come," old Koah smiled. “We

255 desire your advice. How many human sacrifices do you think we should make this day to the great Lono?" "None!" I replied rather abruptly, for the thought revolted me. "Lono does not like that."

I think I might have come out with the fact that Captain Cook was a man and in no way a god, but he had permitted himself to be adored as such in Kauai, and I could all but feel his penetrating eyes upon me, demanding that I bow to his will and leave to him all decisions and all policies. He pointed to a small pile of nails and bits of rusty iron cut from the hoops of our empty casks. Some of the nails had been hammered into chisels by Cook's armorer, it appeared, but some were unchanged, as though they might have been stolen from the ship's supplies or drawn from the cleats and planking. I could not but wonder what parts of the vessels had been weakened by their loss. "This one I got in exchange for six hogs," said Nooah proudly, holding up a fourpenny nail which had been flattened at the point by blows of a hammer. "It cuts wood as easily as one can cut taro with a shell. We are very wealthy people now, with these many scrapers and cutters, but I have a better plan for getting them." "What is that?" asked Koah. “I am a warrior, and it is my honored profession to plunder and steal. I wish to man twenty war canoes and fill them with throwing stones. With this force I can easily capture both of the

LOST EDEN 

floating islands, and we will then be the wealthiest people in the world."

It was obvious that the assembled warriors liked the plan well enough, but a murmur went up from the priests, who saw in this proposal a likelihood of power being taken from them. "No! No!" they cried. "Lono will strike you dead. This is business for priests and not for warriors."

The priests' council of caution prevailed because many of the chiefs were doubtful of the outcome of such an attack as Nooah suggested. They were superstitious about the strange floating islands and the objects upon them which appeared to have been wrought by the powerful magic of supernatural beings. "Our women who have gone to these floating islands say that these are not gods but men in all respects," Nooah persisted, and I doubted not that his wife Ilima was a leader in the type of investigation to which he alluded. “It is only their color that is different, and I think a famine sickness makes them white, because they eat great quantities of our food and accept everything edible that is offered them." "We must wait and see," said Koah gravely. "Let the priests make a fitting welcome, and let the warriors be watchful. The white men know many useful arts, and we must induce them to stay with us if we can. What do you think about that, Hono?" “I think none will stay but many will wish to stay,” I answered. “They must go away again because Lono has ordered it." "Then so be it," Koah decided reluctantly, for I am sure he wished to walk with the white gods in Hawaii for the remainder of his days and share their importance. "Now we must go to the beach and make a fitting welcome."

While the chiefs and the other Indians assembled upon the shore to the number of some ten thousand, having come from many parts of the island on hearing the great news of Lono, Iolana and I watched Cook's landing from a safe distance.

Soon both ships were anchored some two cable lengths from the shore in the place Bligh had chosen. The Indians swam about them like shoals of fish and swarmed upon the decks and up into the rigging. Some ventured as far aloft as the main and foretops, but took their route through the lubber's hole rather

257 than by the futtock shrouds, nor would they go upon the yards. 

Others measured the ships with lines of braided sennit and tied knots indicating length and breadth as a record for future generations. Having no written language, such knotted lines were kept in the native archives as we store books in our libraries. 

I saw Captain Cook move among those on the Resolution, his reefer hat and powdered hair showing above the heads of all but the very tallest. Here and there I recognized another face or figure, and I could guess with what longing my mates surveyed the shore and with what anxiety they looked forward to the delights they had known in Tahiti and Kauai and would now find here in yet greater abundance.

Soon a boat put off from the Resolution and made for the shore, with Captain Cook, Lieutenant King, and Lieutenant Bligh dressed in their finest, Webb and Mason pulling at the sweeps with Zimmerman and Farley, and the bosun at the tiller. What a precious creature she was! I knew not what slight threads our happiness might hang upon, but I was determined to take desperate chances, if need be, to preserve it. 

From the background we witnessed Cook's remarkable progress through the prostrate crowd. As soon as he passed the prone worshipers they would get upon their feet, but when he turned his head they would again fall flat, a comical performance in so vast a throng.

Four priests walked ahead with their symbolical small sticks 259

LOST EDEN 

covered with the skins of dogs' tails and intoned a chant to Lono. 

It was this power which kept the natives in check, and without which the white people, so vastly outnumbered, might have fared badly.

Yes, Captain Cook was believed to be a god. But whatever he thought of this adoration, he merely smiled and accepted all as his right or as the best expedient.

The banqueting and the festivities which now began were on such a scale as might not easily be imagined. A real god in all his splendor had come to honor this land and was entertained accordingly. It is the way of these generous Indians to give half their meal to anyone who asks, and if a second should ask they then divide their remaining portion with him. And what they offered to the gods and demigods of the floating islands this day was little short of all they possessed. 

Endless streams of men and women came over the deeply worn trails bearing gifts, and the sickly, tired sailors of Britain found themselves in luck beyond their fondest dreaming. Beautiful women vied for the privilege of caressing even the ugliest and the most decrepit. 

The best dancers in all the island clamored for the chance to appear before Lono, and the seductive hula-hula was presented with animation such as I never before had seen. 

Lieutenant Molesworth Phillips drilled the marines, in their black-and-white striped breeches, to the accompaniment of fifes and drums and French horns, but he got not half the applause given to the dancers. Lieutenant Bligh, often caustic in his comments, wrote of this performance, "Phillips was as little capable of drilling his men as the music was adaptable."

But the Englishmen scored with their fireworks at the end of a long evening of revelry. At the swoosh of the first rocket, the whole congregation fled, women as well as men, the only ones to remain being those who were trampled in the stampede. And so, laughing, and full of good food and high spirits, the mighty gods from Britain sought their repose, each in his own way. 

Dawn was breaking when I walked home with Iolana. It appeared that no one yet had told Cook of my presence here, and 

I was not without hope.

And so I learned about the ships and their people bit by bit in many visits with Flint in the following days. They had sailed farther north than any before them. They went through Behring 

Strait without recognizing it as such, for Behring, in 1728, had

LOST EDEN 

placed it inaccurately upon his map, not having the blessing of a Hanley watch like Cook's. They encountered a barrier of ice at 

70 degrees and 44 minutes north latitude and could go no farther.

In returning, they had narrowly missed being crushed in the ice floes. 

But, nothing daunted, Cook was planning to go north again and try once more for the Northern Passage! Accordingly, he encouraged his men to fill their skins with the produce of Hawaii, and this they did with zest. 

But he stayed nineteen days, which was too long a time. He wore out his welcome, as they say, and he took everything these generous people offered and gave almost nothing in return. 

In addition to these difficulties, the differences between the white and the brown began to make themselves felt. I first noticed 

Cook's impatience with the Indians at a meal where the best of the land was provided for him. 

At this banquet one of the chiefs took some roasted pork in his fingers and offered to feed Cook. The captain declined, with some impatience at this unclean procedure, whereupon the chief thought he had not gone far enough in his hospitality and therewith chewed the meat and offered it again. "Away with that!" Cook's quick temper flashed and he knocked the proffered mouthful from the Indian's hand. 

Now a god should not act contrary to the customs of heaven. 

He should not violate his own customs. In addition to insulting his host, Cook had given the first indication that he was not the true, benign god called Lono. 

And when William Watman died of drink, hardship, and long abuse of his body, he was buried in the temple. The chiefs looked on in amazement. "These are mortal men and not gods," they whispered. "They die as we die. Can they not then be killed with stones and spears?" 

Other suspicious actions the Indians observed near the temple where a plot of ground had been allotted to Cook for his observatory. In order to protect the astronomical instruments there, a tabu was made, but this was neither properly understood nor respected by the Englishmen.

LOST EDEN 

265 A tabu carries with it the thought of a watchful demon who rides the wings of death, and I think only by such a superstition could my whereabouts have been kept a secret. But the white gods began to violate this sacred edict. This was occasioned by the yearning of the men for women at night. One by one they began to creep out of the compound in the darkness and return with women, and soon all were engaged openly in this activity, there being little chance of secrecy between a number of men on a half acre of ground all bent upon the same thing. 

Soon most of the natives knew what was going on, and so, after a few days, the tabu sticks were taken down and the Indians came to the observatory openly and talked and wrestled and learned the use of firearms, having lost much respect for the white gods whose tabus could be broken without penalty. 

Whereas at first large presents of food were made with a regularity more like the discharge of a religious duty than the effort of mere liberality, the Indians began to change their attitude, especially the chiefs, and their familiar trait of stealing came to the fore.

They began to take many small articles from the ships, especially those made of iron. Some of the most expert swimmers were one day discovered under the ships, drawing out the filling nails of the sheathing. This operation they performed very dexterously by means of a short stick with a hard, sharp stone fixed in the end of it.

To put a stop to this practice, which endangered the very existence of the vessels, Cook at first fired small shot at the offenders, but they easily got out of reach by diving under the ship's bottom. It therefore was found necessary to make an example by flogging one of them.

This cruel punishment they resented, and they might have caused trouble that day had not their king returned from Maui with his fleet of war canoes, which formed a most picturesque pageant in the bay.

King Terreeoboo went aboard the Resolution speechless with wonder and placed his priceless cloak around Cook, who unbuckled his hangar and fastened it about the waist of the king. 

It appeared by this ceremony that the English yet were held

LOST EDEN 

in high esteem, but the Indians began to ask them when they would depart and would pat the round paunches of some of our people who indeed had grown fat on this land. "Is there a famine on the island where you live?" they would ask the white men. "We have not much food remaining for our own people," the king finally said one day to Cook. "Could you go away now and come back at the next breadfruit season when there will be plenty again?" 

And so, after nineteen days, Cook at last decided to set sail.

He was generously supplied with fresh produce and had salted down a large part of the pork of the island, and was as well equipped for a voyage to the North Pole as he might hope to be, except for the matter of firewood. Never had there been a large supply of dried wood on hand, but Cook desired to stock both ships at once, and so he ordered his men to go to the temple and tear down the wooden fence there and take whatever else of wood they might find. This order was a serious blunder. 

When the sailors began to destroy the temple, the amazement of the Indians was plain to see. Lono was desecrating the holy of holies! "I think he is not the real Lono," said Chief Nooah. "He destroys the morai where he is worshiped. What do you think, Pua?" "It is very strange to me," answered the chief called Pua. "Certain I am that the men with him are not gods, because one of them lies dead these many days and does not come back to life. 

All could be killed, perhaps, except Lono and the others with moons upon their heads. Shall we kill them now?" "I am for it. How many others will be with us in battle?" "Peace!" the priest Koah commanded. "You will bring the vengeance of the gods upon all of us. Few warriors will stand by your side in battle, and you will lose your life by fire from the poo-poos." "You priests have taught nonsense to the people," Nooah replied hotly. "Otherwise I would have an army behind me this moment."

It was Captain Clerke's unpleasant duty to carry out Cook's

LOST EDEN 

267 order, and so he took along a couple of good hatchets to offer in payment for the wood of the temple. "Here," he said to Koah. "Cook sends you these hatchets."

But Koah made no reply, nor would he make any sign of acceptance. Lono could demolish his temple if he chose, and no payment was required. Accordingly, Clerke threw down the implements and permitted the work to proceed. 

Even when the sailors carried off the wooden idols to the ships, there was no complaint. Mr. Clerke went again to the priests. "I am sorry about the images," he said. "I will have them returned at once." 

Still the priests did not answer. "You want none of them to remain?" he asked. "It would be well to bring back Kane.” "Which one?" "The large one that stood in the center." 

Aye, Lono might take the lesser gods who were beneath him in station, but he should return the creator of the world. 

And so Kane was all that remained of the sacred relics of the temple. All else went for firewood. 

Flint came to me in haste. "I have come to say good-by once more," he said, "because 

I think we will set sail this day."
