Chapter 40 

A FINAL BANQUET WAS SPREAD FOR CAPTAIN COOK AND HIS MEN. AS 

a farewell tribute, the konohikis, or tax collectors, were sent about the island to comb it for all that remained of produce, hogs, tapa, and other gifts.

All was laid before King Terreeoboo, and carefully he took one third for his people and gave two thirds to Cook. Such generosity is seldom met with upon this earth. The captain accepted his share readily enough, though he must have guessed that the Indians would have little to eat but fish and poi for a long time to come. 

So at last, with a boisterous, rowdy fling on shore, the company went off to the ships, set sail, and weighed for a voyage to the ice islands of the North Pole region, on the fourth of February.

I sat on the headland with Iolana and watched the stately Resolution and Discovery shake out their canvas and sail out of the bay, in a good breeze, each with a bone in her teeth, as the sailors say in describing the white water at the bow of a swiftly moving ship. "There goes all that remains to me of England," I said, but with only momentary sadness, for the joy of Iolana was wonderful to behold.

She placed her arms about me, and the warm mauka breeze blew her jasmine-scented hair against my cheek. "You are mine now, forever," my sweetheart said. "Aye, so be it." Never did the land appear so green, the airs so pleasant in their caress, the sea so blue, the surf so merry. This land all mine again! 268

LOST EDEN 

269 My secret was kept, and I had the priests to thank for that, but they were a dismal lot this day. They lost much prestige with the sailing of the white gods, and in addition they were saddened by parting with friends, as always. Most of them wept. 

So it was that the priests began to pray for Lono's return. 

They went to the desecrated temple and prayed to the only idol which remained, Kane, the creator of the universe. It was an allnight vigil, and it began with a human sacrifice. “O Kane, master of the clouds and the rains and the winds," they petitioned, “cause a storm to blow upon the floating islands of Lono and push them back again to Hawaii."

They paused only to look for signs from the heavens, and soon after sunrise came what all considered an unmistakable answer to their prayers. The wind shifted, and so sudden and violent was the change that almost every canoe in the bay was overturned. 

So severe was the tempest that many Indians were drowned, notwithstanding their unusual ability to swim. 

Iolana and I had gone to a pleasant pool for our morning swim, and we were walking through a grove of tall coconut trees when the storm struck. Now of all places to be during a tropical storm, a coconut grove is perhaps the most dangerous and terrifying.

Each tall tree, often reaching well above two hundred feet, has a large cluster of nuts at the top, and several of each cluster always have ripened and are ready to fall at a touch. Imagine then this arsenal of Nature's cannon balls, hundreds of them, ready to be catapulted from unseen heights.

At the first blast of the wind the tall, slender trees bent far over. Then they snapped back! This snapping action flicked off the ripe nuts. A vast battery showered the grove with ammunition as effective as five-pound shot. The nuts thudded, bounced, galloped, crashed.

A man beside us took one on the head and died instantly, a hollow in the back of his skull in which you might have laid your fist.

The best defense I knew-and I had seen a few sudden gales, but none with the force of this one-was to retreat walking backward. In this way we could see most of the missiles coming

LOST EDEN 

and have a better chance of dodging them. And so backward we went, toward the open space of the pool. "Auwe!" Iolana screamed as she tripped and fell.

I caught her up, taking a severe blow upon my left leg the while, and, jumping and stumbling, we reached the safety of the pool. "Hono, the gods are very angry this day!" Iolana gasped. "Yes, very," I agreed, rubbing my sore leg to ease the pain, and in this predicament I was astonished to hear cries of joy as two priests came running backward, as we had, and joined us in the pool. "Kane answers our prayers!" they cried in delight, and renewed their efforts to bring Cook back again. "Blow the floating islands to the shore! Blow! Blow!"

Soon the pool was well filled with those who sought to escape the coconut cannonade, and most of them seemed to believe that their akunas had brought about the storm. They had additional proof when the wind subsided somewhat and we could look out again upon the sea, for there, not five leagues off, were the ships! "Kane be praised! Kane be thanked. O great is Kane, the maker of the strong wind. Blow Lono back. Blow! Blow!" So went the exhortations of the priests in their hour of glory. They continued to call out in this manner for hours, taking encouragement from every fresh gust of wind.

The ships brought to under their mizzen staysails, and 

Iolana and I watched their struggle. fided. "I am praying a different way from the akunas," she con- “Good,” I said. “Pray hard.”

She did. She closed her eyes and recited her litany until she could scarce speak any more. Hour after hour the ships fought the storm, and about four of the afternoon there came a shift of the wind, and quickly sails were shaken out. Soon they grew smaller and at last disappeared over the horizon. 

I longed for the sunshine and the steady northeast breeze which would blow my mates away for good, but there was no break in the weather. For seven days the storm raged so that no

LOST EDEN 

271 canoes ventured out into the bay, and reports came that the floating islands could yet be seen near the land a few leagues up coast. the 

And on the morning of the seventh day of the storm which

Kane made we saw two tiny specks of white upon the horizon. "Lono! It can't be!" cried Iolana.

But Lono it was, and sadly we watched him approach, his sails growing larger and larger until he was scarce a league from the land. I saw the wind split both the fore and main topsails of the 

Resolution, and I knew my friends were in serious trouble. 

They bent on other sails, but these also ripped. I thought they must be in grave danger of demasting the ships as they fought against being blown back upon this lee shore, and I was right. 

The foremast of the Resolution gave way for the second time on the voyage. The first time it had been repaired in Nootka 

Sound, on the coast of America, but the fishes which had been put on the head were now sprung by this storm, and the parts so defective as to make it absolutely necessary to replace them and, of course, to unship the mast and take it ashore for repairs.

In this difficulty, Captain Cook was for some time in doubt whether he should run the chance of meeting with another harbor or return to the one of Kealakekua, which he had left but a week before.

But in the end he had only one choice. If he searched for another harbor and found none, he could not regain this one by sailing against a heavy wind with a defective mast, and so he stood in for his old anchorage beneath the headland where we sat! "O Kane! All powerful! You have heard and answered!” the priests chanted. 

I swore.

It was a dismal moment indeed when I watched the ships come in again and anchor. 

Ilima came to me, boiling with rage. "I will tell now, and they will take you away as they took my Flint away!"

There seemed to be nothing left for me now but a long voyage in my war canoe, and so I sought out Kahele. "I must run for it," I told him. "Will you take the canoe

LOST EDEN 

when the weather calms and meet me up the coast near Napoopoo?" "Yes, Hono." "It will be very dangerous. We must go far out on the ocean for many days and you must try to guide us back by the stars." "Yes, Hono. It is well, because we have trouble now between the chiefs and the priests. The chiefs are angry because we made a prayer and brought Lono back. They say we will now have famine in the land because the bellies of the white men are big and always empty. There is talk of fighting." "Do you think they will attack Lono?" “I cannot be sure. We have laid a tabu upon the bay so that the people will not approach Lono, but there is much anger among them, and when anger lays hold of them they forget the akunas and think only of killing. I fear for your own safety."

This turn of events I had not expected. "Then I must be watchful, and any moment I may disappear inland," I said, and when I saw the cutter shove off from the Resolution I remarked, "That is the kind of boat we should have. I would feel much safer in her than in my canoe." "I will see what can be done," Kahele replied, but it did not occur to me that he planned to steal that very cutter. My good old taio was as simple as he was faithful. "Let us hope there will be no trouble," I said. "The tabu is very strong," he answered, shaking his head, "but it is difficult to read the hearts of so many people. We must watch Nooah. He is the one who wishes most to fight." 

And so I watched, this time more like a hare ready to bound away, for now I had not only to watch our people but the chiefs as well.

There was trouble at the watering place. Although the 

Englishmen had been at sea but seven days, their first thought, as always, was for fresh water, and accordingly a boat was dispatched to a small stream.

But none of the Indians would help with the casks, whereas before they readily had lent their services. They were forbidden by their chiefs, and some threw stones at the white men.

LOST EDEN 

273 I found Nooah as soon as I could, to sound out his temper. "Will there be fighting with my people?" I asked him. 

He grew sullen, deigning at first not to reply. "If you fight," I continued, “many of you will be killed by the poo-poos." "But enough will remain to win a victory," he retorted, his red eyes glaring fiercely, and I knew that he was right. 

The marines could fire their pieces but twice and then would be obliged to insert fresh cartouches, and this the Indians seemed to know. They had seen men fall before the muskets, but also they had noted the delay necessary to reload. A crowd of warriors attacking during this delay would certainly overwhelm our scanty forces. Until now the natives had feared the noise and fire of the pieces, but this day Nooah gave evidence that he no longer was afraid.

He and Kamehameha went out to the Resolution and were welcomed on board. As usual, they took presents of pigs and produce. They asked for iron in return, magic iron for weapons. 

Other natives went to the ships, and among them were the outstanding thieves of Hawaii, who plied their trade with a will. 

One seized the iron tongs from the armorer's forge and jumped overboard with them. He was caught and flogged.

Nooah watched but said nothing, so Vancouver told me later, and he was of the opinion that Nooah had given the command to steal the tongs, thinking that he could make iron weapons with them as our people did.

Then another native stole the tongs again, and a chisel as well, and jumped over the side, with Nooah watching. I knew the 

Indian well enough to consider him a brave man, a warrior whose business was plundering, and he would gladly risk flogging or even death at his chief's order.

The man got clean off with the tongs, perhaps the most successful theft yet perpetrated, and Cook's temper began to rise. 

On the following day Nooah went to the ship again with

Kamehameha, and each carried a beautiful feather cloak, the most favored of all trade articles. 

Kamehameha, whose name means "the very lonely one," demanded eight iron daggers for his cloak. He, as well as others,

LOST EDEN 

would rather have had muskets, but these he knew he could not buy. What plans he had for becoming king, I know not, but he was indeed a lonely fellow in his thoughts and behavior, as much apart as a wise king who finds about him only men of lesser intelligence.

Nooah asked for only one dagger, but he insisted that it be a big one. "Like this?" Cook asked, offering him the largest aboard, something more than a foot in length. "No. This long!" Nooah insisted, and, holding out his arm, he indicated that he desired one as long as from his shoulder to his finger tips. "Very well," said Cook, for the proffered feather cloak had few to compare with it in these islands, I am sure. It was more enchanting than my own by far, and often I had longed to have one like it.

And while the armorer went about the business of making a dagger the length of a tall man's arm, Nooah asked some pertinent questions. "Are you a tata toa [a warrior]?" "Yes, I am," Cook smiled, for indeed he was a captain in the King's Navy. He did not know that he was settling in the 

Indian's mind the question of whether he was an immortal or just another fighting man who could be killed.

Nooah still had some doubt because of the great superstitions the priests had woven about Cook and the apparent magic which surrounded him. "If you are a tata toa, you should have some scars,” Nooah observed. "Can you show me some?"

Laughingly, Cook held up his hand, the one which had been severely cut between thumb and forefinger. It was a scar worthy of any tata toa. "Yes, yes," said Nooah, much delighted, and soon he went ashore without his favorite cloak, but with the largest dagger anyone here had ever seen.

He was much too wise to attack Cook on the ship, where marines stood at the ready and officers wore their hangers, and I do not know what plans he made, but in the night something

LOST EDEN 

275 happened which led directly to battle. A native swam out to the 

Resolution in the dark, cut the painter to which the cutter was tied, and slowly pushed it ahead of him until he got clean off with it.

In the morning Kahele was very happy. "They have stolen the boat you like," he smiled. “Now if we must go away we will have a small floating island of our own, and its magic will overcome all the storms."

I knew the loss of the cutter was a serious matter to the people on the Resolution, but they could build another if they chose. I must confess that I was pleased, because now I desired to escape not only from Cook but also from the chiefs who seemed to be angry with all white men, and for me it might be a matter of life or death.

But when Cook discovered the theft of the cutter he acted with his usual force and directness. He called all the men to arms. "Load your pieces with ball, in both barrels," he ordered. "Man the boats and see that not one canoe leaves the bay. We will hold them all until we get the cutter back, and will destroy them all if necessary." 

Then he decided to play his trump card. He would take

King Terreeoboo on board the Resolution and hold him as hostage for the missing cutter. Accordingly Cook came off with two armed boats, landed at the village, and demanded to see the king. 

With Cook on shore were only twelve marines. Others in the two boats remained offshore, just beyond the reach of stones and spears. Our captain was a brave man. He seemed to be completely devoid of fear, and his large eyes, wide apart, held the 

Indians as in a spell.

He found the little, infirm king, invited him aboard, and took him by the hand, the better to see that the invitation was accepted. The king started willingly enough, and all seemed to me to be going well until I turned my head and saw Nooah and Kamehameha surrounded by their warriors and heard their angry murmurs. "Lono must not take the king!" Nooah insisted, with unusually clear insight into what was happening. "Quite so," Kamehameha agreed, and he passed out about

LOST EDEN 

a dozen iron daggers, and so did Nooah, reserving for himself the one as long as his arm.

All concealed their weapons and went to the beach. 

Cook had now progressed to a point near the water, with the king and his two small sons as well, but here met the objections of Kaneekabareea, the king's favorite wife and mother of the two boys. "No! They must not go!" she wailed loudly, at which time 

Nooah and Kamehameha came up and required the king to sit down, holding him by the shoulders in defiance of Cook. 

Our captain's ability to perceive trouble came to his aid and he decided to give up his plan, at least for the time being, and I am sure all would have been well, and the priests, if not the chiefs, would have found a way to restore friendly relations, but for a tragic happening across the bay.

In pursuing the canoes, our men had fired many rounds, and one ball killed a high chief. A messenger now came running with news of the disaster, and I heard the rumor spreading from the back of the crowd which had assembled. "It is war! Kill Lono and all the white malahines!"

Chapter 41 

AS THE CLAMOR FOR WAR INCREASED, I SAW TWO CHIEFS BRING A 

huge idol of wickerwork from a sacred house, but I did not know at the time that it was their battle symbol, for I had not seen it before. Such images were kept hidden, in strict tabu, and when brought forth upon the occasion of war, the people thought that here was a god who had gone away after the previous conflict and suddenly had returned from the heavens.

The image was as hideous a demon as ever I saw. A huge bust surmounted a pole, towering about nine feet high and covered with feathers of various colors. Its eyes, being large disks of pearl shell with black kukui nuts in the center, gave a most frightening aspect to the face. In the mouth was a double row of shark's teeth, and the diabolical sounds the chiefs made seemed to issue from this ferocious maw. 

Here indeed was the god of war. Now there could be no retreat. Iolana saw at once the danger that was closing down upon my outnumbered countrymen. "I will tell Lono to go!" she cried, and darted away through the crowd. "Stop her!" Ilima shouted. "Kill the malahine she-devil!"

Ilima ran after Iolana and I after Ilima. Behind us surged a crowd of men, armed with spears and stones and shields. 

Iolana broke through the edge of the crowd. "Go, Lono! They will kill you!" she shouted.

But Ilima and others were upon Iolana even before her words could be heard by Cook. Savages pelted her with stones, struck her, and kicked her.

As I rushed to Iolana's side with the only weapons I had- 277

LOST EDEN 

my fists-the attack engulfed me too, and I was all but forced to the ground.

The Englishmen, who thought me but another Indian and had not sensed Iolana's words, made not a move to help me. Then as I stumbled and sank under the punishment of stones and clubs I called out in my own tongue, and one beyond the milling mob heard and recognized me, and he charged in to my rescue like a mad bull, bellowing oaths and flailing his great fists. "Flint!" I cried, for he it was. 

He threw me clear of my attackers and felled one adversary after another.

As I turned to aid my rescuer, another hand caught me, and I heard the voice of Captain Cook. "Westermark, you young fool! Stand with the marines!"

With a sweep of his long arm Cook sent me flying into the ranks of our men who stood at the edge of the water with their muskets at the ready.

When I turned to look again, both Iolana and Flint had disappeared behind the advancing waves of that angry brown sea.

The crowd, now numbering several thousand, shouted wildly and came at us like a terrible wave of destruction, against which my feeble efforts were as naught. "Stop!" I ordered. “Make way!"

I started again into the angry throng. Always in the past these people had obeyed me, respected me as a high chief, but now all was changed. They continued to advance with their deadly spears at the ready. Two faces I recognized, but no longer were they friendly. A fanatical and murderous look was upon them, resembling somewhat the ferocity of the big, wide-eyed war god of wickerwork and feathers. "Come back!" called a marine named Farrington, tall and powerful, and he caught me and fetched me a blow on one ear which sent me spinning toward the water's edge. Nor would the other marines permit me to advance against that horde of angry Indians. Quite right they were in restraining me, for one man alone could have no hope of survival against such odds, but somewhere in the turmoil was Iolana. I knew not what fate she might

LOST EDEN 

279 be suffering and could think of naught but going to her aid, whether I lived or died.

But so fast did the battle develop that in those few seconds of mounting hostilities I found myself hopelessly isolated from the natives I had called my own people for a year, and all I could do was stand with that pitifully small band of Englishmen, facing an angry host of thousands. 

A frenzied Indian with a shield and spear advanced toward 

Cook with threatening gestures.

Cook raised his piece and fired at the man. Although he had ordered all muskets loaded with ball when hostilities threatened, he himself humanely had loaded one barrel with small shot with which to frighten rather than injure, and this barrel he discharged first.

The small shot had no effect upon the advancing native other than to encourage him. I could see that he had dipped his grass shield in water as had many others who believed that it was the fire and heat from our muskets that hurt rather than the speeding shot which could not be seen in the air. As this man strode forward, the ball from the other barrel of Cook's piece tumbled him dead upon the sand.

Now all the marines opened fire, including those in the boats, and immediately a shower of stones and spears came at us. 

One spear struck a marine in the face and broke off, leaving about four inches of its wooden point in his flesh.

For a brief moment Flint appeared at the edge of the crowd. Up to this time the natives had but matched their fists against his, and he had won through, but now their weapons came into play, and three spears struck him at once. As he fell, two

Indians attacked him with iron daggers and ended his life. So quickly did they dispatch him that I had taken but a single step toward him when I saw that for him all was over. He had fought for a friend, as many would have fought for him, and there he would rest on an island where he longed to be. Into his turbulent existence finally came peace.

Nor was there aught I could do for Iolana when hundreds of warriors rushed to the fore and formed a solid phalanx. Having

LOST EDEN 

no weapons of my own, I gathered up what spears and stones I could and threw them back. In despairing rage, I somehow contrived to catch a spear in mid-air as I had seen the warriors do and hurled it into the body of an Indian almost upon Cook, brandishing a great club with a murderous square end. little.

Three at least I killed, but three in many thousand meant Cook got to the water's edge and called out to the men in the boats, but so great was the din that he could not make himself heard. Some think he ordered that they cease firing, but others believe he wanted a boat to come in close and take him off, for, like the majority of our people, he could not swim. 

Our captain waded into the water. The moment he turned his back upon the Indians, the effect of his extraordinary eyes was gone. Perhaps in that contemptuous turning away he thought only to show his indifference to his foes. But in those seconds of apparent retreat the spell he had cast was broken and a shower of stones fell about him.

One stone struck him on the back of the head. He faltered and stumbled in the water.

It was at this moment that I saw Nooah emerge from the crowd. He must now have satisfied himself that Cook was indeed mortal and could be wounded, because the arm-length dagger was in Nooah's hand. 

Like a tiger, his red eyes blazing, he bounded forward and plunged the blade into Cook's back, just below his right shoulder.

So savage was the thrust that the point passed entirely through the body and emerged from the breast. 

Cook fell. Other chiefs swarmed upon the kill. Each would seize the long dagger and plunge it again into our captain's body lying in the wash of the surf.

When we saw that we could not save Captain Cook or any others who had fallen, we scrambled for the boats, and though their bottoms almost touched the land, some of the marines on shore had such slight knowledge of swimming that they were in danger of drowning and had to be helped through the water. I did what I could in this matter. 

One in the midshipman's uniform floundered and splashed

LOST EDEN 

281 helplessly, and I caught him up and turned him on his back only to discover that he was Lord Wadleigh with a spear wound in his chest. "Ah. Again we meet," he gasped, but now there was no ferocity left in him. "I am done for. You'd best let me go." "No. You were born to be hanged. You'll not get off with a drowning." And so I towed him to a boat. 

Lieutenant Phillips, who commanded the boats and was severely censured by Bligh for his actions this day, rescued one man from drowning though himself severely wounded in the side. 

The guns of the ships began to fire. The five-pound shots tearing through that frenzied throng deterred it little from its purpose. One shot cut down a palm tree and another struck a rock which flew in pieces, one fragment striking Terreeoboo, wounding him, but not fatally. 

Our own casualties were four dead and three seriously wounded.

In the end I found myself in the pinnace, pulling for the Resolution, upon whose deck I had hoped never again to set foot. 

Some of my friends greeted me with cheers and others with laughter, for even though I now resembled an Indian and had hidden myself for a year, the long arm of Captain Cook had again drawn me back, almost in the last gesture of his life.

Consternation reigned on board, and the people were for a war of extermination, but Captain Clerke of the Discovery, who now became our commander, had better wisdom. He dispatched 

Lieutenant King to the aid of Lieutenant Bligh, who was across the bay with but a handful of men to guard the mast and sails which he had under repair. "Shoot if necessary," Clerke ordered, "but think first of bringing off the mast. We are in a desperate plight without it." 

We could see hundreds of Indians making for Bligh's party, and soon we heard the sound of shots. Then the near-by village went up in flames. "Maitai! [Very fine!]" cried some of the women who had remained on board throughout the fight. They enjoyed the spectacle of the fire, seemingly without knowing or caring of the consequences.

LOST EDEN 

Many feared for Bligh, but soon we saw his party coming across the bay, towing the mast, and he standing invincible in the bow of the foremost boat. "All accounted for, sir," he called to Clerke, for all the hands were safe, and now alongside the Resolution was the whole of the mast and all of the precious canvas from the shore. 

George Vancouver climbed aboard from one of the boats and greeted me most cordially. "I'm damned,” he said, "if Bligh hasn't a charmed life! But tell me, how have you fared?" "Very well until this day," I answered. 

I could go no further. “Easy does it, mate,” said George. “We'll save this talk for another time. There may be work yet to do this night." 

When darkness came we were all under arms, with a double guard and both boats pulling slowly around the ships. We could see many fires upon the shore and hear loud lamentations and chanting and the blowing of conches.

Along toward midnight we heard a canoe approaching in the darkness. The marines immediately opened fire. "Hono! Hono!" I heard from the darkness. "Don't fire!" I shouted. “It is Kahele, my friend."

And so it was. He came humbly over the side. There were three holes in his canoe, but he was unhurt. "I bring you this," he said to Captain Clerke, presenting a neat package of native cloth.

When he opened the bundle, to our horror we saw a piece of flesh weighing about ten pounds and upon it two human hands. "It is a part of Lono," Kahele said, and sorrowfully began a lamentation.

We recognized the scar between thumb and forefinger of one hand. Indeed, it was that of our commander. "Where is the rest of the body?" Clerke asked. "Only the bones remain, and they are given to the king.” "Can you get them for us?” "I will try," Kahele replied, though obviously puzzled at the request, for the Indians had but carried out their own custom of disposing of an enemy chief. "The king has gone to the caves on

LOST EDEN 

283 the side of the high cliff which are reached only by ropes from above." "Hold him as hostage," Bligh advised, "until you get what you want." "No. No," Kahele pleaded. "I will do all I can."

He hooked his two forefingers together in their sign of truth, which Clerke recognized and respected. "Very well," the captain said, "we will expect them to morrow. You may go now." "Thank you. Thank you," said my old taio. Then sadly he turned to me. “Have you the news of Iolana?” "Tell me." "She is dead."

I was stunned beyond the power of speaking. Nor could he find anything more to say. sadly.

Your people have killed eight of the highest today, and some were your best friends." Tears came to his eyes. "I will be lonely without you, Hono." "A better friend than you, Kahele, I will never find."

And so my good old taio departed and I was left there on the Resolution, desolate, with only my memories of the dream that had ended so suddenly and so tragically. 

Gone was the lovely form and happy spirit that for a year had given me quiet satisfaction and the fullest enjoyment of life that man may meet with upon the far-flung lands in the vast seas of the earth.

Never again would I know the contentment and utter happiness of a couch of flower petals, the fragrance of jasminescented hair in the soft breeze of a moonlit night.

I thought of the rainbow god who smiled upon us on our wedding day, when we swam in the warm, spårkling water of the royal pool of Kauai. If any climbed the rainbow to a better land, surely one of the most welcome would be Iolana.

ON THE FOLLOWING DAY CAPTAIN CLERKE CONDUCTED A SAD FUNERAL 

ceremony over Captain Cook's remains and we dropped them into the bay. Many a heavy heart was there upon the decks. 

We stepped our mast with great difficulty, for our ropes, made of sealskin and the hides of sea horses from the northern ice, were so rotten that they parted again and again. There was a rotted place in our keel into which you could have thrust four or five coconuts, but we dared not linger to repair it. 

We sailed from Kealakekua Bay on the twenty-seventh of 

February, headed once more for the regions of the North Pole.

As we shook out our canvas, cockroaches again rained upon the deck in torrents, for if there had been a good supply of them before, the Resolution now had a full cargo.

The sails filled at once, and off our ship went at a good speed. One day, if I survived, I would walk up the well-remembered path to the front door of our home in Newcastle. How happy 

Mother would be! What satisfying hugs she would give, and what endless talk we would have as she baked a cake and spread upon it my favorite chocolate frosting, the kind with just the right amount of vanilla flavoring. 

And what of Lorna? Would she be happy too? What torment came to both of us that evening so long ago when I sailed away, waving my new reefer hat to that lovely girl with a broken heart so bravely holding aloft her kerchief as her lover's boat carried him from the English shore.

In such mood, with the green of Hawaii already turning purple and sinking beneath the horizon, Vancouver came to me. "Captain Clerke wants to see you," he said. 284

285 "Here it comes," I ventured. "Two dozen lashes at least." "He has promised to let me speak for you, and I have thought of some arguments in your favor," Vancouver continued. "Clerke is not as stern as Cook was. It may be on account of the poor man's consumption. There is not much left of him."

We walked into the commander's quarters, where we found him making an entry in the log with one of the albatross quills that Dr. Anderson had provided before he died. 

Clerke studied me with the tired look of a sick man, but his calm gray eyes, like his emaciated figure, still showed indomitable determination. Standing there while he measured me, I knew that here was a leader who would carry on the ventures Cook had undertaken, though they led him to his own death, which indeed they did, for within the year he expired in the cold northern regions. "This is a court-martial," said Clerke crisply. "You are accused of desertion from His Majesty's Ship Resolution. Tell me, what was it like in your dream Paradise?”

And so again I was a midshipman and could enjoy the company of my commander and talk with him, which I did for a long time, and his interest never lagged. "Westermark," he said when at last he had done questioning me, “this is the most extraordinary voyage of exploration the world has ever known." "Aye, 'tis the best I could imagine and the worst at the same time." 

There was a twinkle in the captain's eye when he dismissed me. "At least one midshipman contrived to find the best of it," he said. "Now back to your paints and capture for us what you can of this new world."

I went to my room and found my paints musty and somewhat dried, but it was good to see them. Again I would be an

LOST EDEN 

287 artist. My fingers enjoyed the touch of the brushes. I knew each one as though it had a name, for I had learned its shortcomings as well as its virtues.

The sound of spoken English stimulated me and did much to lift my spirits. Even the smell of vinegar and gunpowder exuded by the decks and bulkheads seemed cheering and homelike. Aye, now I was going home.

Lord Wadleigh came in. He walked hesitatingly and appeared pale and weak. "Westermark," he said, "I will live, thanks to you." "No thanks to me. I saved you because you were needed to work the ship." "Just the same, I am grateful. And I wish to confess that 

Lorna Pierce would have none of me. 'Twas you she wanted. May 

I shake your hand?” "Why, yes. Yes, of course." I grasped his extended hand and for once saw in his face something of the gentleman. "You are still fond of Lorna?" he asked. "Yes, I am." "I've naught but luck to wish you, mate." With that he walked away. He was growing up, like so many of us youngsters on that long, hard voyage. 

I began to think of Lorna, so charming, so far away. Aye, there yet was hope in this life. Though I must now sail as far toward the North Pole as men could force a ship, we might fetch 

England in another year or two. 

And again I was a sailor lad, upon a distant sea, with a vision of a lovely lass to lead him on, though dreams had fallen into ruin and Eden was lost.
